### [1] [SPEAR: A Simulator for Photorealistic Embodied AI Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06701)
*Mike Roberts,Renhan Wang,Rushikesh Zawar,Rachith Dey-Prakash,Quentin Leboutet,Stephan R. Richter,Matthias Müller,German Ros,Rui Tang,Stefan Leutenegger,Yannick Hold-Geoffroy,Kalyan Sunkavalli,Vladlen Koltun*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPEAR is a Python library that programmatically controls Unreal Engine applications via a modular plugin architecture, offering over 14K exposed UE functions, fast photorealistic rendering (73 FPS at 1920x1080), novel ground truth modalities (e.g., non-diffuse intrinsic decomposition, material IDs), and an expressive programming model for complex, deterministic work graphs within a single UE frame.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing photorealistic simulators for embodied AI suffer from limited generality, programmability, and rendering speed, motivating the need for a more flexible, fast, and functionally rich simulator.

Method: SPEAR uses a modular plugin architecture connecting Python to any Unreal Engine application, exposes over 14K UE functions to Python, renders directly to NumPy arrays at high speed, and supports an expressive high-level programming model for specifying deterministic work graphs with arbitrary data dependencies.

Result: SPEAR achieves 73 FPS at 1920x1080 resolution for photorealistic rendering, provides ground truth modalities missing in other UE-based simulators, and enables diverse applications including multi-agent control, city-scale rendering, procedural content generation, synchronized multi-view imaging, interactive co-simulation with MuJoCo, and natural-language scene editing.

Conclusion: SPEAR significantly advances the state of photorealistic simulation for embodied AI research by combining high performance, extensive programmability, novel sensor modalities, and a flexible programming model, as demonstrated through a wide range of example applications.

Abstract: Interactive simulators have become powerful tools for training embodied agents and generating synthetic visual data, but existing photorealistic simulators suffer from limited generality, programmability, and rendering speed. We address these limitations by introducing SPEAR: A Simulator for Photorealistic Embodied AI Research. At its core, SPEAR is a Python library that can connect to, and programmatically control, any Unreal Engine (UE) application via a modular plugin architecture. SPEAR exposes over 14K unique UE functions to Python, representing an order-of-magnitude increase in programmable functionality over existing UE-based simulators. Additionally, a single SPEAR instance can render 1920x1080 photorealistic beauty images directly into a user's NumPy array at 73 frames per second - an order of magnitude faster than existing UE plugins - while also providing ground truth image modalities that are not available in any existing UE-based simulator (e.g., a non-diffuse intrinsic image decomposition, material IDs, and physically based shading parameters). Finally, SPEAR introduces an expressive high-level programming model that enables users to specify complex graphs of UE work with arbitrary data dependencies among work items, and to execute these graphs deterministically within a single UE frame. We demonstrate the utility of SPEAR through a diverse collection of example applications: controlling multiple embodied agents with distinct action spaces (e.g., humans, cars, and robots) across several in-the-wild UE projects; rendering photorealistic city-scale environments; manipulating UE's procedural content generation systems; rendering synchronized multi-view images of detailed human faces; coordinating an interactive co-simulation with the MuJoCo physics simulator; and editing scenes with natural language via an AI coding assistant.

</details>


### [2] [Pixel-Precise Explainable Stress Indexing: A Semantic Segmentation Framework for Disease Severity Quantification in Field Crops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06585)
*Raunak Kumar,Soumyashree Kar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified deep learning pipeline integrating semantic segmentation, regression-based severity estimation, and disease classification for plant stress quantification. Using the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset, U-Net with MobileNetV2 achieves the best performance: 98.20% pixel accuracy, 0.70 mIoU, 99.41% detection accuracy at 14.7 ms per image, with severity index strongly correlated to expert annotations (r=0.968, R²=0.937).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plant diseases cause 20-40% annual yield loss globally (>USD 220 billion), and traditional manual stress assessments are labour-intensive and subjective. Accurate and scalable stress quantification is needed for precision agriculture.

Method: A unified deep learning pipeline integrating semantic segmentation, regression-based severity estimation, and disease classification. Stress severity is categorized into four levels (Low to Very High) based on infected leaf area proportion. Four models (U-Net with MobileNetV2, SegFormer, FCN, PSPNet) are evaluated on the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset (1,641 samples, six classes).

Result: U-Net with MobileNetV2 achieves 98.20% pixel accuracy, 0.70 mIoU, 99.41% detection accuracy, and 14.7 ms per image. SegFormer achieves 0.66 mIoU; FCN and PSPNet achieve ~0.49 mIoU. Severity index correlation with expert annotations: r=0.968, R²=0.937.

Conclusion: The U-Net with MobileNetV2 pipeline is reliable, accurate, and fast enough for real-time automated crop monitoring and decision support, with severity estimates strongly aligned with expert assessments.

Abstract: Plant diseases, resulting from both biotic and abiotic stresses, cause an estimated 20-40% loss in global agricultural yield annually, resulting in economic damages exceeding USD 220 billion. Accurate and scalable stress quantification is essential for precision agriculture, yet traditional manual assessments are labour-intensive and subjective. This paper proposes a unified deep learning pipeline integrating semantic segmentation, regression-based severity estimation, and disease classification. Stress severity is categorised into four levels (Low to Very High) based on the proportion of infected leaf area. Experiments on the Apple Tree Leaf Disease Segmentation dataset (1,641 samples, six classes) evaluate four models: U-Net (MobileNetV2), SegFormer, FCN, and PSPNet. U-Net with MobileNetV2 achieves the best performance with 98.20% pixel accuracy, 0.70 mIoU, and 99.41% detection accuracy at 14.7 ms per image, making it suitable for real-time use. SegFormer performs competitively (mIoU 0.66), while FCN and PSPNet show lower spatial accuracy (approximately 0.49 mIoU). The computed severity index strongly correlates with expert annotations (r = 0.968, R^2 = 0.937), demonstrating the system's reliability for automated crop monitoring and decision support.

</details>


### [3] [CoFINN: Conservation Flux Informed Neural Networks for Physics Problems Governed by Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06587)
*Adnan Harun Doğan,Mert Deniz,Hande Alemdar,Özgür Uğraş Baran*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoFINN is a physics-informed deep learning framework that embeds finite-volume conservation laws into CNN training for compressible flow prediction, achieving up to 34% reduction in drag prediction error, especially in limited-data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional data-driven CNNs optimize only pixel-wise similarity metrics and lack physical conservation guarantees, while classical physics-informed methods enforce differential-equation residuals via automatic differentiation rather than adopting a finite-volume perspective consistent with modern CFD methodology.

Method: CoFINN interprets CNN output fields as structured computational grids where each pixel represents a finite-volume cell, and enforces conservation consistency through sophisticated numerical flux calculations embedded directly into the training loss.

Result: CoFINN reduces drag prediction error by up to 34% at extreme angles of attack and approximately 15% on average across the test set for transonic flow (M=0.7, Re=6×10^6), with particularly significant improvements in limited-data regimes.

Conclusion: The conservation-based loss acts as an effective physical regularizer, maintaining computational efficiency of CNN surrogates while improving physical consistency; the framework is architecture-agnostic and extensible to broader conservation-law-governed systems.

Abstract: We present CoFINN (Conservation Flux Informed Neural Networks), a physics-informed deep learning framework for predicting compressible flow fields governed by conservation laws. Unlike conventional data-driven convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which optimize only pixel-wise similarity metrics, CoFINN embeds finite-volume conservation physics directly into the training process. Unlike classical physics-informed methods which enforce differential-equation residuals at collocation points through automatic differentiation, CoFINN adopts a finite-volume perspective consistent with modern CFD methodology. CoFINN interprets CNN output fields as structured computational grids, where each pixel represents a finite-volume cell, and enforces conservation consistency through sophisticated numerical flux calculations. The framework is evaluated on transonic flow prediction around airfoils at (M=0.7, Re=6 * 10^6), including challenging conditions involving shock waves and high angles of attack. Results show that CoFINN improves aerodynamic force prediction accuracy, reducing drag prediction error by up to 34% at extreme angles of attack and by approximately 15% on average across the test set. Improvements are particularly significant in limited-data regimes, demonstrating that the conservation-based loss acts as an effective physical regularizer. The proposed approach maintains the computational efficiency advantages of CNN surrogates while significantly improving physical consistency and conservation behavior. The framework is architecture-agnostic and extensible to broader classes of conservation-law-governed physical systems.

</details>


### [4] [AI for Cultural Heritage Textiles: Fine-Tuned Latent Diffusion for Novel Ulos Motif Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06590)
*Humasak Tommy Argo Simanjuntak,Jesika Purba,Sitogab Girsang,Widya Manurung,Samuel Situmeang,Arlinta Barus,Daniel Oranova Siahaan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study fine-tunes two latent diffusion models (Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4) on Ulos textile motifs to generate culturally consistent yet novel designs, with Protogen v3.4 outperforming SD v1.4 significantly in FID (~10.5x lower) and IS (2.0x higher). Lower strength values improve fidelity; higher strength increases diversity. Guidance scale 5-9 provides optimal balance. The work demonstrates fine-tuned generative AI can support creative renewal of intangible cultural heritage while preserving stylistic and symbolic integrity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Ulos weaving faces a narrow range of motifs and time-intensive design processes, limiting creative renewal. There is a need to balance fidelity to cultural tradition with innovative approaches that meet contemporary design demands.

Method: A generative AI framework fine-tuning two pretrained latent diffusion models (Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4) on a curated, annotated dataset of high-resolution Ulos motifs. Quantitative evaluation uses FID and IS; qualitative assessment involves traditional weavers and public members. Effects of strength and guidance scale on generation quality are examined.

Result: Protogen v3.4 consistently outperforms Stable Diffusion v1.4, achieving ~10.5x lower FID and 2.0x higher IS. Lower strength values yield higher fidelity; higher strength increases diversity with reduced realism, revealing a fidelity-diversity tradeoff. Guidance scale 5-9 provides the most effective balance across all configurations.

Conclusion: Carefully fine-tuned generative AI can support creative renewal of intangible cultural heritage while preserving its stylistic and symbolic integrity. The recommended operating range for high-quality, diverse Ulos motif generation is a guidance scale of 5-9.

Abstract: Preserving and revitalising traditional textiles such as Ulos, a cultural heritage of the Batak ethnic group in North Sumatra, Indonesia, requires balancing fidelity to tradition with innovative approaches that meet contemporary design demands. Traditional Ulos weaving faces two key limitations: a narrow range of motifs and a time-intensive design process. This study presents a generative AI framework that fine-tunes two pretrained latent diffusion models: Protogen v3.4 and Stable Diffusion v1.4, on a curated, annotated dataset of high-resolution Ulos motifs to generate culturally consistent yet novel designs. Model performance is evaluated quantitatively using Frechet Inception Distance (FID), Inception Score (IS), and qualitatively through assessments by traditional weavers and members of the public. Protogen v3.4 consistently outperforms Stable Diffusion v1.4, achieving substantially lower FID (~10.5x) and higher IS (2.0x), indicating superior visual fidelity, diversity, and closer alignment with the real Ulos motif distribution. We further examine the effects of strength and guidance scale on generation quality across both models. Lower strength values consistently yield higher fidelity (lower FID), while higher strength values increase generative diversity at the cost of realism, revealing a clear fidelity-diversity tradeoff for both models. Across all tested configurations, a guidance scale of 5-9 provides the most effective balance between fidelity and diversity, stabilising FID, KID, and IS, and is recommended as the operating range for high-quality, diverse Ulos motif generation. These findings demonstrate that carefully fine-tuned generative AI can support the creative renewal of intangible cultural heritage while preserving its stylistic and symbolic integrity.

</details>


### [5] [LipSSD: Lipschitz-Constrained Single-Shot Detection for Adversarially Robust Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06592)
*Vincent Lébé,Yannick Prudent,Corentin Friedrich,Thomas Massena,Ronan Sicre,Franck Mamalet*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Lipschitz-constrained object detectors (LipSSD) as robust-by-design alternatives to standard detectors, showing they improve adversarial robustness, complement adversarial training (up to +15 mAP@50 on unseen attacks), and allow controllable accuracy-robustness trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object detectors are sensitive to adversarial attacks, limiting their use in safety-critical systems. Adversarial robustness for detection is understudied, and existing methods (e.g., adversarial training) often fail to transfer across attacks, budgets, or architectures.

Method: The authors propose Lipschitz-constrained variants of object detection architectures (LipSSD based on SSD). They control the accuracy-robustness trade-off via a single training hyperparameter, and combine Lipschitz constraints with adversarial training.

Result: LipSSD improves mAP@50 by up to 15 points over adversarially trained SSD on unseen attacks (Pascal VOC). It preserves clean performance on safety-critical datasets (LARD, KITTI) while improving robustness. The trade-off is controllable via one hyperparameter.

Conclusion: Architectural Lipschitz control is a practical, attack-agnostic direction for improving object detector robustness, complementary to adversarial training.

Abstract: Object detectors have many applications in safety-critical systems, but they are known to be sensitive to worst-case perturbations such as adversarial attacks, which limits their applicability in real-world scenarios. Compared with classification, adversarial robustness for object detection has received less attention, and existing methods are often tied to adversarial training, whose performance may not transfer across attacks, perturbation budgets, or architectures. In this work, we introduce Lipschitz-constrained variants of object detection architectures as robust-by-design alternatives to standard detectors. We validate this approach with LipSSD, a Lipschitz-constrained Single Shot MultiBox Detector (SSD), and provide a comprehensive study of its adversarial robustness using multiple white-box adversarial attacks and datasets. We first analyze the accuracyrobustness trade-off induced by Lipschitz constraints and show that it can be controlled through a single training hyperparameter. We then demonstrate that Lipschitzconstrained detectors are complementary to adversarial training: under the same training setup on the Pascal VOC dataset, adversarially trained LipSSD improves mAP@50 on unseen attacks by up to 15 points over classical adversarially trained SSD. Finally, we use more specific safety-critical datasets such as LARD and KITTI, and show that Lipschitz-constrained detectors can improve robustness while largely preserving clean performance. These results suggest that architectural Lipschitz control is a practical and attack-agnostic direction for improving the robustness of object detectors.

</details>


### [6] [MiLSD: A Micro Line-Segment Detector for Resource-Constrained Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06600)
*Parsa Hassani Shariat Panahi,Amir Hossein Jalilvand,M. Hassan Najafi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MiLSD is a sub-megabyte line segment detection model for MCUs, comparing output representations and quantization strategies, achieving 24.1 sAP10 on ShanghaiTech Wireframe within 1 MB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning line segment detectors achieve high accuracy but require several megabytes of memory, exceeding the capacity of low-cost MCUs. This work investigates the maximum achievable accuracy under a sub-megabyte budget for embedded vision systems.

Method: Proposes MiLSD, a compact fully-convolutional backbone tailored for MCU constraints. Systematically compares three output representations (with F-Clip center-with-length-and-angle formulation performing best). Employs 8-bit/4-bit quantization, sub-pixel decoding, test-time augmentation, and a lightweight verifier. Maps accuracy-memory trade-offs across representations, bit-widths, capacities, and post-processing strategies.

Result: MiLSD improves sAP10 from 10.6 (25k params, 0.25 MB) to 24.1 within 1 MB on ShanghaiTech Wireframe. 8-bit quantization preserves full-precision performance; 4-bit quantization causes significant degradation especially in angle regression, with QAT recovering only partial loss.

Conclusion: Rather than competing with GPU-scale parsers, the work systematically maps the accuracy-memory trade-off for embedded line segment detection, enabling deployment on MCU-class hardware.

Abstract: Line segment detection is a key building block in visual SLAM, 3D reconstruction, and industrial inspection. Recent deep learning methods have greatly improved accuracy, yet even the smallest models require several megabytes of memory, exceeding low-cost MCU capacity. This work investigates the maximum achievable accuracy under a sub-megabyte budget. We propose MiLSD, a detector tailored for MCU-level constraints, and systematically compare three output representations within a compact fully-convolutional backbone. Our study shows that the proposed F-Clip center-with-length-and-angle formulation learns most effectively at small model sizes. We find that 8-bit quantization preserves full-precision performance, while 4-bit quantization causes significant degradation, particularly in angle regression, with quantization-aware training recovering only part of the loss. With a one-megabyte activation budget and inference enhancements including sub-pixel decoding, test-time augmentation, and a lightweight verifier, MiLSD improves sAP10 on ShanghaiTech Wireframe from 10.6 (25k parameters, 0.25 MB) to 24.1 within 1 MB. Rather than competing with GPU-scale parsers, we map the accuracy memory trade-off across representations, bit-widths, capacities, and post-processing strategies for embedded vision systems.

</details>


### [7] [Dynamic-in-Few-Step: Unifying Dynamic Computation and Few-Step Distillation for Efficient Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06631)
*Yu Cheng,Siyue Yao,Zhongang Qi,Shanyan Guan,Wei Li,Fajie Yuan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A post-training acceleration framework for Video Diffusion Models that jointly optimizes denoising steps and structured sparsity to create a step-specific Mixture-of-Models (MoM), achieving 30x speedup over 50-step teacher with 24% FLOPs reduction and 1.2x wall-clock gain on Wan-14B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VDMs suffer from prohibitive computational costs, and existing few-step distillation methods enforce static architectures across all denoising stages, ignoring the varying computational demands at different noise levels.

Method: Integrates dynamic structural sparsification into distillation process; jointly optimizes denoising steps and structured sparsity to transform a pre-trained VDM into a step-specific Mixture-of-Models (MoM); uses Progressive Training Strategy with Output Rollout Mechanism for stable training; develops a specialized inference engine for efficient deployment.

Result: On Wan-14B, removes 24% per-step FLOPs on top of 4-step distillation, adds 1.2x wall-clock gain, achieves 30x speedup over 50-step teacher while preserving competitive generation quality.

Conclusion: The method is orthogonal to existing acceleration techniques, highly effective in reducing computational costs while maintaining quality, and transforms VDMs into compact step-specific MoMs.

Abstract: Video Diffusion Models (VDMs) have demonstrated superior generation quality but suffer from prohibitive computational costs. While recent few-step distillation techniques significantly accelerate inference, they typically enforce a static model architecture across all denoising stages, ignoring the varying computational demands inherent to different noise levels. In this work, we propose a novel post-training acceleration framework that exploits this redundancy by integrating dynamic structural sparsification directly into the distillation process. Unlike conventional post-hoc compression applied to a fixed diffusion pipeline, our approach jointly optimizes the denoising steps and structured model sparsity, transforming a pre-trained VDM into a compact, step-specific Mixture-of-Models (MoM). To address the training instability arising from this joint optimization, we introduce a Progressive Training Strategy coupled with an Output Rollout Mechanism, which ensures the coherent learning of structural decisions across timesteps. Furthermore, we develop a specialized inference engine to deploy the resulting MoM efficiently. Our method is orthogonal to existing acceleration techniques and highly effective: On Wan-14B, it removes 24% of the per-step FLOPs on top of 4-step distillation, adding a 1.2x wall-clock gain and reaching a 30x speedup over the 50-step teacher while preserving competitive generation quality.

</details>


### [8] [A Good Initialization is All You Need for Faithful Visual Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06726)
*Zihan Gu,Jiayu Wang,Hua Zhang,Yue Hu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoPAIR and TRACE, two forward-only methods for compact top-k visual attribution masks, achieving state-of-the-art faithfulness in full-ordering attribution and high MLLM repair rates (94-96%) without requiring complete region rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing search-based perturbation methods for visual attribution produce full region orderings, but many applications (especially MLLM attribution and repair) only need compact top-k evidence masks. The combinatorial nature of exactly k-region masks and the limitations of coarse grouping vs. one-step scoring motivate the need for methods that directly optimize fixed-cardinality evidence sets.

Method: Two forward-only methods: (1) CoPAIR constructs coarse singleton/pair candidates via PhaseWin-Greedy gap diagnosis for warm-starting full-ordering search. (2) TRACE directly searches fixed-cardinality fine-region masks using cross-entropy sampling, elite retention, and distribution updates, with finite-budget recovery analysis.

Result: CoPAIR and TRACE establish a new SOTA frontier for faithful full-ordering attribution under inclusive forward-call accounting on ImageNet classification (CLIP ViT-L/14, CLIP RN101, ResNet-101). TRACE+Greedy gives strongest search-based MLLM attribution on POPE/RePOPE (Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, LLaVA-v1.5-7B). Direct TRACE masks achieve 94.44% and 96.00% single-point RePOPE repair rates.

Conclusion: Compact evidence masks from TRACE are actionable attribution outputs, not merely prefixes of full rankings, demonstrating that mask-first attribution is both effective and practical for downstream tasks like MLLM repair.

Abstract: Faithful visual attribution identifies which image regions support a model prediction. Search-based perturbation methods lead the insertion--deletion faithfulness frontier by masking regions and measuring score changes, but they usually output a complete ordering of all regions. Many applications, especially MLLM attribution and repair, only need a compact top-\(k\) evidence mask. We study this mask-first attribution problem. An exactly \(k\)-region mask is combinatorial: useful evidence can depend on interactions among fine regions. Coarse grouping can stabilize early search but aggregates redundant content, whereas one-step scoring can miss high-value combinations. We introduce two forward-only methods. \textsc{CoPAIR} uses a PhaseWin--Greedy gap diagnosis to construct coarse singleton/pair candidates that warm-start full-ordering search. \textsc{TRACE} directly searches fixed-cardinality fine-region masks with cross-entropy sampling, elite retention, and distribution updates, with a finite-budget recovery analysis. The resulting evidence set can be returned as a compact attribution mask or used to initialize Greedy or PhaseWin when a complete ranking is required. Across ImageNet classification with CLIP ViT-L/14, CLIP RN101, and ResNet-101, our initialized search methods establish a new state-of-the-art frontier for faithful full-ordering attribution under inclusive forward-call accounting. On POPE and RePOPE with Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct and LLaVA-v1.5-7B, \textsc{TRACE}+Greedy gives the strongest search-based MLLM attribution results. Direct \textsc{TRACE} masks further achieve single-point RePOPE repair rates of \(94.44\%\) and \(96.00\%\), showing that compact evidence masks can be actionable attribution outputs, not merely prefixes of full rankings.

</details>


### [9] [Hardware-aware Graph Neural Networks prunning for embedded event-based vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06739)
*Piotr Wzorek,Kamil Jeziorek,Tomasz Kryjak*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an optimization strategy for Graph Convolutional Neural Networks (GCNs) targeting event-based camera data on resource-constrained FPGA platforms, using hardware-aware pruning and quantization combined with Fine Grid Search and Greedy layer-wise Iterative Deepening Search to balance on-chip memory savings against inference accuracy, achieving 26.5-31.4% BRAM reductions with modest accuracy losses across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event-based cameras require efficient real-time processing with low latency and power consumption for mobile robotics, but the stringent hardware constraints of embedded heterogeneous FPGA platforms make it challenging to deploy efficient algorithms that preserve the temporal sparsity of event data.

Method: The method incorporates hardware-aware pruning and quantization to adapt GCN architectures to FPGA resource limits, using Fine Grid Search and Greedy layer-wise Iterative Deepening Search for strategic design space exploration.

Result: BRAM memory reductions of 28.8% (CIFAR-10, 1.65% accuracy drop), 31.4% (MNIST-DVS, 3.55% accuracy drop), and 26.5% (N-Caltech101, 5.18% accuracy reduction).

Conclusion: The proposed optimization strategy enables flexible adaptation of GCN models to target FPGA platforms while effectively trading off memory savings for inference accuracy.

Abstract: Event-based cameras are gaining popularity as the sensor of choice for mobile robotics, due to their high performance in dynamic environments. However, these applications require efficient real-time data processing with low latency and power consumption. One strategy to meet these stringent requirements is hardware acceleration of efficient algorithms that preserve the temporal sparsity of event data. In this work, we propose an optimization strategy for Graph Convolutional Neural Networks models aimed at adapting their architecture to the limited resources of embedded heterogeneous FPGA platforms. Our method incorporates hardware-aware pruning and quantization, taking into account the trade-off between on-chip memory savings and inference accuracy. Strategic exploration of the design space with Fine Grid Search and Greedy layer-wise Iterative Deepening Search methods enables flexible adaptation of the model architecture to the target platform. Our approach was evaluated across various network configurations and multiple datasets, resulting in BRAM memory reductions of 28.8% for CIFAR-10 (with a 1.65% decrease in accuracy), 31.4% for MNIST-DVS (accuracy drop of 3.55%), and 26.5% for N-Caltech101 (with a 5.18% accuracy reduction).

</details>


### [10] [URS-Stereo: Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search for Real-Time Stereo Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06779)
*Pouya Sohrabipour,Chaitanya kumar reddy Pallerla,Dongyi Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: URS-Stereo proposes an uncertainty-guided search adaptation mechanism for coarse-to-fine stereo matching that uses an Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM) to predict disparity reliability and adaptively relocate local cost volume centers, improving robustness against inaccurate propagated disparities while maintaining real-time performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coarse-to-fine stereo matching methods suffer from unrecoverable matching failures when propagated disparity estimates are inaccurate, because ground-truth correspondences fall outside the predefined local search range during refinement.

Method: Introduces an Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM) that predicts both the reliability of propagated disparities and residual search offsets, adaptively relocating local cost volume centers before disparity refinement based on confidence levels.

Result: Extensive experiments on SceneFlow, KITTI 2012, KITTI 2015, Middlebury, and ETH3D show consistent improvement in disparity estimation accuracy while maintaining real-time inference speed.

Conclusion: The uncertainty-guided search strategy effectively addresses the propagation error accumulation problem in coarse-to-fine stereo matching, improving robustness of local correspondence estimation without sacrificing computational efficiency.

Abstract: Real-time stereo matching is crucial for robotics, autonomous systems, and embedded vision applications, where both computational efficiency and disparity accuracy are required. Recent coarse-to-fine stereo matching methods improve efficiency by progressively refining disparity estimates using local cost volumes at higher resolutions. However, these methods rely heavily on the accuracy of propagated disparity estimates from previous stages. When the propagated disparity is inaccurate, the ground-truth correspondence may fall outside the predefined local search range, leading to unrecoverable matching failures during subsequent refinement. In this paper, we propose URS-Stereo, a real-time coarse-to-fine stereo matching framework that addresses this limitation through uncertainty-guided search adaptation. Specifically, we introduce an Uncertainty-Guided Residual Search Module (UGRSM), which predicts the reliability of propagated disparities together with residual search offsets to adaptively relocate the centers of local cost volumes before disparity refinement. By dynamically adjusting the search region according to the confidence of the propagated disparity, the proposed method significantly improves the robustness of local correspondence estimation while preserving the computational efficiency of coarse-to-fine stereo matching. Extensive experiments on SceneFlow, KITTI 2012, KITTI 2015, Middlebury, and ETH3D demonstrate that URS-Stereo consistently improves disparity estimation while maintaining real-time inference speed, validating the effectiveness of the proposed uncertainty-guided search strategy

</details>


### [11] [Rail Track Extraction from Rasterized Classified Point Clouds Using a Full-Resolution, Fully Convolutional Recurrent Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06829)
*Alexander Gribov,Jie Chang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A novel rail track extraction method from 3D point clouds using a fully convolutional recurrent neural network trained on synthetic data, with morphological refinement, centerline extraction, smoothing, and DTW-based dual-track centerline derivation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rail track extraction is critical for automated railway asset inspection and maintenance workflows, but existing methods struggle with noise and spatial resolution preservation in 3D point cloud data.

Method: 1) Rasterize classified 3D point clouds; 2) Apply a fully convolutional recurrent neural network (trained on synthetic data) for noise reduction; 3) Morphological operations for refinement; 4) Track centerline extraction; 5) Smoothing to remove irregularities; 6) Transfer 3D LiDAR information onto 2D polylines with vertical smoothing; 7) Derive single centerline for both tracks using Dynamic Time Warping (DTW).

Result: Experimental validation confirms high-quality rail track extraction with minimal manual intervention, producing rail top centerlines and track centerlines for rail pairs.

Conclusion: The proposed method effectively extracts rail tracks from 3D point clouds with high accuracy, leveraging synthetic training data and a spatial-resolution-preserving neural architecture combined with post-processing refinement.

Abstract: Rail track extraction is essential for effective railway asset management and maintenance, especially in automated inspection and mapping workflows. This paper introduces a novel method for extracting rail tracks from classified 3D point clouds using a fully convolutional recurrent neural network that preserves full spatial resolution and is trained exclusively on synthetically generated data. This approach enhances per-pixel quality and is particularly suited for rail track extraction. The proposed method begins by rasterizing points corresponding to railroad tracks, then applies the neural network to reduce noise and yield a cleaner track representation suitable for vectorization [1]. Subsequent morphological operations further refine the resultant data, enabling accurate track centerline extraction. Next, the extracted centerlines undergo smoothing to eliminate residual irregularities [2, 3]. Finally, the algorithm transfers 3D information from lidar points onto 2D polylines and applies additional vertical smoothing. A single centerline for both tracks is found using the Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) algorithm [4]. The final outcome consists of rail top centerlines and track centerlines derived for rail pairs, with minimal manual intervention. Experimental validation confirms the effectiveness of this method in yielding high-quality rail track extraction.

</details>


### [12] [WildCity: A Real-World City-Scale Testbed for Rendering, Simulation, and Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06838)
*Xiangyu Han,Mengyu Yang,Jiaqi Li,Bowen Chang,Ziyu Chen,Hexu Zhao,Rahul Kumar Agrawal,Anthony Rodriguez,Fiona Hua,Marco Pavone,Chen Feng,Yiming Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WildCity is a real-world multimodal dataset of 18 city-scale trajectories (avg. 83.7 km each) collected by autonomous fleets in complex urban environments, designed to enable city-scale 3D reconstruction and simulation-ready digital twins for AI spatial intelligence research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing foundation models struggle to build spatial representations at city-scale due to the lack of real-world, large-scale multimodal datasets capturing complex urban environments with in-the-wild challenges such as dynamic objects, lighting variations, and imperfect camera poses.

Method: The authors collect 18 trajectories averaging 83.7 km using autonomous fleets traversing complex urban environments, preserving realistic perception challenges. They establish an urban-tailored reconstruction baseline and convert the reconstructed environments into a closed-loop simulator.

Result: A real-world multimodal dataset at city-scale with 18 long trajectories, accompanying reconstruction baseline and simulator, along with systematic analysis of key challenges including scalability, extrapolation, and uncertainty for simulation-ready urban digital twins.

Conclusion: WildCity aims to advance city-scale neural rendering and push toward AI systems capable of perceiving, remembering, and reasoning across space at a scale comparable to human spatial cognition.

Abstract: Humans can navigate an unfamiliar city and gradually form a coherent spatial mental map spanning tens of square kilometers. Can AI build spatial representations at a comparable scale? Although recent foundation models have advanced scene reconstruction and embodied intelligence, scaling to entire cities remains an open challenge, primarily due to the lack of city-scale data. To bridge the gap, we introduce WildCity, a real-world multimodal dataset collected by autonomous fleets traversing complex urban environments. Our dataset includes 18 trajectories, each averaging 83.7 kilometers in length, and preserves the core challenges of in-the-wild perception, e.g., dynamic objects, lighting variations, and imperfect camera poses. We further establish an urban-tailored reconstruction baseline and convert the reconstructed environments into a closed-loop simulator. Beyond the dataset and baseline, we systematically analyze the key challenges on the path to simulation-ready urban digital twins: scalability, extrapolation, and uncertainty. Ultimately, WildCity aims to catalyze progress not only in city-scale rendering, but more broadly in the pursuit of AI that can perceive, remember, and reason across space at a scale comparable to human cognition. Project page: https://han-xiangyu.github.io/Wild-City/

</details>


### [13] [Retrieving and Refining Winning Noise Tickets for Diffusion-Based Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06843)
*Sakuya Ota,Qing Yu,Kent Fujiwara,Satoshi Ikehata,Ikuro Sato*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: WINRO is a training-free framework that improves text-to-motion alignment by selecting and refining "winning noise tickets" in the Gaussian noise space before diffusion sampling, achieving better semantic consistency and temporal robustness without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based text-to-motion models suffer from semantic drift between input text and generated motions, especially in compositional and long-duration sequences requiring consistent semantics across multiple action segments and smooth kinematic transitions.

Method: WINRO (WInning Noise Retrieval and Optimization) maps random noises to motion features under null prompts, retrieves the best-aligned noise for a given text, and refines it via a KL-regularized objective that reduces the semantic gap while preserving the Gaussian prior. An optional LoRA-based adapter amortizes this refinement into a single forward pass.

Result: Consistently improves text-motion fidelity across MDM and MotionLCM on HumanML3D without retraining, improves temporal robustness on the MTT benchmark, and generalizes to motion stylization and spatial constraint satisfaction.

Conclusion: The initial noise is central to semantic consistency in diffusion-based motion generation; selecting and optimizing "winning noise tickets" effectively bridges the text-motion gap in a training-free, model-agnostic manner.

Abstract: Diffusion-based text-to-motion models synthesize realistic human motions but often exhibit semantic drift from the input text. Motion is inherently temporal, especially in compositional and long-duration sequences that require semantic consistency across multiple action segments and smooth kinematic transitions throughout the trajectory. We posit that the initial noise is central to this consistency: within the Gaussian noise space, certain instances, i.e. winning noise tickets, carry latent structure that biases denoising toward particular motion semantics, even under null prompts. We propose WInning Noise Retrieval and Optimization (WINRO), a training-free, model-agnostic framework that improves text-motion alignment by selecting and refining such tickets before diffusion sampling. WINRO maps random noises to motion features generated under null prompts, retrieves the best-aligned noise for a given text, and refines it via a KL-regularized objective that reduces the residual semantic gap while preserving the Gaussian prior. An optional LoRA-based adapter amortizes this refinement into a single forward pass. WINRO consistently improves text-motion fidelity across different base models, MDM and MotionLCM, on HumanML3D without retraining, improves temporal robustness on the MTT benchmark, and generalizes to applications such as motion stylization and spatial constraint satisfaction.

</details>


### [14] [Gen4U: Unifying Video Generation and Understanding via Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06856)
*Michael King,Aravindh Mahendran,Matthew Koichi Grimes,Fedor Kitashov,Adham Elarabawy,Pedro Velez,Maks Ovsjanikov,Viorica Pătrăucean*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Video diffusion models (e.g., Gen4U) encode highly structured visual representations that bridge low-level geometry and high-level semantics, serving as competitive frozen video encoders across diverse tasks without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows diffusion representations capture low-level geometry but struggle with high-level semantics. The authors aim to investigate whether video diffusion models overcome this limitation and can serve as general-purpose video encoders.

Method: Systematic probing of intermediate activations of state-of-the-art video diffusion models using mutual-kNN alignment metrics across depth and noise levels; introducing Gen4U, a framework using frozen video diffusion representations with a single forward pass for downstream tasks.

Result: Moderate noise levels yield linearly separable global semantics; fine-grained details persist at lower noise levels but are spatially scattered (require attention). Gen4U achieves strong performance on classification, depth estimation, camera pose estimation, and captioning without fine-tuning, while preserving generative ability.

Conclusion: Frozen large-scale video diffusion models function as highly competitive video encoders across semantic and non-semantic tasks. Gen4U unifies generation and understanding, bypassing the need for fine-tuning.

Abstract: Prior work suggests that diffusion representations capture low-level geometry but struggle with high-level semantics. We demonstrate that state-of-the-art video diffusion models overcome this limitation. By systematically probing their intermediate activations using recent mutual-kNN alignment metrics, we reveal a highly structured latent space where visual representations evolve across both network depth and noise levels. We show that while moderate noise levels yield linearly separable global semantics, fine-grained details persist at lower noise levels but become spatially scattered, requiring attention mechanisms to decode. Building on these insights, we introduce Gen4U (Generation for Understanding), a framework that repurposes these generative representations with a single forward pass. Our experiments establish that frozen, large-scale video diffusion models function as highly competitive video encoders across a wide spectrum of tasks, spanning semantic and non-semantic objectives (video classification, depth estimation, camera pose estimation, image and video captioning). Bypassing fine-tuning, Gen4U unifies the generation and understanding paradigms, achieving strong perception performance while fully preserving the model's ability to generate high-quality video.

</details>


### [15] [Geometric Collapse: When Vision Models Fail to Verify Physical Causality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06871)
*Wentao Zhang,Jinhu Qi,Weiqiang Jin,Yifei Zhang,Chan-Tong Lam,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Scrambled Edges exposes that dense geometric predictors (depth estimators) lack physical plausibility checks: injecting edge-like cues that violate surface/illumination/occlusion coherence causes up to 3.2x larger depth errors than noise, with global error propagation that even oracle-level repair cannot fully fix.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It remains unclear whether large-scale self-supervised learning for dense geometric prediction yields inference-time physical plausibility checks.

Method: Propose Scrambled Edges, a controlled counterfactual with energy-matched and structure-matched controls; test on CNN/ViT/SSL depth predictors (NYU Depth v2, KITTI) and additional diffusion/flow-matching models.

Result: Scrambled Edges induce up to 3.2x larger deviation than energy-matched noise; diffusion/flow-matching models show attenuated but still significant collapse. Output-level repair recovers only 47%, with substantial error outside the mask.

Conclusion: Dense predictors lack reliable mechanisms to quarantine physically unsupported edge cues, motivating explicit plausibility scoring and selective cue integration.

Abstract: Recent progress in large-scale self-supervised learning has improved dense geometric prediction, but it remains unclear whether such scaling yields inference-time physical plausibility checks. We propose Scrambled Edges, a controlled counterfactual that injects salient edge-like cues while violating surface continuity, illumination coherence, and occlusion ordering. With energy-matched and structure-matched controls, we isolate the effect of unsupported edge evidence from high-frequency energy and edge sparsity. Across CNN/ViT/SSL depth predictors on NYU Depth v2 and KITTI, Scrambled Edges induce up to 3.2x larger deviation from clean predictions than energy-matched noise; additional diffusion and flow-matching depth estimators show attenuated but still significant collapse. The resulting Geometric Collapse propagates globally: even with oracle knowledge of the corrupted region, output-level repair recovers only 47%, with substantial error outside the mask. These findings provide controlled behavioral evidence that current dense predictors lack reliable mechanisms to quarantine physically unsupported edge cues, motivating explicit plausibility scoring and selective cue integration.

</details>


### [16] [Ensemble Deep Learning Approaches for AI-Altered Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06872)
*Laiba Khan,Hung-Mao Wu,Wei Lin,Frank Bi,Yousef Abdelhadi,Joshua Jung*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multimodal deepfake detection system combining audio (AASIST) and visual (EfficientNet, XceptionNet, MesoNet) analysis with ensemble fusion (mean averaging and stacking) improves robustness across diverse deepfakes, though generalization remains challenging with ~70% average accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid increase in AI-generated videos makes distinguishing real from manipulated content difficult. Existing single-model detection methods struggle to generalize across different deepfake types, motivating the need for a more robust multimodal ensemble approach.

Method: The pipeline takes a video, separates audio (analyzed by AASIST), and extracts face frames via MTCNN (analyzed by EfficientNet, XceptionNet, MesoNet). Each model outputs a fake likelihood score, combined via mean averaging (simple baseline) and stacking (trained meta-model for learned combination).

Result: Individual models perform well on in-distribution datasets but degrade on diverse data. The ensemble approach improves robustness and consistency across deepfake types. Average accuracy across unseen manipulations is around 70%, highlighting generalization as the main challenge.

Conclusion: Combining audio and visual modalities via ensemble fusion is more reliable than single-modality approaches for deepfake detection. However, generalizing to unseen manipulations remains the central open challenge, with current accuracy plateauing at ~70%.

Abstract: The increasing accessibility of artificial intelligence has led to a rapid rise in AI-generated videos, making it more difficult to distinguish between real and manipulated content. Many existing detection methods rely on a single model and often struggle to generalize across different types of deepfakes. In this work, we developed a multimodal deepfake detection system that combines both audio and visual analysis using an ensemble of models. The system includes AASIST for audio-based detection, and EfficientNet, XceptionNet, and MesoNet for analyzing visual features in video frames. The pipeline takes a video as input, separates the audio, and extracts face frames using MTCNN. Each model produces a score indicating the likelihood of the input being fake. These scores are then combined using ensemble strategies, including mean averaging and stacking. Mean fusion provides a simple and stable baseline, while stacking uses a trained meta-model to learn how to combine predictions more effectively. Results show that while individual models perform well on the datasets they were trained on, their performance drops when tested on more diverse datasets. The ensemble approach helps improve overall robustness by combining predictions from multiple models, leading to more consistent performance across different types of deepfakes. This suggests that using both audio and visual information together is a more reliable approach for deepfake detection. Our results highlight generalization to unseen manipulations as the central open challenge, with average accuracy around 70%.

</details>


### [17] [Video2Reaction: Mapping Video to Audience Reaction Distribution in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06875)
*Trang Nguyen,Sidong Zhang,Shiv Shankar,Gauri Jagatap,Deepak Chandran,Andrea Fanelli,Madalina Fiterau*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Video2Reaction is a multimodal dataset mapping short movie segments to viewer-induced emotions expressed on social media. It includes 10,000+ videos with annotations via a two-stage multi-agent LLM pipeline (86% correctness). Foundation video models fail zero-shot but fine-tuning achieves SOTA (77% Top-3 F1 for LLaVA-Next), though the task remains challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable audience reaction prediction and content engagement applications, as understanding and forecasting audience reactions to video content is crucial for improving content creation, recommendation systems, and media analysis.

Method: Constructed a multimodal dataset (Video2Reaction) with 10,000+ short movie segments mapping to viewer-induced emotions from social media. Used a two-stage multi-agent pipeline with open-source LLMs for cost-effective continuous annotations, achieving 86% correctness under blind human verification. Evaluated pretrained foundation video models in zero-shot and fine-tuned settings for reaction distribution prediction.

Result: Pretrained foundation video models fail in zero-shot settings. Fine-tuning transforms them into state-of-the-art predictors. Best method (LLaVA-Next) achieves only 77% Top-3 F1 in dominant reaction prediction, indicating substantial room for improvement.

Conclusion: Video2Reaction serves as a reliable benchmark for audience reaction prediction. The task remains challenging despite achieving SOTA results, highlighting a gap in modeling collective audience reactions from video alone.

Abstract: Understanding and forecasting audience reactions to video content are crucial for improving content creation, recommendation systems, and media analysis. To enable audience reaction prediction and other content engagement applications, we introduce $\textbf{Video2Reaction}$, a multimodal dataset that maps short movie segments to a distribution of $\textit{induced emotions}$ of viewers in the wild, as expressed through social media. $\textbf{Video2Reaction}$ spans more than 10,000 videos and serves as a reliable benchmark as well as a training resource for audience reaction prediction. To enable cost-effective continuous annotations as reactions may change over time, we develop a two-stage multi-agent pipeline using only open-source LLMs, achieving 86% correctness under blind human verification despite the inherently noisy and subjective nature of the task. We establish the first benchmark for video-to-reaction-distribution prediction in the wild and show that pretrained foundation video models fail in zero-shot settings, while finetuning transforms them into state-of-the-art predictors capable of modeling both full reaction distributions and dominant responses from video alone. However, the task remains challenging: even the strongest methods achieve only 77% Top-3 F1 in dominant reaction prediction (LLaVA-Next), highlighting a substantial gap in modeling collective audience reaction. \modification{Dataset and code are available at our project page: https://information-fusion-lab-umass.github.io/video2reaction-bench.github.io

</details>


### [18] [ReMoDEx: A Local-to-Global Relevance-Based Model Decision Explainability Framework for large-Scale Image Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06889)
*Abhay Kumar Pathak,Mrityunjay Chaubey,Manjari Gupta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ReMoDEx is a scalable framework for dataset-level explanation of deep image classifiers, combining local explainers (GradCAM++, Integrated Gradients, Occlusion Sensitivity, LRP) with a global clustering module to automatically group decision strategies. Applied to a VGG16 COVID-19 chest X-ray classifier (86.27% accuracy, 0.9624 AUC), it revealed two recurring strategies—central thoracic vs. border/corner reliance—indicating shortcut learning invisible to accuracy metrics alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning image classifiers are opaque; existing explanation methods (like heatmaps) require per-sample inspection, which does not scale to large datasets. This makes it hard to detect shortcut learning, spurious correlations, or reliance on irrelevant features at dataset scale.

Method: ReMoDEx pipeline: (1) model inference, (2) target class selection, (3) relevance map generation (using GradCAM++, Integrated Gradients, Occlusion Sensitivity, or LRP), (4) heatmap standardization, (5) similarity-based clustering of relevance patterns, (6) cluster-level interpretation, (7) spatial relevance assessment. Each local explainer is paired with a single global clustering module.

Result: Applied to a VGG16 COVID-19 chest X-ray classifier (test accuracy 86.27%, AUC 0.9624). All four explainers consistently revealed two decision strategies: central thoracic region decisions and border/corner sensitive decisions, indicating shortcut learning. Masked image validation confirmed confidence/class changes when central or peripheral regions were occluded.

Conclusion: ReMoDEx provides a scalable, automated framework for assessing model decision behavior at dataset scale, serving as an essential complement to traditional accuracy-based evaluation by exposing shortcut learning and spurious correlations that metrics alone cannot reveal.

Abstract: Deep learning image classifiers achieve strong predictive performance yet remain opaque in how decisions are formed. A model may predict correctly while relying on irrelevant cues, shortcut associations, peripheral structures, or device level artifacts instead of task relevant regions. On large scale datasets this opacity is especially problematic, since inspecting heatmaps one sample at a time cannot scale to thousands of predictions. We propose Relevance Based Model Decision Explainability (ReMoDEx), a framework for systematic, dataset scale assessment of model decision behaviour in image classification. ReMoDEx defines a stepwise pipeline: model inference, target class selection, relevance map generation, heatmap standardisation, similarity based grouping of patterns, cluster level interpretation, and spatial relevance assessment. Local methods GradCAM++, Integrated Gradients, Occlusion Sensitivity, and Layerwise Relevance Propagation are each combined independently with a single global module that summarises an entire set of relevance maps into a few decision strategy clusters, replacing sample by sample inspection with an automatic, scalable summary. To demonstrate ReMoDEx, we applied it to a VGG16 based classifier distinguishing COVID-19, Normal, Lung Opacity, and Viral Pneumonia. The classifier showed stable performance (86.27% test accuracy, 0.9624 test AUC). However, each explainer combined with the global module consistently produced two recurring strategies: central thoracic region decisions and border/corner sensitive decisions, indicating possible shortcut learning that conventional metrics could not reveal. Masked image validation confirmed that model confidence and predicted class changed when central or peripheral regions were occluded. ReMoDEx thus provides a scalable relevance based decision assessment framework and an essential complement to accuracy based evaluation.

</details>


### [19] [Seeing What Matters: Lesion-Aware High-Resolution Patch Discovery and Fusion for Chest X-ray Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06909)
*Yingshu Li,Yunyi Liu,Zhenghao Chen,Tong Chen,Zailong Chen,Lingqiao Liu,Lei Wang,Luping Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LePaX is the first RRG framework enabling efficient high-resolution (up to 1920x1920) chest X-ray perception without increasing vision-token count. It uses Learnable Spatial Resolution Allocation (LSRA) to adaptively allocate high-res capacity to diagnostically relevant regions and Global-Regional Fusion (GRF) for token-preserving region-to-global refinement. It achieves over 10x fewer visual tokens than naive tiling while improving clinical and linguistic metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RRG systems use heavily downsampled inputs (e.g., 256x256) due to fixed visual token budgets of pretrained vision encoders, suppressing clinically important cues in native-resolution CXRs. Naive high-res tiling causes token inflation, while global compression suppresses subtle lesions.

Method: LePaX formulates high-res perception as a constrained spatial resolution allocation problem under fixed token budget. It introduces (1) LSRA, which learns a spatial utility map to adaptively allocate limited high-res capacity to diagnostically relevant regions, and (2) GRF, which performs token-preserving region-to-global refinement via spatially aligned resolution write-back onto the global feature grid.

Result: LePaX consistently improves both clinical and linguistic metrics on multiple CXR benchmarks, and enables native-resolution CXR perception with over 10x fewer visual tokens than naive high-res tiling.

Conclusion: LePaX demonstrates efficient high-resolution CXR perception for RRG without token inflation, inspired by radiologists' workflow of localizing suspicious regions before detailed assessment.

Abstract: Despite rapid advances in chest X-ray (CXR) foundation models, most radiology report generation (RRG) systems still rely on heavily downsampled inputs (e.g., 256x256) due to the fixed visual token budgets of pretrained vision encoders, suppressing subtle yet clinically important cues present in native-resolution images. However, enabling high-resolution (high-res) perception remains challenging: naive tiling causes prohibitive token inflation, while global compression suppresses subtle lesions and degrades diagnostic fidelity. Inspired by radiologists' workflow, localizing suspicious regions before detailed high-res assessment. We propose Lesion-Aware High-Resolution Patch Discovery and Fusion for Chest X-ray Reporting (LePaX), the first RRG framework that enables efficient high-res CXR perception (up to 1920x1920) without increasing the vision-token count. LePaX formulates high-res perception as a constrained spatial resolution allocation problem under a fixed token budget and introduces two key components: Learnable Spatial Resolution Allocation (LSRA), which learns a spatial utility map that adaptively allocates limited high-res capacity to diagnostically relevant regions, enabling targeted extraction of high-res patches from native CXRs; and Global-Regional Fusion (GRF), which performs token-preserving region-to-global refinement by projecting high-resolution regional evidence back onto the global feature grid through spatially aligned resolution write-back, avoiding token inflation. Experiments on multiple CXR benchmarks demonstrate that LePaX consistently improves both clinical and linguistic metrics while enabling native-resolution CXR perception with over 10x fewer visual tokens than naive high-res tiling.

</details>


### [20] [Smart Scissor: Coupling Spatial Redundancy Reduction and CNN Compression for Embedded Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06915)
*Hao Kong,Di Liu,Shuo Huai,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Smart Scissor is a unified CNN compression framework that combines dynamic image cropping (foreground-aware cropping via a lightweight predictor) with compound shrinking (co-compressing depth, width, and resolution) to reduce computational overhead while maintaining accuracy, achieving 41.5% FLOPs reduction on ResNet50 with +0.3% top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly downscaling input images loses foreground object details due to spatial redundancy (e.g., background pixels), causing accuracy degradation. Additionally, computational redundancy exists in CNN architectures themselves. Prior compression methods do not jointly address both input-level and architecture-level redundancy.

Method: (1) Dynamic image cropping: a lightweight foreground predictor localizes and crops the foreground object instance-wise, enabling small-resolution recognition without losing key features. (2) Compound shrinking: coordinately compresses three CNN dimensions (depth, width, resolution) for embedded deployment. (3) Unified framework (Smart Scissor) seamlessly integrates both strategies.

Result: On ImageNet-1K with ResNet50: 41.5% computational cost reduction with 0.3% top-1 accuracy improvement. Compared to HRank (SOTA), achieves 4.1% higher top-1 accuracy at the same computational cost.

Conclusion: Jointly addressing spatial redundancy via dynamic cropping and architectural redundancy via compound shrinking is effective for edge AI, significantly reducing computational overhead while maintaining or even improving accuracy.

Abstract: Scaling down the resolution of input images can greatly reduce the computational overhead of convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which is promising for edge AI. However, as an image usually contains much spatial redundancy, e.g., background pixels, directly shrinking the whole image will lose important features of the foreground object and lead to severe accuracy degradation. In this paper, we propose a dynamic image cropping framework to reduce the spatial redundancy by accurately cropping the foreground object from images. To achieve the instance-aware fine cropping, we introduce a lightweight foreground predictor to efficiently localize and crop the foreground of an image. The finely cropped images can be correctly recognized even at a small resolution. Meanwhile, computational redundancy also exists in CNN architectures. To pursue higher execution efficiency on resource-constrained embedded devices, we also propose a compound shrinking strategy to coordinately compress the three dimensions (depth, width, resolution) of CNNs. Eventually, we seamlessly combine the proposed dynamic image cropping and compound shrinking into a unified compression framework, Smart Scissor, which is expected to significantly reduce the computational overhead of CNNs while still maintaining high accuracy. Experiments on ImageNet-1K demonstrate that our method reduces the computational cost of ResNet50 by 41.5% while improving the top-1 accuracy by 0.3%. Moreover, compared to HRank, the state-of-the-art CNN compression framework, our method achieves 4.1% higher top-1 accuracy at the same computational cost. The codes and data are available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/smart-scissor

</details>


### [21] [LoCA: Spatially-Aware Low-Rank Convolutional Adaptation of Vision Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06918)
*Sojung An,Junha Lee,Sujeong You,Nam Ik Cho,Donghyun Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LoCA is a convolution-aware PEFT framework that decouples channel and spatial adaptation via low-rank adaptation, outperforming LoRA on fine-grained classification, domain-generalized semantic segmentation, and generative tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LoRA-based adaptation is designed for 2D matrices in transformer self-attention layers, but convolutional kernels are 4D tensors coupling spatial and channel information. Forcing them into 2D matrices disrupts inherent spatial topology, leading to suboptimal adaptation for convolutional VFMs.

Method: LoCA decouples channel and spatial adaptation by: (1) low-rank channel adaptation for dense cross-channel mixing, and (2) refining spatial bases extracted from pre-trained kernels via SVD to preserve spatial priors.

Result: LoCA preserves pre-trained spatial priors and achieves competitive or state-of-the-art performance across fine-grained classification, domain-generalized semantic segmentation, and generative benchmarks.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that convolution-aware PEFT (LoCA) outperforms LoRA by properly handling the 4D tensor structure of convolutional kernels, establishing a new paradigm for adapting convolutional vision foundation models.

Abstract: Pre-trained Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) provide strong visual representations for diverse downstream tasks. The key challenge of VFM adaptation stems from the prohibitive costs of full fine-tuning and catastrophic forgetting. To address this, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has emerged as the prevailing paradigm for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT). However, LoRA is typically designed for transformer self-attention layers parameterized by 2D matrices. Since convolutional kernels inherently couple spatial and channel information within a 4D tensor, forcing them into a monolithic 2D matrix disrupts the inherent spatial topology. In this paper, we propose Low-Rank Convolutional Adaptation (LoCA), a convolution-aware PEFT framework that addresses spatial-channel entanglement by decoupling channel and spatial adaptation. LoCA introduces a low-rank channel adaptation for dense cross-channel mixing and refines spatial bases extracted from pre-trained kernels via Singular Value Decomposition (SVD). Experimental results show that LoCA preserves pre-trained spatial priors and achieves competitive or state-of-the-art performance across fine-grained classification, domain-generalized semantic segmentation, and generative benchmarks.

</details>


### [22] [Compass: Prostate Cancer Detection Needs Multi-View Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06919)
*Paul F. R. Wilson,Mohamed Harmanani,Zhuoxin Guo,Obed K. Dzikunu,Hannes Cash,Adam Kinnaird,Brian Wodlinger,Purang Abolmaesumi,Parvin Mousavi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Compass is a novel AI method that analyzes micro-ultrasound video streams (rather than single images) for prostate cancer detection, using a transformer conditioned on probe rotation angle to aggregate evidence across frames, achieving strong performance on multi-center clinical trial data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI methods for micro-ultrasound prostate cancer detection analyze single images in isolation, whereas expert clinicians assess full video studies with three-dimensional context. This gap motivates the need for AI that can leverage multi-view, video-level information to improve detection.

Method: Compass models a micro-ultrasound study as a stream of 2D images, jointly integrates rotational sweep videos with biopsy frames, and uses a transformer conditioned on the probe's rotational angle for evidence aggregation across the study. A decoder head predicts both frame-level and study-level risk scores.

Result: The model demonstrates strong performance on a multi-center clinical trial dataset, outperforming baseline AI methods and comparing favorably to clinical expert risk scores.

Conclusion: Multi-view contextual aggregation via transformers enhances micro-ultrasound-based prostate cancer detection, offering a potentially powerful tool to complement human expertise in diagnosis.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) analysis of micro-ultrasound ($μ$US) has shown promise for prostate cancer (PCa) detection. However, most existing AI methods focus on the analysis of single $μ$US images in isolation. By contrast, expert $μ$US readers typically assess a full recorded video study, which provides three-dimensional context, to improve PCa detection compared to single-frame analysis. Inspired by this clinical workflow, we propose Compass, a novel AI methodology which models a $μ$US study as a stream of 2D images. Compass jointly integrates rotational sweep videos of the prostate with $μ$US frames acquired at the moment of biopsy, and performs evidence aggregation across the study using a transformer conditioned on the probe's rotational angle. Finally, a decoder head predicts frame-level and study-level risk scores for the patient. The model is trained and evaluated using a multi-center clinical trial dataset of $μ$US studies, including continuous rotational scans of the prostate and videos captured during biopsy acquisition. We compare the proposed method to baseline AI methods from the literature and to risk scores provided by clinical experts. Our framework shows strong performance, highlighting the value of multi-view context for $μ$US PCa detection, and providing a potentially powerful tool to complement human expertise in $μ$US-based PCa diagnosis. Our code is available at: https://github.com/mharmanani/Compass.

</details>


### [23] [Bi-PT: Bidirectional Cross-Attention Point Transformers for Four-Chamber Heart Reconstruction from Sparse Cardiac MRI Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06923)
*Chenchuhui Hu,Shaoming Pan,Leon Axel,Meng Ye*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Bi-PT reconstructs 3D four-chamber heart meshes from sparse clinical CMR data using bidirectional point cross-attention and a Neural ODE-based deformation field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical CMR protocols produce only sparse 2D long-axis and short-axis views, making accurate 3D cardiac shape reconstruction error-prone; Bi-PT addresses this challenge.

Method: Uses bidirectional point cross-attention between an atlas and sparse point cloud (SPC) with per-point semantic labels; deforms the atlas via a NODE-parameterized locally affine diffeomorphic field; integrates semantic label loss into Chamfer distance with smoothness regularization.

Result: Extensive experiments show Bi-PT achieves accurate and robust performance compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Bi-PT enables reliable 3D four-chamber heart mesh reconstruction from clinically sparse CMR data.

Abstract: We propose Bi-PT, a pipeline for reconstructing 3D four-chamber human heart meshes from clinical sparsely sampled cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (CMR) data. This work addresses the error-prone generation of 3D cardiac shape from a sparse point cloud (SPC) extracted from 2D long-axis and short-axis views used in routine clinical CMR protocols. Bi-PT enables accurate inference of the four-chamber heart mesh from the SPC by learning robust point features via bidirectional point cross-attention between an atlas and the SPC, together with per-point semantic labels that improve correspondence estimation. We formulate the deformation field as a Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (NODE) parameterized by a per-point affine transformation and translation to deform the atlas toward the target heart shape. By learning such a NODE, we can guarantee the deformation field to be a locally affine diffeomorphic deformation. We also integrate a semantic label loss into the Chamfer distance to encourage label-consistent correspondences and add a smoothness regularization to stabilize and improve the learning of the deformation field. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Bi-PT achieves accurate and robust performance compared to baselines.

</details>


### [24] [General Incomplete Multimodal Learning via Dynamic Quality Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06943)
*Xiangyu Meng,Shicai Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GIML is a unified framework for multimodal learning that handles both missing modalities (inter-modality missing) and corrupted modalities (intra-modality degradation) via dynamic quality perception, using a Noise-aware Quality Estimator and a Noise-Semantic Decoupled module.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal learning methods only address inter-modality missing (entire modalities absent) but ignore intra-modality degradation (modalities present but severely corrupted). In practice, both types coexist, making current approaches ineffective.

Method: GIML models heterogeneous missing patterns as continuous modality information degradation, enabling degradation-aware adaptive fusion. It introduces a Noise-aware Quality Estimator (learning corrupted features → noise intensity mapping via controlled noise injection) and a Noise-Semantic Decoupled module (separating semantic information from noise interference).

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse modality types demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of GIML over existing methods.

Conclusion: GIML provides a unified and robust solution for multimodal learning under both inter-modality missing and intra-modality degradation, with improved generalization to unseen corruption patterns.

Abstract: Multimodal learning robust to missing modalities is essential for real-world applications. Existing methods mainly focus on inter-modality missing, where entire modalities are absent, while overlooking intra-modality degradation, where modalities are present but severely corrupted. In practice, these two types of missing often coexist, making existing approaches ineffective. To address this limitation, we propose General Incomplete Multimodal Learning (GIML), a unified framework that simultaneously handles both inter-modality missing and intra-modality degradation through dynamic quality perception. Specifically, GIML models heterogeneous missing patterns as continuous modality information degradation, enabling degradation-aware adaptive fusion. To achieve reliable quality perception, we introduce a Noise-aware Quality Estimator that learns the mapping from corrupted features to noise intensity through controlled noise injection. Furthermore, we propose a Noise-Semantic Decoupled module that separates semantic information from noise interference. This improves robustness and generalization to unseen corruption patterns. Extensive experiments across datasets with diverse modality types demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of GIML. Code is available at: https://github.com/Yu-Five/GIML.

</details>


### [25] [Self-Supervised Pretraining Improves Cross-Site and Cross-Scale Robustness of Point Cloud Leaf-Wood Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06948)
*Heeju Mun,Tackang Yang,Yunsoo Nam,Changhyun Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study demonstrates that self-supervised learning (SSL) pretraining with Point-M2AE on tree point clouds significantly improves leaf-wood segmentation accuracy and generalization across forest types, scales, and climatic zones, while also reducing wood volume estimation error by more than half compared to algorithmic baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing leaf-wood segmentation methods show inconsistent accuracy across different forest types and sites. SSL has shown promise for other forestry point cloud tasks (biomass regression, tree segmentation) but its applicability to leaf-wood segmentation had not been tested.

Method: Pretrained Point-M2AE (SSL architecture) on ShapeNet-55 augmented with 2,400 individual tree point clouds. Used recursive voxel subdivision to handle point density variation across scales. Fine-tuned and tested at both individual-tree and plot scales without architecture changes.

Result: SSL pretraining improved wood IoU from 60.5%→70.0% (needleleaf) and 69.7%→76.3% (broadleaf). Achieved smallest cross-site variation across four countries/three climatic zones. Plot-level mIoU: 84.7% (broadleaf), 77.7% (needleleaf). Wood volume estimation MAE = 2.40 m³, less than half of LeWos (5.94 m³) and CWLS (5.27 m³).

Conclusion: SSL pretraining effectively improves leaf-wood segmentation generalization and downstream wood volume estimation, outperforming existing methods (LeWos, CWLS, PointTransformer) across diverse forest types and scales.

Abstract: The accuracy of existing leaf-wood segmentation methods for tree point clouds varies across forest types and sites. Self-supervised learning (SSL) on point clouds has improved the generalization of deep learning models for forestry point cloud tasks, including biomass regression and individual tree segmentation, but its applicability to leaf-wood segmentation remains untested. In this study, we pretrained Point-M2AE, a widely used SSL architecture for point clouds, on ShapeNet-55 augmented with 2,400 individual tree point clouds. For fine-tuning and inference, we used recursive voxel subdivision to handle the wide variation in point density across inputs, allowing the same model to operate at both individual-tree and plot scales without architecture change. Compared to the model without pretraining, the pretrained model improved wood IoU from 60.5% to 70.0% for needleleaf and from 69.7% to 76.3% for broadleaf trees.
