Motivation: Existing student-teacher regression frameworks for diffusion samplers sacrifice high-frequency texture fidelity while preserving coarse structures under low-NFE constraints, misaligning samplers with perceptual quality.

Method: D2PO reformulates sampler optimization as preference-based alignment via DPO, models sampling policy as an EBM to transform preference comparisons into energy differences, derives a novel energy formulation from the pretrained score network for perturbed-space evaluation, and introduces dynamic preferences where preferred samples progressively improve as policies are learned.

Result: D2PO aligns diffusion samplers with perceptual quality more faithfully, unlocks full potential of high-quality teachers, and consistently outperforms conventional regression-based schedulers under low-NFE constraints.

Conclusion: D2PO provides a principled preference-guided refinement process that replaces rigid static teacher supervision with iterative self-improvement, yielding stronger alignment signals and superior perceptual quality.

Abstract: We propose D2PO (Dynamic Direct Preference Optimization), a principled framework for optimizing diffusion sampling policies with respect to timestep schedules and classifier-free guidance (CFG) weights. Our work is motivated by a fundamental limitation of existing student-teacher regression frameworks; low-NFE student samplers are trained to mimic high-NFEteachers, often sacrificing high-frequency texture fidelity while preserving coarse global structures, thereby misaligning the sampler with perceptual quality. D2PO addresses this challenge by reformulating sampler optimization as a preference-based alignment problem, leveraging the Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) framework. To make DPO applicable to diffusion samplers, we model the sampling policy as an energy-based model (EBM), transforming preference comparisons into tractable energy differences. We further introduce a novel energy formulation derived directly from the pretrained score network, enabling preference evaluation in perturbed spaces that jointly capture structural consistency and fine-grained details. Moreover, we introduce dynamic preferences, where the preferred samples used for alignment progressively improve as the sampling policies are learned. This self-improving mechanism replaces rigid static teacher supervision with an iterative, preference-guided refinement process, providing progressively stronger alignment signals. Extensive experiments demonstrate that D2PO aligns diffusion samplers with perceptual quality more faithfully, unlocking the full potential of high-quality teachers and consistently outperforming conventional regression-based schedulers under low-NFE constraints.

</details>


### [75] [Inertia-1: An Open Exploration of Wearable Motion Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06617)
*Zongzhe Xu,Aakarsh Anand,Sarah Jiang,Chuntung Zhuang,Zitao Shuai,Sriram Sankararaman,Yuzhe Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Inertia-1 is a comprehensive, open investigation into wearable motion foundation models, using over 18.2M hours of accelerometer data to systematically study the effects of data, model, and training choices. Evaluated across 15 diverse datasets, it provides state-of-the-art recipes and serves as an open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable motion sensing offers a continuous, scalable window into human behavior and health, making it a natural fit for foundation models. However, the pretraining and scaling principles for such models remain poorly understood, with prior work studying isolated design choices under fixed settings and narrow tasks that fail to capture real-world sensing diversity.

Method: The authors build Inertia-1, a controlled framework using massive corpora (18.2M+ hours) of global accelerometer data. They systematically study the full lifecycle: data choices (sensor modality, device placement, sampling rate, window length), model choices (architectures, model size), and training choices (pretraining objective, data scale). Extensive evaluations are conducted across 15 datasets covering human activity recognition, freezing-of-gait detection, and disease prediction.

Result: The study reveals intriguing findings for building motion foundation models that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions. Inertia-1 achieves state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Inertia-1 not only provides state-of-the-art recipes for various downstream tasks, but also serves as a comprehensive, practical, and open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.

Abstract: Wearable motion sensing provides a continuous and scalable window into human behavior and health, making it a natural fit for foundation models, yet its pretraining and scaling principles remain poorly understood. Prior work studies isolated design choices, such as sensor placement or sampling frequency, often under fixed settings and narrow downstream tasks that fail to capture real-world sensing diversity. We introduce Inertia-1, a fully open exploration of wearable motion foundation models. Using massive corpora of accelerometer data from global sources spanning more than 18.2M hours, we build a controlled framework for studying the full lifecycle of wearable motion foundation models, covering data choices such as sensor modality, device placement, sampling rate, window length; model choices such as architectures and model size; and training choices such as pretraining objective and data scale. Extensive evaluations across 15 datasets spanning human activity recognition, freezing-of-gait detection, and disease prediction reveal intriguing findings for building motion foundation models that generalize across tasks and sensing conditions. Collectively, Inertia-1 not only presents state-of-the-art recipes for diverse downstream tasks, but also serves as a comprehensive, practical, and open cookbook for wearable motion representation learning.

</details>


### [76] [Open-Ended Scenario Reasoning for Specialist Model Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06625)
*Youcheng Zong,Runda Jia,Ranmeng Lin,Mingxuan Ren,Dakuo He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ROAM uses LLM reasoning to adapt frozen specialist industrial models to new scenarios without retraining, by confining corrections to a low-dimensional latent space with risk-constrained fallback, reducing MAE by over 20% with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process industry specialist models degrade under sensor drift, feedstock variation, and regime switching; retraining is costly and existing adaptation methods require labeled data and parameter modifications, while LLMs as direct predictors cause hallucinations and cannot incorporate unstructured field knowledge.

Method: ROAM (Reasoning-Driven Open Adaptation for Specialist Models) uses LLM world knowledge and reasoning to adapt frozen specialist models via corrections confined to a low-dimensional, semantically interpretable latent space. LLM scenario judgments and online observations are fused under a unified probabilistic framework with a risk-constrained mechanism that suppresses corrections under unreliable LLM evidence or abrupt shifts, falling back to the original frozen model when evidence is insufficient.

Result: On a mineral thickening process and IndPenSim dataset, ROAM reduces MAE by over 20% in major shift settings (e.g., hidden shifts) with only 839 additional parameters and under 0.02 ms per-step overhead.

Conclusion: LLM reasoning can be effectively turned into a conservative adaptation signal for industrial models already in service, enabling reliable adaptation without retraining.

Abstract: Process industries have accumulated validated specialist models, yet sensor drift, feedstock variation, and regime switching cause these models to degrade systematically in new scenarios. Collecting new labeled data and retraining is costly, while continuing with the original model incurs persistent bias. Existing adaptation methods require modifying model parameters with sufficient labeled data, making rapid response on deployed systems difficult. Using LLMs as direct predictors risks hallucinations and uncontrollable outputs. Such predictors also cannot incorporate unstructured scenario knowledge from the field. To address these limitations, this article proposes Reasoning-Driven Open Adaptation for Specialist Models (ROAM), a framework that uses LLM world knowledge and reasoning to adapt frozen specialist models to unseen scenarios without retraining. ROAM confines all corrections to a low-dimensional, semantically interpretable latent space. LLM-generated scenario judgments and online observations are fused under a unified probabilistic framework. A risk-constrained mechanism suppresses corrections under unreliable LLM evidence or abrupt scenario shifts and falls back to the original frozen model when evidence is insufficient. Experiments on a mineral thickening process and the public IndPenSim penicillin fermentation dataset show that ROAM reduces MAE by over 20\% in major shift settings such as hidden shifts with only 839 additional parameters and under 0.02\,ms per-step overhead. These results indicate that LLM reasoning can be turned into a conservative adaptation signal for industrial models already in service.

</details>


### [77] [Reward Valuation in Vision Language Models: Causal Mechanisms Underlying Anhedonia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06626)
*Melika Honarmand,Samin Mahdipour Aghabagher,Martin Schrimpf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vision-Language models contain functionally identified reward-anticipatory units analogous to the human Nucleus Accumbens; perturbing these units induces anhedonia-like behavioral deficits in effort-based decision-making, mirroring clinical symptoms of major depressive disorder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether Vision-Language Models exhibit alignment with human cognition in reward valuation, and to establish a causal mechanistic link between model internal representations and reward-related behavioral deficits, inspired by neuroscientific frameworks of anhedonia in major depressive disorder.

Method: Leveraged clinical tests (DARS, MAP-SR) from anhedonia assessment in MDD to functionally identify reward-anticipatory units in VLMs, analogized to NAc/dopaminergic reward system; applied targeted perturbations to these units and measured behavioral changes in effort-based decision-making tasks.

Result: Perturbing NAc-selective units caused the model to shift toward low-effort, low-reward options, mirroring human anhedonia; baseline task performance was preserved when reward-based choice was removed, indicating a specific reward valuation deficit rather than general capability loss; behavioral patterns aligned with clinical DARS and MAP-SR scales.

Conclusion: Reward valuation circuits exist in AI models that parallel those in humans, demonstrating alignment between AI and human cognition in reward processing and offering a mechanistic framework for studying motivation deficits.

Abstract: Recent Vision-Language Models capture increasingly complex aspects of human cognition. Here we ask whether this alignment extends to reward valuation, which we assess in a mechanistic framework built on clinical tests that were developed to evaluate anhedonia and motivational deficits in major depressive disorder. In the brain, anhedonia is frequently linked to dysregulation in the Nucleus Accumbens (NAc) and the broader dopaminergic reward system. While neuroimaging has localized these deficits, establishing a causal link between NAc activity and specific behavioral symptoms remains a challenge. We use these ideas from neuroscience to functionally identify reward-anticipatory units in vision language models, and test their causal role via targeted perturbations. Perturbing NAc-selective units induces behavioral effects that mirror human anhedonia: the model shifts toward low-effort, low-reward options in effort-based decision-making tasks. Crucially, our results reflect a specific deficit in reward valuation and anticipation rather than a loss of task capability: the perturbed model maintains baseline performance when reward-based choice is removed. This induced vulnerability further aligns with clinical anhedonia and motivation scales, including DARS and MAP-SR. Taken together, these results reveal reward valuation circuits in AI models that parallel those in humans.

</details>


### [78] [Cross-Trajectory Chimera Interventions Reveal Dissociable Roles of Weight Magnitude and Direction in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06628)
*Truong Xuan Khanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-trajectory chimera interventions reveal that weight direction (not norm) causally transfers circuit identity between independently trained networks, while norm only affects susceptibility to overwriting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine which properties of a partially trained network are causally portable across different, independently trained networks, moving beyond single-trajectory intervention analysis.

Method: Cross-trajectory chimera interventions: split weight vectors into norm and unit direction, recombine one run's norm with another's direction, continue training on modular arithmetic tasks that exhibit grokking.

Result: Direction transfers donor-specific circuit identity (40/40 cases), norm only has modest distributed delay effect; transfer is threshold-like with location perfectly predicted by recipient's norm (p=1.9e-4), threshold localized to +/-1/64.

Conclusion: Direction indexes which solution trajectory the network approaches; norm governs how susceptible that identity is to being overwritten. These components dissociate and play distinct causal roles in portability.

Abstract: Which properties of a partially trained network are causally portable to a different, independently trained network? Single-trajectory interventions show necessity within one run, not portability across runs. We introduce cross-trajectory chimera interventions: given two runs from different seeds, we split each weight vector into a norm and a unit direction, recombine one run's norm with the other's direction, and continue training. On two modular-arithmetic tasks that grok, the components dissociate. Direction carries a transferable, donor-specific circuit identity: implanting a donor's direction at the recipient's norm drives the run to the donor's circuit in 40/40 cases, while an angle-matched random control yields no shift. The transfer is threshold-like, and its location is predicted by the recipient's norm, separating perfectly by norm class over all 20 pairs (joint permutation probability 1.9e-4). Norm carries only a modest, distributed delay effect and no identity signal. An adaptive bisection procedure localizes the threshold to +/-1/64. Direction indexes which solution a trajectory approaches; norm governs how susceptible that identity is to being overwritten.

</details>


### [79] [STST-JEPA: Shallow-Target Spatio-Temporal Joint Embedding Prediction Architecture For EEG Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06629)
*Roy Segal,Yoni Svechinsky,Tomer Fekete*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STST-JEPA is a self-supervised transformer pretrained on 47,703 EEG sessions (ages 5-81) that achieves state-of-the-art age regression (3.06 years MAE, r=0.924) and rank-1 leaderboard placements for sex classification, age prediction, and psychopathology regression, while its age residuals negatively correlate with cognitive efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG-based brain-age biomarkers are attractive due to EEG's low cost, portability, and temporal richness, but existing models face challenges including cross-site montage heterogeneity, small labeled datasets, and subject-level non-stationarity. No prior EEG foundation model has demonstrated competitive age regression across the full pediatric-to-older-adult range needed for real-world deployment.

Method: The model uses a self-supervised transformer with a latent-prediction objective (predicting masked-token representations against an EMA-of-tokenizer target) plus an auxiliary signal-reconstruction term, applied to 30-second multi-channel windows under spatiotemporal block masks. Pretraining on 47,703 sessions from brain.space and HBN (ages 5-81). Evaluation via lightweight attentive probe on frozen embeddings and light task-specific fine-tuning of final layers.

Result: Best held-out MAE of 3.06 years (r=0.924) for age regression on 3,367 sessions (vs. ~10 years MAE baseline). Rank-1 on NeuralBench x brain.space leaderboard: sex classification (balanced accuracy 0.911), age prediction (r=0.749), psychopathology composite regression (r=0.215). Age-prediction residual negatively correlates with cognitive efficiency.

Conclusion: STST-JEPA demonstrates that self-supervised pretraining on large-scale heterogeneous EEG data can produce a foundation model capable of accurate age regression across the full lifespan, with transferable representations that generalize to multiple downstream tasks and show clinically relevant correlations with cognitive function.

Abstract: Brain age -- the age inferred from a physiological recording -- is an emerging biomarker whose deviation from chronological age tracks neurological and psychiatric burden, and EEG is an attractive substrate for it because it is cheap, portable, and temporally rich. Yet EEG brain-age models must contend with cross-site montage heterogeneity, small labelled cohorts, and dominant subject-level non-stationarity, and few EEG foundation models have been shown to deliver competitive age regression across the full pediatric-to-older-adult range in which such a biomarker would actually be deployed. We introduce STST-JEPA, a self-supervised transformer for resting-state and task EEG, pretrained on 47,703 sessions spanning ages 5-81 from the brain.space and Healthy Brain Network (HBN) corpora. The model combines a latent-prediction objective - predicting masked-token representations against an EMA-of-tokenizer target - with an auxiliary signal-reconstruction term, applied to 30-second multi-channel windows under spatiotemporal block masks. A lightweight attentive probe trained on frozen pretrained embeddings achieves a best held-out-validation mean absolute error of 3.06 years (r = 0.924) for age regression on 3,367 sessions, against a predict-the-mean baseline of approximately 10 years MAE. With light task-specific fine-tuning of the model's final layers, the same pretrained encoder achieves rank-1 placements - with the model's native 30-second windows - on the public NeuralBench x brain.space EEG leaderboard for sex classification (balanced accuracy 0.911), age prediction (r = 0.749), and psychopathology composite regression (r = 0.215). We further show that the model's age-prediction residual is negatively correlated with cognitive efficiency over several tasks we examined.

</details>


### [80] [When Certificates Fail: A Unified Safety Framework for Embedded Neural Interface Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06630)
*Jasmeet Singh Bindra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Formal robustness certificates for neural-interface models (e.g., EEGNet) can remain valid even when task accuracy drops significantly (e.g., 25.7% under PGD attack), revealing a gap between mathematical certification and operational safety. The paper identifies three alignment failures—verification insufficiency, proxy-fidelity divergence, and latent information exfiltration—and proposes an empirical audit framework, demonstrating that operational safety auditing, not certificate verification alone, is necessary for responsible neural-interface deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a critical alignment gap between formal robustness certificates and actual operational safety in neural interfaces, where certificates pass despite task accuracy collapsing under attack. This reveals that training objectives diverge from user welfare, motivating a need for broader empirical auditing beyond certificate verification.

Method: The authors propose a unified empirical audit framework targeting three failures: (1) verification insufficiency (certificates pass while task behavior degrades), (2) proxy-fidelity divergence (task-optimized representations damage neural signal structure), and (3) latent information exfiltration (public-task embeddings leak private attributes). The framework is instantiated on BCI Competition IV 2a and SEED-IV using multiple deep and classical EEG decoders (EEGNet, CSP+LDA, FBCSP+LDA), with official session-level validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests.

Result: Under perturbation budget ε=0.25, EEGNet classification accuracy drops by 25.7% under PGD attack while Lipschitz-style certificates remain valid across all 9 subjects. The verification gap persists across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA (architecture-independent). A time-domain auxiliary objective reduces reconstruction MSE by 0.1132 while worsening spectral log-MSE. Subject identity is recoverable at 48.1% versus 6.7% chance from public-task embeddings.

Conclusion: Operational safety auditing—not certificate verification alone—is necessary for responsible neural-interface deployment. The gap between mathematical certification and operational safety is a systemic alignment failure requiring empirical audit frameworks to detect verification insufficiency, proxy-fidelity divergence, and latent information exfiltration.

Abstract: Formal robustness certificates for embedded neural-interface models can pass while task accuracy collapses: at perturbation budget e=0.25, EEGNet classification accuracy drops by 25.7% under projected-gradient attack while the Lipschitz-style certificate remains valid for all 9 tested subjects. We argue that this gap between mathematical certification and operational safety is one instance of a broader alignment failure in neural interfaces, where training objectives diverge from user welfare. We propose a unified empirical audit framework organized around three such failures: verification insufficiency, in which certificates pass while task behavior degrades; proxy-fidelity divergence, in which task-optimized representations damage neural signal structure (a time-domain auxiliary objective reduces reconstruction MSE by 0.1132 while worsening spectral log-MSE); and latent information exfiltration, in which public-task embeddings retain private attributes (subject identity recoverable at 48.1% versus 6.7% chance). We instantiate the framework on BCI Competition IV 2a and SEED-IV using multiple deep and classical EEG decoders, official session-level validation, null controls, and paired statistical tests. The verification gap persists across EEGNet, CSP+LDA, and FBCSP+LDA, and is therefore architecture-independent. Our results establish that operational safety auditing, not certificate verification alone, is necessary for responsible neural-interface deployment.

</details>


### [81] [Does Demand Response Increase Vulnerability to Cyber Attacks by Adversarial Data Modifications?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06632)
*Clemens Kortmann,Eike Cramer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how adversarial attacks on electricity price forecasts impact industrial demand response decision-making. Using a generalized process model, the authors show that attacks can erode demand response profits, but limited perturbations preserve ~90% of financial advantage. The impact depends more on perturbation orientation than magnitude, suggesting attack design should incorporate scheduling optimization model sensitivities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While adversarial attacks on electricity grid problems (e.g., forecasting, state estimation) have been studied, their potential impact on the demand side—specifically industrial demand response—remains largely unexplored. The authors aim to fill this gap.

Method: The authors design adversarial attacks targeting electricity price forecasting models, then solve scheduling optimization problems for energy-intensive production processes using distorted price forecasts. They employ a generalized process model to investigate vulnerability across production scheduling problems with varying levels of process flexibility.

Result: Adversarial attacks can erode demand response profits. With limited perturbations (hard to detect), demand response retains ~90% of its financial advantage. The impact depends more on the orientation of adversarial perturbations than their magnitude.

Conclusion: Attack analyses should explicitly incorporate scheduling optimization model sensitivities into attack design to enable more rigorous assessments of decision-making under adversarial attacks.

Abstract: Adversarial attacks are crafted data manipulations that aim to deteriorate the outcomes of prediction or decision-making algorithms. In the energy systems literature, adversarial attacks have been studied with a focus on problems regarding the electricity grid. Such problems include forecasting and grid state estimation, where adversarial attacks are also known as false data injection attacks. Only few studies have analyzed the potential impact that adversarial attacks have on the demand side. We analyze how manipulated price forecasts impact the decision-making in industrial demand response. To this end, we design adversarial attacks that aim to deteriorate the output of electricity price forecasting models and solve scheduling optimization problems of energy-intensive production processes using the distorted price forecasts. We make use of a generalized process model to investigate the vulnerability to adversarial attacks for a range of production scheduling problems with different levels of process flexibility. We find that adversarial attacks can erode the profits gained from demand response. However, when perturbations are limited in extent (so that they are hard to detect by the human user), demand response preserves about 90\% of its financial advantage compared to steady-state process operation. Further, we find that the impact of adversarial attacks on demand response does not only depend on the magnitude of the perturbations but rather on the orientation of the adversarial perturbations. Therefore, we argue that attack analyses should explicitly incorporate the sensitivities of scheduling optimization models into the attack design to enable more rigorous assessments of decision-making under adversarial attacks.

</details>


### [82] [Gimitest: A Comprehensive Tool for Testing Reinforcement Learning Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07029)
*Dennis Gross,Quentin Mazouni,Helge Spieker,Arnaud Gotlieb*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gimitest is an open-source framework for testing reinforcement learning policies across various environments, supporting both single- and multi-agent scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL policies can be unsafe and vulnerable to attacks; existing automated testing methods are limited to selected environments, scenarios, and algorithms, creating a need for a more comprehensive testing solution.

Method: The authors propose a comprehensive framework for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies under varying conditions, implemented as Gimitest, an open-source tool supporting various gym frameworks with modifiable integrated components.

Result: Gimitest demonstrates effectiveness in testing multiple RL policies across environments like official Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo.

Conclusion: Gimitest provides a flexible, open-source solution for systematically testing RL policy reliability across diverse environments and settings.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) policies can be unsafe and vulnerable to attacks. Ensuring their reliability is often a pain point as existing automated testing methods target only selected environments, testing scenarios, and RL algorithms. To address this, we propose a comprehensive framework for testing single- and multi-agent RL policies under varying conditions. Our implementation of this framework, Gimitest, is an open-source tool that supports various gym frameworks and allows for modifications of their integrated components. This article describes the framework and details Gimitest's functionality and architecture. It showcases its effectiveness in testing multiple RL policies in environments such as the official Farama Gymnasium and PettingZoo.

</details>


### [83] [When Do Geometric Algebra Layers Beat Scalarization? A Controlled Study on SO(3)-Equivariant Vector Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06634)
*Fabien Polly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Clifford algebra Cl(3,0) networks excel over scalarization baselines only when learning compositional targets that deeply nest group operations; they offer no advantage for single-stage equivariant laws, and no model generalizes well under invariant magnitude shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the geometric algebra structure in Cl(3,0) networks contributes anything beyond exact SO(3)-equivariance, or if simpler scalarization approaches are sufficient.

Method: Compared a Cl(3,0) Clifford network against a minimal scalarization baseline (invariant dot products → MLP → equivariant basis coefficients) on single-stage and compositional 3D vector learning tasks, with additional baselines including Vector Neurons, e3nn, and multiplicative coefficient networks.

Result: Scalarization matches or beats Cl(3,0) on single-stage laws (rotation, cross product, central force). Cl(3,0) beats scalarization by 10x data efficiency on compositional targets (e.g., nested rotations, torque through orientation). Scalarization fails on 4-rotation chains. No model extrapolates invariant magnitudes.

Conclusion: Geometric algebra layers are not a general low-data 3D learning shortcut; they help specifically when the target composes group operations in depth, but fail to generalize under distribution shifts of invariant features.

Abstract: Compact networks built from Clifford algebra Cl(3,0) primitives are exactly SO(3)-equivariant and learn synthetic 3D vector laws from few samples. We ask whether the geometric algebra structure itself contributes anything beyond exact equivariance. We compare against a minimal scalarization baseline: invariant dot products fed to a small MLP that outputs coefficients on the equivariant basis {v_i, v_i x v_j}, which is also exactly equivariant. On single-stage laws (rotation by axis-angle, cross product, central force), scalarization matches or beats the Cl(3,0) network at a fraction of the training cost, so the geometric algebra adds nothing there. On compositional targets whose computation graph nests group operations (apply R2 R1 to a point; map a local force through an orientation, then take a torque), the Cl(3,0) network beats scalarization by an order of magnitude in the low-data regime, reaching with 100 samples what the baseline needs 3000 for, and the gap survives strengthening the baseline with the triple-product invariant and 17x more parameters, external Vector Neurons and e3nn baselines, and a multiplicative coefficient network. Ablations show the required network depth tracks the rotation chain length, and scalarization falls below the constant predictor on chains of four rotations. The advantage is not composition per se: on a rotation-free nested cross product, which flattens into polynomial invariant coefficients, scalarization wins by 24x. No tested model, equivariant or not, extrapolates invariant magnitudes: on radius and separation shifts every model is worse than a constant predictor once errors are normalized. We conclude that geometric algebra layers are not a general shortcut for low-data 3D learning, but become useful precisely when the target composes group elements in depth.

</details>


### [84] [ORCAID: Oblique Rule-Based Continuous-Action Interpretation for Deep RL Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07235)
*Ignacio D. Lopez-Miguel,Ezio Bartocci,Thomas Eiter,Martin Tappler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORCAID extracts interpretable rule-based policies from RL agents in continuous action spaces using oblique decision trees with a three-stage split search, producing concise rules that maintain performance and can even improve the original deep RL policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explainability remains a key challenge in reinforcement learning, particularly when distilling interpretable policies from agents trained in complex environments with continuous action spaces.

Method: ORCAID uses an oblique decision tree training algorithm that partitions the state space by hyperplanes and fits local linear models, employing a three-stage split search: random initialization, local refinement, and backward elimination, followed by adjacent leaf merging for concise rule extraction.

Result: Extracted rule-based policies maintain strong performance with low parameter counts and can even improve the performance of the original deep RL policy across multiple environments.

Conclusion: ORCAID effectively bridges the gap between high-performance deep RL and interpretability in mixed continuous-discrete environments with continuous action spaces.

Abstract: Explainability remains a key issue in reinforcement learning (RL). Distilling an interpretable policy from an agent trained in a complex environment is particularly challenging when the action space is continuous. We introduce ORCAID, a novel method for extracting interpretable rule-based policies from RL agents operating in mixed continuous-discrete environments with continuous action spaces. Our main contribution is an efficient oblique decision tree training algorithm that partitions the state space by hyperplanes and fits local linear models. The key idea lies in a three-stage split search: efficient random initialization, local refinement, and backward elimination. Finally, adjacent leaves are merged to yield a concise set of interpretable rules describing a given deep RL policy. We evaluate ORCAID across multiple RL environments, demonstrating that the extracted rule-based policies maintain strong performance with a low number of parameters and can even be used to improve the performance of the original deep RL policy.

</details>


### [85] [Optimized Instance Alteration for Explaining and Assessing Robustness of Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06637)
*Evgenii Kuriabov,David Miller,Jia Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified optimization framework (XA-L₀ penalty + classifier loss) is proposed for diagnosing misclassification and assessing robustness of black-box classifiers, validated on image and tabular datasets using the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-Confusion Matrix).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To simultaneously address two key challenges in black-box classifiers: (1) identifying why misclassifications occur (interpretability), and (2) quantifying how robust a classifier is (robustness).

Method: An optimization framework combining an explainability-aware L₀ penalty (XA-L₀) for sparse/interpretable modifications and a classifier loss to steer perturbations toward a target label. Robustness is quantified via the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-Confusion Matrix), modeling class-to-class transition probabilities under bounded perturbations.

Result: Validated on both image and tabular datasets, showing the method can jointly deliver interpretability and robustness assessment.

Conclusion: The proposed unified approach effectively diagnoses misclassification causes and evaluates classifier robustness through interpretable perturbations and a structured confusion matrix metric.

Abstract: In this work, we propose a unified approach for diagnosing misclassification and assessing the robustness of black-box classifiers. Central to our method is an optimization framework that modifies an instance so that the classifier predicts a specified target label, while ensuring that the modification remains easily explainable. The objective function contains two components: an explainability-aware $L_0$ (XA-$L_0$) penalty that promotes sparse and interpretable modifications, and a classifier loss objective that steers the perturbed instance toward the desired output. This integrated optimization formulation is used both to identify the underlying causes of misclassification and to evaluate robustness by determining how an instance can change within a tolerance region before being reassigned to another class. To quantify robustness, we introduce the Tolerance Region Confusion Matrix (TOR-Confusion Matrix), which measures a classifier's susceptibility by modeling the class-to-class transition probabilities induced by tolerance-bounded perturbations. We validate the proposed method on both image and tabular datasets, demonstrating its ability to jointly deliver interpretability and robustness assessment.

</details>


### [86] [UASPL: Uncertainty-Aware Self-Paced Learning with Evidential Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06638)
*Yifan Zhang,Yuxin Hu,Zhuobin Hao,Xiaozhuan Gao,Lipeng Pan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UASPL integrates evidential neural networks into self-paced learning to use uncertainty estimates for more reliable sample selection, outperforming existing SPL methods in classification, interpretability, and generality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional SPL assumes samples with smaller loss values are "easy" and thus selects them first, but these predictions are not always reliable. This work aims to incorporate uncertainty estimation to make sample selection more robust and interpretable.

Method: Proposes UASPL (Uncertainty-Aware Self-Paced Learning) based on evidential neural networks within the Subjective Logic framework, using a general loss function that integrates predictive reliability (uncertainty) into sample selection. This loss function can be extended to various SPL variants and includes a sample selection preference term for interpretability.

Result: Experimental results across multiple datasets demonstrate that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in classification performance, interpretability, and generality.

Conclusion: Integrating evidential uncertainty estimation into self-paced learning yields more reliable sample selection and better overall model performance, with the added benefit of interpretability and broad applicability across SPL variants.

Abstract: Self-paced learning (SPL) is an effective learning paradigm that simulates the human learning process by progressing from easy to difficult samples based on the value of the loss function during the learning process. It has shown great potential in improving model performance and training efficiency. However, the prediction results of samples with smaller loss values are not necessarily reliable, indicating that such samples are not always simple samples for the model. Hence, this article proposes an uncertainty-aware self-paced learning based on evidential neural networks, termed UASPL, which integrates predictive reliability into sample selection through a general loss function within the Subjective Logic framework. This loss function incorporates uncertainty estimation and can be extended to different variants of SPL. Moreover, this loss function couples a sample selection preference, thereby ensuring the interpretability of the sample selection process. Finally, the experimental results on multiple datasets show that UASPL outperforms other SPL methods in terms of classification performance, interpretability, and generality. The source code is available at: https://github.com/treelife979/UASPL.

</details>


### [87] [At-Grok Is Not Converged:A Measurement-Validity Audit for Grokking Representation Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06639)
*Truong Xuan Khanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates the phenomenon of "compression lag" in neural network training on modular arithmetic tasks. Embedding compression continues long after accuracy has plateaued, with the effective rank at grokking transition overstating converged values by 3-5x (MLP) or 1.3-1.5x (transformer). LayerNorm significantly reduces this compression lag. The authors provide an audit toolkit to separate generalization onset from compression, detect censoring, and validate metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why embedding compression (rank reduction) continues for tens of thousands of steps after a network has already generalized on modular arithmetic tasks, and to quantify the discrepancy between the compression observed at the grokking transition versus convergence.

Method: The authors measure effective rank evolution during training on modular arithmetic, compare MLP vs transformer architectures, perform ablation studies (particularly adding LayerNorm), pre-register control experiments to rule out scale invariance, and develop a comprehensive audit suite for validating compression metrics.

Result: Compression lags accuracy transition by at least 10,000 steps (order of time-to-grok). Effective rank at grokking transition overstates converged value by 3-5x (MLP) or 1.3-1.5x (transformer). Adding LayerNorm shifts compression fraction at grok step from 0.87 to 0.25. An MLP-specific depth law linking norm budget to converged floor fails on transformers and flips sign under free weight decay.

Conclusion: Compression and generalization onset are separable phenomena; the audit toolkit helps researchers avoid overconfidence in early compression metrics. LayerNorm is a key architectural factor that reduces compression lag, but scale invariance is not the mechanism.

Abstract: On modular arithmetic, a network's embedding keeps compressing for tens of thousands of steps after it has already generalized. Reading effective rank at the grokking transition overstates the converged value by 3-5x on an MLP, and by 1.3-1.5x on a transformer trained to convergence; on the MLP it also erases which cells compress at all. Compression lags the accuracy transition by an amount on the order of the time-to-grok, at least 10,000 steps, rather than coinciding with it. A one-variable ablation shows what sets the lag size: adding LayerNorm to an otherwise identical transformer moves the fraction of compression done by the grok step from 0.87 to 0.25, and a pre-registered control rules out scale invariance as the mechanism. We package this as an audit that separates onset from compression, flags censoring, excludes boundary cells that never fully generalize, and checks that the reference floor has plateaued, with an adversarial suite that caught a false-confidence bug in our own branch. A secondary, MLP-specific depth law linking norm budget to converged floor fails a generality test on a transformer and flips sign under free weight decay. Code and the toolkit are released.

</details>


### [88] [The Rank-One Corner: How Much Value Equivalence Does a Task Need from a World Model?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06640)
*Donna Vakalis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A world model's latent representation installs predictive structure according to the dimensionality of its training objective, not its capacity or observations. A scalar reward signal installs only a 1D projection of a multi-dimensional closure, but higher-dimensional objectives install proportionally more predictive directions. This dimensional law holds across auxiliary and value heads, is bounded by regimes where reconstruction already provides structure, and reframes value equivalence as a rank-one corner of a continuous spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the standard view that world model quality is monolithic (good vs. bad reconstruction), and instead argue that what matters is the "closure"—the specific predictive coordinates a task needs. The paper aims to show that the objective's dimensionality, not model capacity, determines how much of that closure is captured.

Method: Controlled experiments on a DreamerV3 stack with known ground-truth closure. Compares scalar reward signal vs. full objective across dimensionalities (1–4) using linear probes, auxiliary heads, and the model's own value head. Includes capacity-matched comparisons and in-situ pressure checks, plus a closed-loop task to measure the regime boundary.

Result: Scalar objective installs only a 1D projection (R² from 0.10→0.76 when replaced by full objective). Sweeping dimensionality from 1 to 4 installs exactly that many predictive directions through auxiliary heads; same rank pattern appears (attenuated) through the value head. The dimensional law holds until reconstruction already provides structure (closed-loop task), where scalar suffices.

Conclusion: Value equivalence is dimensional rather than binary: the objective's rank determines how much of the closure is installed. The familiar single-reward objective is its rank-one corner case. The objective decides what a latent represents precisely where cheaper training signals cannot already recover the structure.

Abstract: A learned world model is usually judged by how faithfully it reconstructs its observations or predicts reward, as though quality were something the model simply has or lacks. But what a task actually needs from a model is narrower: the few predictive coordinates its queries depend on, which we call the closure. We show that how much of that closure a latent comes to represent is set not by the model's capacity or its observations but by the dimensionality of the objective it is trained against, and we measure this directly on a DreamerV3 stack in a controlled environment with known ground-truth closure. An aligned scalar value signal -- the objective at the heart of value equivalence -- installs only a one-dimensional projection of a closure that needs several dimensions: read through a single linear probe, the recoverable structure rises from R^2=0.10 to 0.76 as the scalar is replaced by the full objective. Sweeping the objective's dimensionality from one to four installs exactly that many predictive directions through an auxiliary head, and the same staircase appears -- at attenuated magnitude but the same rank -- through the model's own value head, so the dissociation is dimensional rather than an artifact of head form. Capacity-matched comparisons and in-situ pressure checks rule out the obvious alternatives. The law governs a regime, and we measure its boundary: on a companion closed-loop task whose structure is observable frame by frame, reconstruction installs that structure and the scalar objective suffices -- the objective decides what a latent represents exactly where cheaper training signals cannot already recover it. Value equivalence is thus not all-or-nothing but dimensional: the familiar single-reward objective is its rank-one corner, and a model installs as much of a task's structure as the objective it is asked to predict.

</details>


### [89] [The Approximation Ratio for the Risk of Myopic Bayesian Active Learning for Linear Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06642)
*Stephen Mussmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves a tight approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm in active learning for linear regression, identifying the maximum initial leverage score (MILS) as a key determinant of performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active learning addresses the fundamental question of which data to observe, and the greedy algorithm (a common heuristic equivalent to myopic Bayesian active learning for linear regression) lacks theoretical guarantees on its risk.

Method: The authors prove the first approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm's risk that is tight up to an absolute constant, and identify the maximum initial leverage score (MILS) as a key quantity governing the algorithm's performance.

Result: The approximation ratio is linear in the maximum initial leverage score (MILS).

Conclusion: The greedy algorithm's risk has a provable approximation guarantee (tight up to a constant), with MILS being fundamental to its performance; results are validated via numerical simulations.

Abstract: Active learning studies the fundamental question: what data should we choose to observe? The greedy algorithm in optimal experiment design is a common heuristic and also equivalent to myopic Bayesian active learning for linear regression, the common framework where long-term planning is replaced with the one-step optimal choice. In this work, we prove a first-of-its-kind approximation ratio for the greedy algorithm's risk that is tight up to an absolute constant. The approximation ratio is linear in the maximum initial leverage score (MILS), a newly identified quantity fundamental to the greedy algorithm's performance. Finally, we illustrate the results with simple numerical simulations.

</details>


### [90] [Diffusion enabled Optimal Transport distances for graph matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06646)
*Iman Seyedi,Francesco Archetti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DsrFGW is a new graph comparison method that integrates diffusion processes into semi-relaxed fused Gromov-Wasserstein optimal transport, significantly improving robustness to noise, sparsity, and missing edges across synthetic graph matching tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Gromov-Wasserstein and semi-relaxed variants (srGW, srFGW) fail on sparse, noisy, or partially observed graphs. The paper aims to overcome these limitations by incorporating diffusion processes inspired by Graph Diffusion Distance.

Method: DsrFGW unifies node features and structural connectivity via optimal transport, integrating diffusion processes that allow information propagation across nodes to capture both local and global structural patterns while mitigating sensitivity to noise or missing edges.

Result: Evaluated on 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy, medium, hard). DsrFGW achieves 0-20 percentage point accuracy improvements over srFGW, with dramatic ARI gains: srFGW yields negative ARI (worse than random) on medium difficulty while DsrFGW performs better. Under severe noise, DsrFGW improves clustering quality in 92% of tasks.

Conclusion: DsrFGW establishes itself as a robust framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty, with optimal diffusion scales adapting to problem difficulty.

Abstract: This paper introduces Diffusion Semi-Relaxed Fused Gromov-Wasserstein (DsrFGW), a novel method for graph comparison that unifies node features and structural connectivity through optimal transport. While traditional Gromov-Wasserstein and semi-relaxed variants (srGW, srFGW) capture graph structure, they often struggle with sparse, noisy, or partially observed graphs. Inspired by Graph Diffusion Distance, which posits graphs are similar if they enable similar information transmission patterns, DsrFGW incorporates diffusion processes allowing information propagation across nodes, capturing local and global structural patterns while reducing sensitivity to noise or missing edges. An extensive evaluation on 36 synthetic pairwise graph matching tasks (easy, medium, hard) demonstrates consistent superiority over srFGW, achieving accuracy improvements of 0-20 percentage points and dramatic Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) gains: in medium-difficulty scenarios, srFGW often achieves negative ARI (worse than random) while DsrFGW offers better performance in terms of both internal and external clustering quality measures (i.e., Adjusted Rank Index and Accuracy with respect to the true underlying clusters, respectively). Even under severe noise, DsrFGW improves clustering quality in 92% of the synthetic tasks with optimal diffusion scales adapting to problem difficulty, establishing DsrFGW as a robust framework for graph comparison under structural uncertainty.

</details>


### [91] [Final Checkpoints Are Not Enough: Analyzing Latent Reasoning Faithfulness Along Training Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06648)
*Hengyu Jin,Shu Yang,Di Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper tracks how faithfulness of latent reasoning evolves across training, finding that causal contribution of latent steps decays over training, diverges by answer format (decays for binary choice, rises for open-ended), and that converged checkpoints appear similarly unfaithful despite qualitatively different training trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work identified unfaithful behaviors in latent reasoning at converged checkpoints but left how these behaviors form during training unexamined. The authors aim to track faithfulness evolution across training to understand its dynamics.

Method: The authors apply a verifiable counterfactual edit on the input and a noise-ablation activation patch on latent reasoning steps across saved checkpoints of different latent reasoning paradigms, tracking faithfulness at both output and activation levels.

Result: (i) Output-level unfaithfulness at convergence can appear similar across paradigms despite divergent training trajectories; (ii) activation-level causal contribution of latent steps decays across training for both paradigms; (iii) activation-level trajectory diverges by answer format—decaying on binary choice and rising on open-ended decoding.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning faithfulness depends on training stage and answer format, not just the final converged checkpoint, highlighting the need to analyze dynamics across training.

Abstract: Latent reasoning methods perform multi-step inference entirely in the model's continuous hidden states, promising more compact and efficient reasoning. However, these opaque hidden states raise a question of faithfulness: whether these latent reasoning steps causally drive the final answer. Prior work investigates this question at converged checkpoints and reports several unfaithful behaviors, such as latent reasoning steps that can be replaced without changing the answer, but leaves how these behaviors form during training unexamined. We instead track how faithfulness evolves across saved checkpoints for different latent reasoning paradigms, applying a verifiable counterfactual edit on the input and a noise-ablation activation patch on the latent reasoning steps. We find that (i) at the output level, latent reasoning methods can look similarly unfaithful at convergence under counterfactual edits while following qualitatively divergent trajectories; (ii) at the activation level, the causal contribution of latent reasoning steps to the final answer decays across training for both paradigms, with the examples that flip on the output side in (i) also being the examples on which this contribution decays; and (iii) the activation-level trajectory diverges by answer format, decaying on binary choice and rising on open-ended decoding. These findings highlight that latent reasoning faithfulness depends on training stage and answer format.

</details>


### [92] [Entropy-Guided Tensor Compression for Multimodal Federated Learning on Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06651)
*Quoc Bao Phan,Tuy Tan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MESH-FL is an entropy-guided MPS update-compression framework for modality-heterogeneous federated learning that adaptively allocates compression ranks across layers, modalities, and devices, achieving up to 56.8× compression with accuracy improvements over uncompressed FedAvg.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FL compression schemes apply uniform policies across layers and devices, failing to account for modality-specific differences in spectral structure and compressibility in multimodal FL over heterogeneous mobile/edge devices.

Method: MESH-FL estimates spectral entropy of layer-wise updates via truncated SVD, allocates MPS compression ranks adaptively under per-client payload budgets, and solves a convex surrogate rank-allocation problem with theoretical guarantees on monotonicity and convergence.

Result: On a 15-node heterogeneous Raspberry Pi 4/5 cluster, MESH-FL achieves up to 56.8× compression, surpasses uncompressed FedAvg by up to 2.01% in accuracy, and reduces total transmitted data to convergence by up to 66×.

Conclusion: Entropy-guided adaptive rank allocation effectively handles modality heterogeneity in FL, with theoretical convergence guarantees and significant practical compression gains on resource-constrained edge devices.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) over mobile and edge devices increasingly involves multimodal models in which clients differ in both sensing capability and computational capacity. Existing update compression schemes typically apply uniform policies across layers and devices, without accounting for modality-specific differences in spectral structure and compressibility. We propose MESH-FL, an entropy-guided matrix product state (MPS) update-compression framework for modality-heterogeneous FL on resource-constrained devices. MESH-FL estimates the spectral entropy of each layer-wise update via truncated singular value decomposition and allocates MPS compression ranks adaptively across layers, modalities, and devices under per-client payload budgets. We show that higher spectral entropy necessitates a higher reconstruction rank under the majorization order on singular-value energy distributions. Building on this result, we prove that the proposed entropy-guided allocation solves a convex surrogate rank-allocation problem, preserves monotonicity under the exact payload model, and achieves convergence with an explicit compression-dependent error term. Experiments on a 15-node heterogeneous Raspberry Pi~4/5 cluster with modality-heterogeneous clients show that MESH-FL achieves up to $56.8\times$ compression while surpassing the uncompressed FedAvg baseline in final accuracy by up to 2.01%, and reduces total transmitted data to reach convergence by up to $66\times$.

</details>


### [93] [From Jumps to Signatures: a Generative Method for Temporal Point Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06652)
*Niels Cariou-Kotlarek,Vasileios Lampos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces sigTPP, a signature-based generative model for Temporal Point Processes that uses interarrival embeddings to extend rough path signatures to discrete event sequences, enabling path-level training and distributional discrepancy measures for evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rough path signatures cannot directly handle cadlag paths of Temporal Point Processes (TPPs); neural TPP models lack global sequence-level loss, and evaluation of variable-length event sequences lacks proper distributional discrepancy measures.

Method: Proposes interarrival embedding (a stable, injective lift from jump paths to continuous bounded-variation paths) to extend signature methods to discrete events; introduces sigTPP, a signature-based generative model trained with path-level loss; derives three distributional discrepancies for evaluating generative TPP models.

Result: sigTPP achieves the best average rank across eight complementary metrics, outperforms or matches the strongest baseline in 64% of dataset-metric pairs, and improves against every baseline by at least 19% on average according to a relative score.

Conclusion: The proposed pathwise framework successfully extends signature methods to TPPs, enabling effective generative modeling with global loss and principled evaluation metrics.

Abstract: Rough path signatures are a universal feature map for continuous paths and, via the expected signature, characterise path distributions. These guarantees do not directly extend to cadlag paths of Temporal Point Processes (TPPs), limiting the use of signature methods for event sequences. Furthermore, neural TPP models, including recent generative approaches, optimise per-event objectives with no global sequence-level loss, while evaluation of variable-length event sequences lacks distributional discrepancy measures. This paper proposes a common pathwise framework for addressing these limitations. We introduce the interarrival embedding, a stable, injective lift from jump paths to continuous paths of bounded variation, extending signature methods to discrete event sequences. Our theoretical contributions give rise to sigTPP, the first signature-based generative model for TPPs, trained using a path-level loss on complete trajectories. We further analyse the space of counting paths and derive three distributional discrepancies, providing mathematically justified tools for evaluating generative TPP models. Across synthetic and real-world datasets, sigTPP achieves the best average rank based on eight complementary metrics, outperforms or is within a standard error of the strongest baseline in 64% of the dataset-metric pairs, and according to a relative score, improves against every baseline by at least 19% on average.

</details>


### [94] [Dual Attention Heads for Personalized Federated Learning in ECG Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06653)
*Kien Le,Joseph Lindley,Quoc Bao Phan,Tuy Tan Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedDualAtt is a personalized federated learning method for ECG classification that splits Transformer attention heads into global (shared via FedAvg) and local (client-specific) branches, outperforming existing methods on the FedCVD benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FL enables collaborative ECG model training across institutions without sharing sensitive patient data, but data heterogeneity across healthcare providers poses significant technical challenges for robust classification.

Method: Splits Transformer attention heads into global heads (aggregated via FedAvg for shared cross-site patterns) and local heads (kept client-specific for institution-level adaptation).

Result: Outperforms existing FL and personalized FL methods on the FedCVD benchmark for cardiovascular disease detection; different clients benefit from varying levels of architectural personalization.

Conclusion: FedDualAtt effectively addresses ECG data heterogeneity in FL by balancing global shared knowledge with local personalization through dual attention heads.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across institutions without sharing sensitive patient data. However, the inherent heterogeneity of electrocardiogram (ECG) data across healthcare providers presents significant technical challenges for robust classification. We propose FedDualAtt, a personalized federated learning approach that splits transformer attention heads into global and local branches. Global heads are aggregated via FedAvg to capture shared cross-site patterns, while local heads remain client-specific to adapt to institution-level recording characteristics. Experiments on FedCVD, an FL benchmark for cardiovascular disease detection, demonstrate that FedDualAtt outperforms existing FL and personalized FL methods in ECG classification tasks. Analysis of global-local head ratios reveals that different clients benefit from varying levels of architectural personalization.

</details>


### [95] [Robust Human-AI Complementarity under Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06656)
*Yewon Byun,Bryan Wilder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how asymmetric information about prediction quality between humans and AI affects complementary decision-making. It finds that negative error correlation between human and AI predictions enables robust strategies guaranteeing improved expected utility, and validates this condition using real-world forecasting benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models are intended to augment human decision makers, but humans often fail to realize complementary gains from AI predictions in practice. The paper aims to understand the fundamental factors that enable or hinder human-AI complementarity.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze how asymmetric information about prediction quality impacts complementarity, identifying error correlation structure as a key factor. They then empirically validate their theoretical findings using real-world forecasting benchmarks.

Result: Negative correlation between human and AI prediction errors allows decision makers to construct robust strategies guaranteeing improvements in expected utility. This condition for complementarity was empirically observed in real-world forecasting data.

Conclusion: The error correlation structure between human and AI predictions is a critical determinant of successful human-AI complementarity. When AI errors are negatively correlated with human errors, complementary gains are achievable and robust.

Abstract: Machine learning models are often intended to augment rather than replace human decision makers, by providing information that is complementary to human judgement.
  Yet, in practice, human decision makers routinely fail to realize such complementary gains, even when models provide useful signal.
  In this work, we study how asymmetric information about the quality of information available to a human decision maker vs. an AI impacts the ability of a decision maker to extract complementary value from AI predictions.
  We show that a key factor is the error correlation structure between human and AI predictions. In particular, when the AI's prediction errors are \textit{negatively correlated} with those of the human, the decision maker can construct robust strategies which guarantee improvements in expected utility. We empirically investigate whether these conditions for complementarity arise in practice, using real-world forecasting benchmarks.

</details>


### [96] [Trees from Marginals: Autoregressive drafting with factorized priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06763)
*Yuma Oda,Ryan Mathieu,Roman Knyazhitskiy,Artur Chakhvadze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weaver is a lightweight autoregressive adapter that constructs proposal trees from top-K marginals of factorized draft models, restoring conditional dependencies to improve acceptance rates in speculative decoding. Combined with optimized tree-verification for Gated Delta Net layers, it achieves 4.37× speedup over autoregressive decoding and outperforms DFlash by 24.7%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding improves interactivity of autoregressive LMs but factorized draft models suffer from degraded acceptance rates as speculative budget grows due to their independence assumption. The authors aim to overcome this limitation.

Method: Weaver uses a lightweight autoregressive adapter that constructs proposal trees from top-K marginals predicted by a factorized drafter, restoring conditional dependencies between proposed tokens without full-vocabulary projection. A rollback-free tree-verification algorithm with optimized CUDA kernels for Gated Delta Net layers is also introduced.

Result: 4.37-fold speedup over autoregressive decoding; outperforms optimized DFlash baseline by 24.7%.

Conclusion: Combining model-level improvements (restoring conditional dependencies) with systems-level optimizations (efficient tree verification) significantly boosts speculative decoding performance.

Abstract: Speculative decoding greatly increases the interactivity of autoregressive language models by trading off computation for extra tokens generated in a single forward pass. Factorized draft models are especially efficient because they predict future-token marginals in parallel, but their independence assumption causes acceptance rates to degrade sharply as the speculative budget grows. We analyze this limitation and introduce Weaver, a lightweight autoregressive adapter that constructs proposal trees from the top-K marginals of a factorized drafter. Weaver restores conditional dependencies between proposed tokens while avoiding a full-vocabulary projection. To support fast verification for models with Gated Delta Net layers, we derive a rollback-free tree-verification algorithm and implement optimized CUDA kernels in SGLang. By combining these model and systems contributions we achieve a 4.37-fold speedup over autoregressive decoding, and outperform a highly optimized DFlash baseline by 24.7%.

</details>


### [97] [Efficient Long-Horizon Learning for Learned Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06772)
*Xiaolong Huang,Benjamin Thérien,James Harrison,Eugene Belilovsky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ELO is an efficient meta-training algorithm for learned optimizers that reallocates compute to longer horizons and uses decoupled progressive supervision, enabling LOs to outperform AdamW across language modeling and image classification tasks with under 7 H100 GPU-hours.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current meta-training for learned optimizers suffers from two key limitations: (1) inability to efficiently scale meta-training to long-horizon inner problems, and (2) frequent failure to surpass hand-designed optimizers like Adam.

Method: ELO proposes (1) reallocating redundant meta-training compute to longer failure regimes for efficient long-horizon learning, and (2) enforcing decoupled progressive expert supervision to provide stable meta-learning signals that improve generalization.

Result: ELO substantially improves long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization for both element-wise and matrix-based LOs. ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms AdamW on GPT-2 (124M/350M), ViT-B/16, and ResNet-50, and is competitive with Muon on language modeling. All meta-training requires under 7 H100 GPU-hours.

Conclusion: ELO provides an efficient and effective solution to key bottlenecks in learned optimizer meta-training, enabling learned optimizers to reliably surpass hand-designed counterparts with minimal computational cost.

Abstract: Learned optimization aims to improve upon hand-designed optimizers (e.g., Adam and Muon) by meta-learning small neural network optimizers over a distribution of tasks. While recent work has greatly advanced the architectural design and inductive biases of learned optimizers (LOs), current meta-training approaches still suffer from two main difficulties: (1) they cannot efficiently scale meta-training to long-horizon inner problems and (2) they often fail to surpass comparable hand-designed optimizers. To address these limitations, we propose Efficient Long-hOrizon (ELO) learning, an efficient meta-training algorithm that (1) reallocates redundant meta-training compute to longer failure regimes, achieving efficient long-horizon learning, and (2) enforces decoupled progressive expert supervision, providing stable meta-learning signals that additionally improve the generalization of LOs. Our empirical study evaluates ELO for meta-training both element-wise and matrix-based LOs. Across downstream language modeling (GPT-2-124M/350M on FineWeb) and image classification (ViT-B/16, ResNet-50 on ImageNet-1K) tasks, ELO substantially improves the long-unroll performance and out-of-distribution generalization of the base LOs. In particular, ELO-Celo2 consistently outperforms well-tuned AdamW across all evaluated tasks, while remaining competitive with Muon on language modeling. \textit{Notably, all ELO baselines require less than 7 H100 GPU-hours for meta-training.}

</details>


### [98] [Efficient Bayesian Deep Ensembles via Analytic Predictive Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06776)
*Sina Aghaee Dabaghan Fard,Marie Maros,Jaesung Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An efficient Bayesian deep ensemble method for predictive regression that combines low-dimensional ensemble representation, closed-form Bayesian aggregation, and independent ensemble training to achieve interpretability, calibrated uncertainty, and computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance interpretability of deep ensembles while maintaining competitive predictive performance and computational efficiency, and to provide calibrated uncertainty estimates suitable for standalone prediction and broader learning systems.

Method: (i) Low-dimensional ensemble representation using a small number of trained neural predictors for scalable inference; (ii) Closed-form Bayesian aggregation via Bayesian linear regression for interpretable posterior weights and calibrated uncertainty without approximate inference; (iii) Independent ensemble training of multiple neural networks to improve robustness and uncertainty calibration.

Result: Competitive predictive performance with reliable uncertainty estimates across standard regression benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed approach achieves competitive predictive performance while maintaining reliable uncertainty estimates, combining Bayesian rigor with deep ensemble scalability and interpretability.

Abstract: We introduce an efficient Bayesian deep ensemble method for predictive regression designed to enhance interpretability while maintaining competitive predictive performance and computational efficiency. Our method combines the statistical rigor of Bayesian inference with the scalability of deep ensembles, providing calibrated uncertainty estimates that enable its use not only for standalone prediction but also as a component within broader learning systems. To achieve these goals, our work relies on three key design components: (i) low-dimensional ensemble representation: predictions are expressed as a combination of a small number of trained neural predictors, enabling scalable inference whose cost depends on ensemble size rather than dataset size; (ii) closed-form Bayesian aggregation: ensemble predictions are combined using Bayesian linear regression, yielding interpretable posterior weights and calibrated uncertainty without approximate inference; and (iii) Independent ensemble training: multiple neural networks are trained separately, producing diverse predictive representations that improve robustness and uncertainty calibration. Empirical results on standard regression benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves competitive predictive performance while maintaining reliable uncertainty estimates across settings.

</details>


### [99] [On Explicit Super-Expressive Approximation for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06781)
*Feng-Lei Fan,Ze-Yu Li,Chen-Yu Wang,Jian-Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides explicit quantitative bounds on parameter magnitudes for fixed-architecture neural networks using the Chinese Remainder Theorem, achieving dual results to the parameter-bounded, architecture-unbounded paradigm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on super-expressive fixed-size neural networks lacked quantitative, non-asymptotic characterizations of parameter magnitude with respect to approximation error.

Method: Introduces the Chinese Remainder Theorem as a constructive encoding mechanism; constructs depth-5 width-max{D,4} networks for Lipschitz functions, and depth-(r+9) width-max{2D, D+5N+1} networks for Hölder-smooth functions.

Result: For Lipschitz functions: explicit parameter-error trade-offs. For Hölder-smooth C^{r,γ}_A([0,1]^D) functions: log₂ 𝒫 = O(ε^{-2D/(r+γ)} log(1/ε)), which is the dual result to the parameter-bounded, architecture-unbounded regime.

Conclusion: Fixed-architecture networks can achieve approximation with explicit and non-asymptotic parameter bounds, establishing a duality with the traditional parameter-bounded paradigm.

Abstract: In this work, we investigate the fixed-architecture neural network approximation with explicit parameter bounds and elementary activations. While prior work demonstrated super-expressive approximation using fixed-size networks, they lack quantitative and non-asymptotic characterizations of parameter magnitude with respect to the approximation error. We resolve this issue by introducing the Chinese Remainder Theorem as a constructive encoding mechanism. For Lipschitz continuous functions on $[0,1]^D$, we construct a width-$\max\{D,4\}$, depth-$5$ network with explicit parameter-error trade-offs. For Hölder-smooth functions in $C^{r,γ}_A\left([0,1]^D\right)$, our fixed network of width $\max\{2D,\ D+5N+1\}$ and depth $r + 9$ achieves the parameter magnitude $\mathcal{P}$ bounded by $\log_2 \mathcal{P}=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2D/(r+γ)}\log(1/\varepsilon)\bigr)$. This is the dual result compared to those in the parameter-bounded and architecture-unbounded paradigm.

</details>


### [100] [Enhancing deep learning models for time series classification via knowledge distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06796)
*Javidan Abdullayev,Maxime Devanne,Jonathan Weber,Germain Forestier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates Knowledge Distillation for Time Series Classification across three architectures (FCN, Inception, ConvTran) on the UCR Archive, showing KD benefits intermediate-complexity students most, with parameter reductions of 38x (FCN), 42% (Inception), and significant gains for ConvTran with 2 attention heads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High computational and memory demands of state-of-the-art deep learning architectures hinder deployment in resource-limited environments; KD offers a solution by transferring knowledge from large teacher models to smaller student models.

Method: Investigated KD effectiveness for TSC using three architectures (FCN, Inception, ConvTran) on the UCR Archive, modifying architectural components such as convolutional filters, Inception modules, and attention heads.

Result: KD most benefits intermediate-complexity students: FCN student reduces parameters by 38x, Inception student matches teacher performance with 42% fewer parameters, ConvTran student with 2 attention heads shows most significant improvement.

Conclusion: KD is effective for TSC across diverse architectures, especially for students of intermediate complexity, enabling significant parameter reduction while maintaining competitive performance.

Abstract: Deep learning has achieved remarkable success in various domains including time series analysis, computer vision and natural language processing. However, high computational and memory demands of state-of-the-art architectures pose challenges for deployment in resource-limited environments. Knowledge Distillation (KD) addresses this by transferring knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller, more efficient student model while maintaining competitive performance. In this work, we investigate the effectiveness of KD for Time Series Classification (TSC) across three architectures: the classical Fully Convolutional Network (FCN), the convolutional Inception model and the transformer-based ConvTran model. We evaluate our approach on UCR Archive, the largest benchmark repository of time series datasets, by modifying architectural components such as convolutional filters, Inception modules and attention heads across the three architectures.
