Our results consistently show that KD most effectively benefits student models of intermediate complexity across all three architectures, with the distilled FCN student reducing parameters by a factor of 38, the distilled Inception student achieving nearly the same performance as the teacher with 42% fewer parameters and the distilled ConvTran student with 2 attention heads showing the most significant improvement through distillation. To encourage further research and reproducibility, we provide our implementation at https://github.com/MSD-IRIMAS/KD-4-TSC.

</details>


### [101] [What Predicts Correctness in Text-to-SQL? A Selective-Prediction Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06799)
*Robert Richardson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Uncertainty estimation for AI-generated SQL queries is best achieved through verification-based signals (LLM judges, ensembles) rather than black-box signals (self-consistency, log-probability), with fine-tuned verifiers performing well in-domain but failing to generalize across schemas, where large frozen reasoning models remain necessary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study which signals best predict correctness of AI-generated SQL queries (i.e., whether they produce the same result as a human-written reference), focusing on hard multi-table text-to-SQL tasks where uncertainty estimation is critical for safe deployment.

Method: Evaluated multiple uncertainty signals (string/structural/execution self-consistency, schema-relevance score, query executability, log-probability, LLM judge scores, ensemble judges) using AUROC on BIRD and Spider benchmarks; also fine-tuned verifiers (encoder and generative) and tested in-distribution and cross-schema generalization.

Result: Black-box signals and log-probability achieve ~0.61-0.68 AUROC; LLM judges reach 0.72-0.78; two-provider ensemble achieves 0.82 AUROC with low calibration error (ECE 0.03). Fine-tuned verifiers reach ~0.77-0.79 in-distribution but drop to ~0.66 on unseen schemas; scaling, schema diversity, rationale distillation, and cross-benchmark training fail to close the generalization gap.

Conclusion: Correctness uncertainty for text-to-SQL relies on reasoning-based signals; fine-tuned verifiers are effective in-domain but do not generalize across schemas, where large frozen reasoning models are required.

Abstract: Evaluating uncertainty in AI-generated SQL queries requires estimating whether a query is correct, where correct means it executes to the same result as a human-written reference. We study which signals predict correctness on hard multi-table text-to-SQL, using AUROC to measure how well each ranks correct queries above incorrect ones. On BIRD and Spider, black-box signals such as string, structural, and execution self-consistency, a schema-relevance score, and query executability all fall between about 0.61 and 0.68 AUROC, with string self-consistency strongest at 0.675; white-box log-probability is similar (0.67). The signals that move past this ceiling are verification-based: an LLM judge scores from 0.72 (GPT-4o-mini) to 0.78 (Claude). Judges from different providers make different errors, so a two-provider ensemble reaches 0.82 AUROC with a well-calibrated probability (expected calibration error 0.03) and supports useful abstention frontiers (for example, answering 27% of questions at 24% selective risk) where self-consistency offers no valid low-risk subset. The pattern holds across two benchmarks, two generators, and two judge providers. We also ask whether a verifier can be trained. Fine-tuned verifiers, both encoder and generative, reach about 0.77 to 0.79 AUROC in-distribution but fall to about 0.66 on unseen schemas; scaling to 7B, adding schema diversity, distilling a strong judge's rationales, and cross-benchmark training all fail to close that gap. Cross-schema transfer appears to track model scale and reasoning rather than fine-tuning. In practice, correctness uncertainty for text-to-SQL lives in reasoning-based signals: a fine-tuned verifier is a good in-domain tool, but a verifier that generalizes across schemas currently means a large frozen reasoning model.

</details>


### [102] [Generative Diffusion Models of Stochastic Graph Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06833)
*Yiğit Berkay Uslu,Samar Hadou,Sergio Rozada,Shirin Saeedi Bidokhti,Alejandro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified conditional graph signal generative modeling framework using a denoising diffusion model with a novel U-GNN architecture (generalizing U-Net to graphs via learned node selection pooling/unpooling and stride-based graph convolutions), demonstrated on stock price forecasting and wireless resource allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for sampling stochastic signals on graphs rely on application-tailored designs that often regress to a conditional mean rather than sampling from the full conditional distribution, lacking a unified framework.

Method: A denoising diffusion framework with a reverse diffusion process parameterized by GNNs, using a novel U-Graph Neural Network (U-GNN) architecture that generalizes U-Net to graphs via learned nested selection matrices for pooling/unpooling and stride-based graph convolutions on the original graph, bypassing explicit coarsening.

Result: Demonstrated on two tasks: stock price forecasting and optimal wireless resource allocation, with extensive numerical results validating the approach.

Conclusion: The proposed conditional diffusion framework with U-GNN effectively unifies and solves conditional graph signal generative modeling problems, outperforming regression-to-mean approaches.

Abstract: Sampling stochastic signals supported on a graph underlies many graph machine learning tasks, including recommender systems, forecasting in financial markets, and wireless network optimization. In these settings, the target signals are realizations of unknown conditional distributions. However, prevailing approaches rely mostly on intricate, application-tailored designs that often regress to a conditional mean instead of sampling from the conditional law. This paper unifies such problems as conditional graph signal generative modeling and tackles them with a single denoising diffusion framework. We learn a reverse diffusion process, parametrized by graph neural networks (GNNs), that draws graph signals conditioned directly on the graph topology and on node-feature side information. The reverse process is realized by a novel architecture, the U-Graph Neural Network (U-GNN), which generalizes the image-convolutional U-Net to graph-structured signals. The U-GNN performs multi-resolution encoder--decoder processing in which pooling and unpooling reduce to a learned node selection, expressed by nested selection matrices, and a zero-padded lifting of coarse signals back to the full node set. The graph convolutions are carried out on the original graph, with a stride that sets their hop reach, so the U-GNN bypasses explicit graph coarsening at every resolution. We demonstrate our method on two generative tasks: stock price forecasting and optimal wireless resource allocation, with extensive numerical results in both domains.

</details>


### [103] [LEMUR 2: Unlocking Neural Network Diversity for AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06839)
*Tolgay Atinc Uzun,Waleed Khalid,Saif U Din,Sai Revanth Mulukuledu,Akashdeep Singh,Chandini Vysyaraju,Raghuvir Duvvuri,Avi Goyal,Yashkumar Rajeshbhai Lukhi,Muhammad A. Hussain,Krunal Jesani,Usha Shrestha,Yash Mittal,Roman Kochnev,Pritam Kadam,Mohsin Ikram,Harsh R. Moradiya,Alice Arslanian,Dmitry Ignatov,Radu Timofte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEMUR 2 is a large-scale, extensible benchmark framework containing over 14,000 architectures and 750,000+ training records, spanning multimodal tasks and deployment platforms, designed to enable cross-domain, deployment-aware NAS and LLM-driven AutoML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NAS benchmarks (e.g., NAS-Bench, NATS-Bench) are limited to narrow, task-specific regions of architectural design space and lack cross-domain or deployment-aware evaluation, motivating the need for a unified, diverse, and scalable benchmark.

Method: Constructed via AST-based code mutation, genetic/RL evolution, fractal architecture generation, and LLM-guided synthesis (including NN-RAG). Uses NN-VR and NN-Lite pipelines for automated deployment and latency benchmarking on mobile and VR platforms. Contains 14K+ architectures, 750K+ structured training records.

Result: Provides a comprehensive dataset linking diverse architectures, multimodal tasks (image captioning, text-to-image, language modeling), and real-device deployment metadata across heterogeneous platforms.

Conclusion: LEMUR 2 establishes a new data foundation for reproducible, data-driven AI design, supporting LLM fine-tuning, LLM-driven AutoML, and cross-modality/hardware architectural generalization.

Abstract: Existing NAS benchmarks (e.g., NAS-Bench, NATS-Bench) cover only narrow, task-specific regions of the architectural design space and lack cross-domain or deployment-aware evaluation. LEMUR 2 introduces a large-scale, extensible framework unifying generative, evaluative, and deployment pipelines to unlock neural-network diversity. It comprises over 14,000 distinct architectures and more than 750,000 structured training records documenting model performance, hyperparameters, and task outcomes. These models were produced through AST-based code mutation, genetic and reinforcement-learning evolution, generation of fractal architectures, and synthesis guided by a Large Language Model (LLM). This includes deep models generated with the retrieval-augmented system NN-RAG, which derived and used architectural motifs from over 900 PyTorch modules extracted from public repositories. LEMUR 2 further employs NN-VR and NN-Lite pipelines for automated deployment and latency benchmarking on heterogeneous mobile and Unity-based VR platforms, providing real-device performance metadata. It spans multimodal tasks, image captioning, text-to-image synthesis, and language modeling, supporting cross-domain analysis of architectural transferability. By linking diverse architectures, tasks, and deployment data, LEMUR 2 provides the data foundation for LLM fine-tuning and coupling diverse architectural origins with large-scale, cross-platform empirical validation. This dataset defines a new basis for reproducible and data-driven AI design, advancing the emerging paradigm of LLM-driven AutoML and architectural generalization across modalities and hardware.

</details>


### [104] [A Gold-Standard Study of What Makes a Lightweight Game-Playing Agent Strong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06854)
*Nima Kelidari,Mohammadsaeed Haghi,Mahdi Salmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, game-agnostic reinforcement learning recipe is developed for Gin Rummy using a fixed rule-based expert as a yardstick. Key techniques (trust region updates, curriculum learning, warm starting, checkpointing) improve a self-play champion from 30% to 36% against the expert, while many common ideas (reward shaping, learned embeddings, imitation learning, LLM opponents) fail. The approach transfers to Leduc Hold'em and is released as a reusable package.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning agents for imperfect-information card games are hard to evaluate because they dominate random opponents and tie with copies of themselves; a fixed, strong yardstick is needed to meaningfully measure progress.

Method: A fixed rule-based expert is built as an evaluation-only yardstick. Ablation studies across 100+ runs isolate effective techniques: trust region updates, well-aimed reward, curriculum of tougher opponents, warm starting, and best checkpoint retention. Various neural architectures (MLP, CNN, set-based, attention, RNN) and common ideas (reward shaping, imitation learning, LLM opponents) are tested.

Result: The best agent reaches 36% win rate against the expert (up from 30% for self-play champion). Extra network capacity does not significantly improve performance, suggesting an information ceiling. Many techniques (reward shaping, learned embeddings, DAgger, LLM opponents) were ineffective or impractical.

Conclusion: A lightweight, game-agnostic recipe using self-play with specific augmentations and a fixed expert yardstick produces competitive agents without training on the expert, generalizing to other imperfect-information games like Leduc Hold'em.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents for imperfect-information card games are only as strong as the opponents they train against, and they are hard to grade, since they beat a random opponent over 99 percent of the time and only tie copies of themselves. So we build a strong, fixed, rule-based expert for Gin Rummy and use it only as a yardstick, never for training. It beats every agent we trained 70 to 99 percent of the time. Across more than a hundred runs, we isolate what makes a lightweight agent stronger. Trust region updates, a well-aimed reward, a curriculum of tougher opponents, warm starting, and keeping the best checkpoint all help, and stacking them lifts a self-play champion from about 30 to 36 percent against the expert. Several ideas did not pay off. Short-term and longer-term reward shaping, learned state embeddings, imitation and DAgger, and a live large language model opponent were each unhelpful, too slow, or too heavy to train at scale. Comparing MLP, convolutional, set-based, attention, and recurrent encoders shows that extra capacity does little to break the ceiling, suggesting the limit is information rather than network size. We add standard baselines (neural fictitious self-play and information set Monte Carlo search) and confirm the approach carries over to Leduc Hold'em, where the optimum is computable. The result is a lightweight, game-agnostic recipe that trains competitive agents without training on the expert, for any game a small model can handle, reported with robust statistics and released as a reusable package.

</details>


### [105] [Online Data Selection Is Implicit Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07023)
*Aoxiong Zeng,Yuxin Yang,Xiangquan Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that online data selection during supervised fine-tuning acts as an implicit alignment mechanism, where scoring and selecting training examples induces behavioral drift (e.g., changes in refusal rate, verbosity, sycophancy) even without explicit preference optimization. The authors formalize this as reweighted SFT, propose Alignment Drift Auditing (ADA) for measuring selection-induced drift, and introduce Alignment-Aware Selection (AAS) to constrain drift while maintaining data efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The common view separates SFT as capability adaptation from alignment via preference optimization. The authors challenge this by showing that data selection during SFT already induces behavioral preferences, meaning alignment effects arise earlier than typically assumed. Understanding this implicit alignment mechanism is critical for controllable model development.

Method: The authors compare random, loss-based, quality-based, and diversity-based online selectors on the same base model, optimizer, and token budget. They formalize online selection as a reweighted SFT objective where the scorer acts as a reward model. They introduce ADA (Alignment Drift Auditing) to quantify behavioral drift and AAS (Alignment-Aware Selection) to constrain drift along safety and style axes.

Result: Selectors that are statistically indistinguishable in task accuracy diverge sharply in refusal rate, verbosity, and sycophancy. The direction of behavioral shift is predictable from the attribute mixture of selected data. High-scoring data systematically favors longer, more assertive, more compliant, or more refusal-prone behaviors depending on the score definition.

Conclusion: Online data selection during SFT is an implicit alignment mechanism that should not be overlooked. The paper provides tools (ADA and AAS) to quantify and control this alignment drift, offering more nuanced control over model behavior without resorting to separate preference optimization stages.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is often treated as a capability-adaptation step, while alignment is attributed to later preference optimization or reinforcement learning. This separation is incomplete: when examples are scored and kept online during fine-tuning, the choice of which data to train on already changes the model's behavioral preferences. We study online data selection as an implicit alignment mechanism. Given the same base model, optimizer, and selected-token budget, we compare random, loss-based, quality-based, and diversity-based online selectors and measure the behavioral drift they induce without any preference optimization. The proposed evaluation tracks helpfulness, refusal rate, verbosity, truthfulness, sycophancy, calibration, and jailbreak robustness, together with diagnostics for which behavioral modes are over-represented in the selected data. We formalize online selection as a reweighted SFT objective whose weights define an implicit preference over response styles and safety postures, so that an online scorer plays the role usually assigned to a reward model. This view predicts that high-scoring data can systematically favor longer, more assertive, more compliant, or more refusal-prone behaviors depending on how the online score is defined. Empirically, selectors that are statistically indistinguishable in task accuracy diverge sharply in refusal rate, verbosity, and sycophancy, and we show that the direction of the shift is predictable from the attribute mixture of the selected data. We introduce Alignment Drift Auditing (ADA), a controlled protocol for quantifying selection-induced behavioral movement, and Alignment-Aware Selection (AAS), a diagnostic online selector that retains data efficiency while constraining drift along safety and style axes.

</details>


### [106] [Constrained Decoding for Diffusion Language Models via Efficient Inference over Finite Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07026)
*Meihua Dang,Stefano Ermon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an exact and tractable algorithm for constrained decoding in diffusion language models. By viewing finite automata as graphical models, the method enables efficient sampling from constrained mean-field posteriors, supporting greedy and stochastic decoding with logarithmic depth reduction. Experiments on Dream-7B and LLaDA-8B show significant accuracy improvements (e.g., +7.6% on BFCL-Live greedy, +46.7% on stochastic) with under 5% overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing constrained decoding systems are designed for autoregressive LLMs and assume left-to-right generation. Diffusion language models break this assumption by sampling multiple positions simultaneously from a fully-factorized mean-field distribution at each denoising step, requiring a new constrained decoding approach.

Method: The algorithm views finite automata as graphical models to obtain tractable representations of constrained distributions, enabling efficient inference. It applies depth-reduction techniques from arithmetic circuit theory to reduce sampling depth from linear to logarithmic in sequence length. The approach guarantees constraint satisfaction by construction and supports both greedy and sampling-based decoding under arbitrary remasking schedules.

Result: On BFCL-Live, Dream-7B's greedy decoding accuracy improved from 63.9% to 71.5%, and stochastic sampling accuracy from 22.3% to 69.0%, with under 5% wall-clock overhead. Accuracy gains were demonstrated across function calling (xLAM, BFCL), planning (Sudoku, Countdown), text-to-SQL (Spider), and math reasoning (GSM-Symbolic).

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm enables effective constrained decoding for diffusion language models, achieving substantial accuracy improvements across diverse tasks with minimal inference overhead, and resolving the fundamental incompatibility between existing constrained decoding methods and the parallel generation paradigm of diffusion models.

Abstract: Constrained decoding is essential for serving LLMs, ensuring that generated outputs follow specific structures such as JSON schema-formatted function calls. Existing systems are designed for autoregressive models and assume left-to-right generation, masking out invalid next tokens at each step. Diffusion language models, however, break this assumption: they sample multiple positions simultaneously from a fully-factorized mean-field distribution at each denoising step. In this paper, we present an exact and tractable algorithm for sampling from the constrained mean-field posterior under any constraint expressible as a finite automaton. Viewing finite automata as graphical models, we obtain tractable representations of the constrained distribution that enable efficient inference. The approach guarantees constraint satisfaction by construction, supports both greedy and sampling-based decoding, and is compatible with parallel and block-wise decoding under arbitrary remasking schedules. Applying depth-reduction techniques from arithmetic circuit theory, we further reduce sampling depth from linear to logarithmic in the sequence length. Empirical evaluations on Dream-7B and LLaDA-8B show substantial accuracy gains across various tasks including function calling (xLAM, BFCL), planning (Sudoku, Countdown), text-to-SQL (Spider), and math reasoning (GSM-Symbolic), with little inference overhead relative to unconstrained decoding. For example, on BFCL-Live, our approach improves Dream-7B's greedy decoding accuracy from 63.9% to 71.5%, and stochastic sampling accuracy from 22.3% to 69.0%, where the unconstrained baseline collapses, with under 5% wall-clock overhead.

</details>


### [107] [Latent graph encoding of multimodal neuroimaging features with generative AI architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07027)
*Ishaan Batta,Meenu Ajith,Vince Calhoun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper designs and systematically evaluates a multimodal generative framework for structural and functional MRI features, finding that a modality-aware graph VAE (gMMVAE) with graph encoding of functional connectivity outperforms VAE, transformer, GAN, and diffusion model variants across generation fidelity, reconstruction quality, efficiency, and latent discriminability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop optimal architectural frameworks with appropriate encoding and latent space processes for studying structural and functional properties of the brain through generative models.

Method: Systematic evaluation of encoding strategies, latent multimodal fusion, and generative model selection using structural GMV and static FNC features; comparison of VAEs, transformers, GANs, and diffusion models; proposed multimodal graph VAE (gMMVAE) with modality-aware graph encoding of functional connectivity into lower-dimensional latent space.

Result: gMMVAE surpasses alternative generative variants across multiple metrics for generation fidelity, reconstruction quality, efficiency, and latent space discriminability; graph encoding of functional connectivity outperforms vectorized encoders or direct data space approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed gMMVAE demonstrates robust potential for multimodal neuroimaging analysis, highlighting the importance of graph-based encoding strategies for functional connectivity in generative frameworks.

Abstract: While generative models enable encoding of complex neuroimaging data for feature generation and reconstruction, developing optimal architectural frameworks with appropriate encoding and latent space processes is crucial for studying structural and functional properties of the brain. We design a multimodal generative framework for structural and functional magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) features through systematic evaluation of encoding strategies, latent multimodal fusion, and generative model selection. Using structural gray matter volume (GMV) and static functional network connectivity (sFNC) features from a large neuroimaging dataset, we analyze generative frameworks involving variational autoencoders (VAEs), transformers, generative adversarial networks (GANs), and diffusion models. Architectures that employ modality-aware graph encoding of functional connectivity into a lower-dimensional latent space outperform vectorized encoders or direct data space approaches. The proposed multimodal graph VAE (gMMVAE) surpasses alternative generative variants across multiple metrics for generation fidelity, reconstruction quality, efficiency, and latent space discriminability, highlighting its potential for robust multimodal neuroimaging analysis.

</details>


### [108] [Gauge-Invariant Learnable Spectral Positional Encodings for Directed Graphs via Hermitian Block Krylov Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07032)
*Jiaqing Xie,Yuxin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes learnable spectral positional encodings for directed graphs using a matrix function of a normalized magnetic operator, computed efficiently via Hermitian block Krylov subspace methods. The method is gauge-invariant by construction, requires only O(log(1/ε)) block steps for heat-resolvent families, and outperforms direction-blind PEs on directed SBMs while also improving heterophilous undirected benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral positional encodings for directed graphs face two key challenges: (1) magnetic Laplacians require expensive O(n³) Hermitian eigendecompositions per potential, and (2) complex eigenvectors are defined only up to unitary gauge, requiring basis-invariant architectures.

Method: The PE takes the form h_θ(A_q)R, where A_q is a normalized magnetic operator, h_θ is a learnable scalar spectral response, and R is a block of random probes. It is computed in a Hermitian block Krylov subspace using only sparse matrix-vector products, avoiding explicit eigendecomposition. Theoretical guarantees show k = O(log(1/ε)) block steps suffice for heat-resolvent families, with a covering-number argument for why low-dimensional structured families generalize better than per-eigenvalue weights.

Result: On a directed SBM where symmetrization is uninformative, direction-blind PEs perform at chance while magnetic Krylov PEs converge to the exact-eigendecomposition oracle with increasing depth. The probes yield gauge-invariant pairwise features with 1/√s Monte-Carlo error, and the undirected (q=0) case improves heterophilous benchmarks over no-PE and polynomial baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed approach provides an efficient, scalable, and theoretically grounded method for spectral PEs on directed graphs, eliminating the need for expensive eigendecompositions and gauge-invariant architectures while achieving strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Spectral positional encodings (PEs) for \emph{directed} graphs face two obstacles: magnetic Laplacians require an $O(n^3)$ Hermitian eigendecomposition per potential, and their complex eigenvectors are defined only up to unitary gauge, which prior work handles with basis-invariant architectures. We propose learnable spectral PEs of the form $h_θ(A_q)\,R$, where $A_q$ is a normalized magnetic operator, $h_θ$ a learnable scalar spectral response, and $R$ a block of random probes. Because the PE is a \emph{matrix function} of the operator, it is gauge-invariant by construction. We compute it in a Hermitian block Krylov subspace from sparse matrix--vector products only, prove that $k = O(\log(1/\varepsilon))$ block steps suffice uniformly over heat--resolvent response families, and give a covering-number argument for why low-dimensional structured families generalize where free per-eigenvalue weights overfit. On a directed SBM whose symmetrization is uninformative by construction, direction-blind PEs stay at chance while magnetic Krylov PEs converge to the exact-eigendecomposition oracle as the depth grows. The same probes yield gauge-invariant pairwise features with $1/\sqrt{s}$ Monte-Carlo error, and the undirected $q{=}0$ case improves heterophilous benchmarks over no-PE and polynomial baselines.

</details>


### [109] [Intrinsic Green's Learning: Supervised Learning on Manifolds via Inverse PDE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07034)
*Alexandre Quemy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IGL models a target function on a manifold as the solution to a linear PDE with a learned source term, integrated against a Green's kernel. It uses an encoder to discover a low-dimensional coordinate chart where the source and kernel decompose as low-rank tensors, reducing high-dimensional integrals to linear-cost 1D integrals. A two-stage algorithm prevents dimensional collapse, and learnable gates automatically discover the intrinsic dimension. Validated on synthetic manifolds and MNIST, achieving near-optimal classification and intrinsic dimension recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To efficiently model functions on manifolds by leveraging the intrinsic geometry, avoiding direct approximation in high-dimensional ambient space and instead learning a source term for a linear PDE solved via Green's functions.

Method: (1) Model target function as solution to linear PDE with learned source. (2) Use encoder to discover low-dimensional coordinate chart where source and Green's kernel decompose as low-rank tensors. (3) Collapse high-dimensional integral into independent 1D integrals with O(d) cost. (4) Two-stage algorithm: coordinate discovery first, then near-convex linear solve for source fitting. (5) Learnable gates on each coordinate to auto-discover intrinsic dimension.

Result: Validated on synthetic manifolds and MNIST, achieving near-optimal classification and automatic recovery of the intrinsic dimension.

Conclusion: IGL provides an efficient, geometrically principled framework for learning on manifolds that scales linearly with intrinsic dimension, avoids dimensional collapse via two-stage training, and automatically discovers the manifold's intrinsic dimension.

Abstract: We introduce Intrinsic Green's Learning (IGL), a framework that models a target function on a manifold as the solution to a linear PDE whose source term is learned from data. Rather than approximating the target directly, IGL learns a source and integrates it against a Green's kernel. An encoder discovers a low-dimensional coordinate chart on the manifold where both the source and the kernel decompose as low-rank tensors, collapsing a high-dimensional integral into independent one-dimensional integrals with cost linear in the intrinsic dimension. A two-stage algorithm separates coordinate discovery from source fitting, a near-convex linear solve, preventing the dimensional collapse of joint training. Learnable gates on each coordinate automatically discover the intrinsic dimension of the manifold. We validate IGL on synthetic manifolds and on MNIST, where it simultaneously achieves near-optimal classification and automatic recovery of the intrinsic dimension.

</details>


### [110] [On the Principles of Deep Feedforward ReLU Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07035)
*Changcun Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies the mechanism of deep feedforward ReLU networks by extending principles from two-layer networks, using path-based relationships to explain how hidden-layer units form piecewise linear manifolds (rather than hyperplanes) to partition input space, thereby revealing the "black box" of training solutions obtained via back-propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover the internal mechanism of deep feedforward ReLU networks (a key component of deep learning) by explaining how training solutions arise from back-propagation, addressing the longstanding "black box" problem.

Method: Based on the simplest two-layer ReLU network, the paper generalizes principles to deeper architectures. It introduces the concept of "path" and relationships between paths as central analytical tools, showing that hidden units form piecewise linear manifolds (not hyperplanes) and that multiple strict partial orders and continuity restrictions govern the network's behavior.

Result: The paper demonstrates that deep ReLU networks can be understood by generalizing principles from two-layer networks: units produce piecewise linear manifolds for space partitioning, and the combination of simple principles (multiple strict partial orders, continuity restriction) yields the complex instantiations observed in training solutions.

Conclusion: The black box of deep feedforward ReLU networks is revealed through path-based analysis, showing that the internal mechanism is a composition of basic, understandable principles rather than an opaque process.

Abstract: The architecture of deep feedforward neural networks is ubiquitous in deep learning, either as a whole system or as a subnetwork of other architectures, and thus its mechanism is a key ingredient of the black box of neural networks. On the basis of the simplest two-layer ReLU network, this paper systematically studies the mechanism of deep feedforward ReLU networks with multiple hidden layers and successfully explains the training solution obtained by the back-propagation algorithm. The concept of a path, especially in terms of the relationships between paths, plays a central role in uncovering the mystery of the black box. It is shown that a unit of a deep ReLU network can form a piecewise linear manifold to divide the input space, instead of a hyperplane of the two-layer case. How to efficiently use the hidden-layer units to produce both linear functions and partitions of the input space is also a central problem. The principles of a two-layer ReLU network can be generalized to the deeper case to a large extent, such as multiple strict partial orders and continuity restriction. The combination of the basic and simple principles proposed can yield complicated instantiations including the training solutions, and in this sense the black box of deep feedforward ReLU networks is revealed.

</details>


### [111] [Complexity-Budgeted, Interaction-Aware Interpretable Model for Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07060)
*Srikumar Krishnamoorthy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IAIML is a framework for inherently interpretable tabular classification that preserves pairwise feature interactions via adaptive discretization, interaction scoring, and a partitioned explanation budget, achieving competitive AUC with far fewer components than comparable methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard interpretable classifiers for tabular data use marginal feature screening, which discards variables whose predictive value only emerges through joint configurations with other variables. IAIML addresses this limitation by detecting and incorporating pairwise interactions.

Method: IAIML uses three coordinated mechanisms: (1) adaptive per-feature discretization, (2) finite-grid pairwise interaction scoring, and (3) a partitioned explanation budget. Detected interactions are handled via either relaxing the screening filter or constructing explicit pair terms for a sparse downstream classifier.

Result: On 40 datasets (24 real-world + 16 synthetic), IAIML achieves mean AUC within 1.4 points of tuned gradient-boosted ensembles while using 14–28× fewer fitted components. Outperforms all baselines on datasets with strong pairwise interactions and low marginal signal. EBM has a small but significant AUC advantage but with a much larger lookup-table footprint.

Conclusion: IAIML is a compact, interaction-aware interpretable framework suitable for settings where bounded explanation size and controlled treatment of feature interactions are design requirements. Performance degrades on datasets requiring higher-order (>pairwise) interactions.

Abstract: Inherently interpretable classifiers for tabular data typically rely on sparse features, rules, or patterns that users can inspect directly. The marginal feature-screening step common to these methods can discard variables whose predictive value emerges only through joint configurations with other variables. We present Interaction Aware Interpretable Machine Learning (IAIML), a framework that addresses this limitation through three coordinated mechanisms: adaptive per-feature discretization, finite-grid pairwise interaction scoring, and a partitioned explanation budget. Detected interactions are routed through one of two strategies: relaxing the screening filter so that interaction-supported variables enter the pattern search, or constructing explicit pair terms for a sparse downstream classifier. On a 40-dataset panel comprising 24 real-world tabular benchmarks and 16 synthetic interaction stress tests, evaluated under nested cross-validation, IAIML achieves mean AUC within 1.4 points of tuned gradient-boosted ensembles while requiring roughly 14--28 times fewer fitted explanation components. On datasets with strong pairwise interaction structure and low marginal signal, IAIML outperforms all baselines. Among compact interpretable methods, IAIML is comparable to RuleFit in AUC and component count and is less expensive to tune. EBM obtains a small but significant AUC advantage across the full panel, with a substantially larger lookup-table footprint. Performance degrades on datasets requiring higher-order interactions beyond the pairwise scope. Component-isolated ablations confirm that adaptive discretization and interaction-aware admission each contribute incrementally. These results support IAIML as a compact, interaction-aware framework appropriate for settings where bounded explanation size and controlled treatment of feature interactions are design requirements.

</details>


### [112] [Multiplication Beyond Groups: Stratified Fourier Mechanisms in Transformer Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07066)
*Zitong Andrew Chen,Junaid Hasan,Akhil Srinivasan,Hemkesh Bandi,Jarod Alper*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Small transformers learn modular multiplication over composite moduli by partitioning inputs into local algebraic regions where group-like Fourier mechanisms apply, rather than using a single global representation space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior mechanistic analyses of transformers' algorithmic reasoning have focused on globally invertible operations (e.g., group operations). The authors aim to understand how transformers learn non-invertible operations like modular multiplication over composite moduli, which involve zero-divisors and lack global group structure.

Method: The authors propose the monoid extension, a localized generalization of Group Composition via Representation (GCR). They analyze transformers trained on square-free modular multiplication by examining embedding organization, attention patterns (class-sensitive routing, low-rank write directions), and local character features.

Result: Embeddings organize around local hierarchical algebraic regions. Attention exhibits class-sensitive routing and low-rank write directions. Local character features explain a large fraction of output logits.

Conclusion: Representation-theoretic mechanisms previously identified for group operations can extend beyond groups to more general algebraic structures (monoids) through localized computation.

Abstract: Transformers have demonstrated a remarkable ability to learn algorithmic reasoning, yet mechanistic analyses have mostly focused on globally invertible operations such as cyclic addition and group composition. In this work, we investigate how small transformers learn modular integer multiplication over composite moduli, a fundamentally non-invertible operation due to the presence of zero-divisors. We propose the monoid extension: a localized generalization of Group Composition via Representation (GCR) that suggests the learned computation does not rely on a single global representation space. Instead, the model partitions the input space into local hierarchical algebraic regions, where group-like structure survives and Fourier mechanisms can be applied. In transformers trained on square-free modular multiplication, we find that embeddings organize around these regions, attention exhibits class-sensitive routing and low-rank write directions, and local character features explain a large fraction of the model's output logits. Our results suggest that representation-theoretic mechanisms previously identified for group operations can extend beyond groups to more general structures.

</details>


### [113] [Distributed Sparse Interventions in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07128)
*Maximilian S. Ernst,Lorenz Linhardt,Aaron Peikert,Oliver Eberle*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI), a neuron-level intervention method that captures nonlinear effects missed by global steering approaches. DSI identifies sparse neuron sets (as few as 0.01% of neurons) across layers to activate task behavior in instruction-tuned language models, enabling fine-grained control and analysis of task-relevant computations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior steering approaches model task representations as approximately linear and additive in activation space, but fail to capture neuron-specific nonlinear effects on model outputs, limiting precise control and understanding of task behavior.

Method: DSI intervenes at the neuron level, accounting for nonlinearities and interactions between neurons across layers, to identify sparse sets of neurons that elicit task-relevant computations through distributed sparse interventions.

Result: DSI activates task behavior in instruction-tuned language models by intervening on as few as 0.01% of neurons, demonstrating effectiveness across a range of tasks with highly sparse, distributed neuron sets.

Conclusion: Sparse, distributed interventions in the neuron basis enable fine-grained control over model behavior, localization of task-relevant neuron sets, and provide insights into individual neuron roles and task composition through set-based analysis.

Abstract: Language models perform a wide range of tasks at varying levels of abstraction with the capacity to flexibly infer tasks from context, execute multiple tasks simultaneously, and select among competing tasks. To study the role of model components in task behaviour, their causal influence can be investigated through interventions. Prior work on model steering has largely focused on interventions along global directions in activation space, modeling task representations as approximately linear and additive. By studying interventions at the neuron level, we find substantial, neuron-specific nonlinear effects on model outputs that are not captured by current steering approaches. We introduce Distributed Sparse Interventions (DSI), an intervention approach that considers nonlinearities and interactions between neurons across layers to identify sparse sets of neurons that elicit task-relevant computations. Across a range of tasks, we demonstrate that DSI can activate task behaviour in instruction-tuned language models by localising and intervening on as few as 0.01% of neurons, highlighting the effectiveness of sparse, distributed interventions in the neuron basis. Additionally, adopting a set-based perspective enables computations over the identified neuron sets, offering insights into the roles of individual neurons by analysing their effects across tasks. Through sparse interventions, DSI enables fine-grained control over model behaviour, localisation of task-relevant neuron sets, and furthers our understanding of task composition.

</details>


### [114] [Fractal KV-Cache Archives: Lossless Symbolic Storage with In-Place Retrieval for Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07144)
*Vladimir Gusev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using contractive iterated-map codes as a storage format for quantized KV caches, enabling lossless compression, O(1) random access, O(1) amortized append, and simultaneous use as a search index. Per-head residual vector quantization reduces the archived cache by 36-54x relative to fp16 with 11-15% perplexity cost, and quantizing keys is ~4x more damaging than quantizing values.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The KV cache dominates memory cost in long-context autoregressive inference. Existing work focuses on compression (quantization, eviction, offloading), but the question of how to efficiently store and manage the resulting quantized symbol stream remains unexplored. The authors ask whether the storage layer can do more than just store.

Method: The authors revisit contractive iterated-map codes to serialize a symbol sequence into low-dimensional real vectors, forming a natural archive format for quantized KV caches. They conduct controlled experiments on GPT-2 with 1024-token contexts, using a small exact window (4 attention sinks + 32 recent tokens) and archiving the rest via per-head residual vector quantization. They also demonstrate the archive doubles as a search index for approximate substring queries.

Result: The archived KV cache achieves 36-54x compression relative to fp16 with only 11-15% perplexity degradation. A sharp key/value asymmetry is quantified: quantizing keys is roughly 4x more damaging than quantizing values, consistent with prior low-bit KV work. The archive supports O(1) random access and O(1) amortized append, and enables approximate substring search directly on stored vectors.

Conclusion: Contractive iterated-map codes are a viable storage format for quantized KV caches, combining compression, efficient access patterns, and search capability. The key/value asymmetry motivates hybrid bit allocation schemes. All code is released and reproducible on a laptop CPU.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache dominates the memory cost of long-context autoregressive inference, and a growing body of work compresses it through quantization, eviction, or offloading. We study a complementary question: once a position's KV state has been quantized to codebook indices, how should the resulting symbol stream be stored, and can the storage layer do more than store? A family of contractive iterated-map codes that serialize a symbol sequence into a sequence of low-dimensional real vectors is revisited, and it is shown that they form a natural archive format for a quantized KV cache with the following features. The method provides exactly the access pattern a growing cache requires. It is lossless, it runs in linear time, and supports O(1) random access and O(1) amortized append. A controlled study of the quantizer feeding this archive is conducted on GPT-2 with 1024-token contexts. Keeping a small exact window (4 attention sinks + 32 recent tokens) and archiving the rest, per-head residual vector quantization reduces the archived cache by 36-54x relative to an fp16 cache at a perplexity cost of 11-15%, and we quantify a sharp key/value asymmetry -- quantizing keys is roughly 4x more damaging than quantizing values, consistent with prior low-bit KV work -- and use it to allocate bits in a hybrid scheme. Finally, we show the archive is simultaneously a search index: approximate substring queries execute directly on the stored vectors, and matched context is decoded from the matched vector without ever materializing the surrounding text. We release all code; every number reproduces from a single command on a laptop CPU.

</details>


### [115] [Prior-matched evaluation of operational Earth-observation classifiers: a three-number reporting method demonstrated on Sentinel-1 internal-wave detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07146)
*Joao Pinelo,Joao Goncalves,Arun Shukla,Adriana Santos-Ferreira*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies a systematic evaluation gap in rare-event classifiers: balanced-test precision overstates real operational performance when the class prior is low (1-in-20). The authors demonstrate that prior correction and calibration cannot fix this at fixed recall, and propose a three-figure prior-matched reporting method (balanced-test, operational-prior, real post-deployment). Applied to an internal wave detection system from Sentinel-1, a precision-first, leakage-controlled development cycle with recall held at 0.80 achieves 0.927 operational-prior precision. The honest measure is the contrast among the three figures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The operational prior (≈1-in-20) was unknown at training time; reporting at a balanced (1:1) class balance produces a systematic artefact where balanced-test scores (e.g., 0.794 precision) greatly overstate real-world precision (0.192). This gap is invisible to the most commonly quoted metric, creating a mismatch that looks like a training problem but is fundamentally an evaluation problem.

Method: The authors propose prior-matched reporting using three precision figures: balanced-test, operational-prior, and real post-deployment. They adopt a precision-first, leakage-controlled development cycle where each classifier improvement is tested against a pre-registered margin. Recall is held at a minimum of 0.80, and the final model is certified against a sealed, single-read lockbox. Calibration is found inert; feature aggregation provides the only real lift.

Result: The promoted model achieves 0.927 precision at the operational prior while maintaining recall ≥ 0.80. An out-of-time check confirms that discrimination transfers to unseen periods but the fixed operating point does not. The gap between balanced-test (0.794) and operational precision (0.192 for the baseline; 0.927 for the promoted model) demonstrates the severity of prior-mismatch artefacts.

Conclusion: Prior-matched reporting—starting balanced and moving to the operational prior as the stream reveals it—is essential for honest evaluation of rare-event detectors. This approach transfers to any operational Earth-observation service bootstrapping a detector under a prior it has yet to discover.

Abstract: The Internal Waves Service screens the Sentinel-1 Wave-mode archive for internal solitary waves, routing detections to experts whose adjudication time is the resource the effort exists to conserve. Because attention is the cost of error, precision leads. Its classifier was trained and reported at a one-to-one class balance, fixed before the operational rate could be known. That rate has since emerged at roughly one scene in twenty, and a balanced-test score badly overstates the precision a validator meets. A model that scores 0.794 balanced-test precision scores 0.192 in real operation: the gap is a systematic artefact of reporting at the wrong prior, invisible to the metric most work quotes. We show the mismatch to be an evaluation problem in the costume of a training one at a fixed recall, prior correction and calibration cannot move precision, and answer it with a prior-matched reporting method based on three figures: balanced-test, operational-prior, and real post-deployment, whose contrast is the honest measure. A precision-first, leakage-controlled development cycle then improves the classifier lever by lever, each promoted only against a pre-registered margin; added capacity not clearing it, calibration inert, feature aggregation the one real lift, so the honest negatives are as much a result as the gain. Holding recall at a floor of 0.80 and certifying against a sealed, single-read lockbox, the promoted model reports 0.927 precision at the operational prior; an out-of-time check confirms discrimination transfers to unseen periods while a fixed operating point does not. Prior-matched reporting, begin balanced, then move to the prior as the stream reveals it, transfers to any operational Earth-observation service bootstrapping a rare-event detector under a prior it has yet to discover.

</details>


### [116] [Information Allocation Dynamics in Neural Network Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07156)
*Zhang Gongyue,Liu Donghan,Ren Weihong,Sheng Yixuan,Wang Zhiyong,Liu Honghai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes optimizer implicit bias through information allocation dynamics between weight-like and bias-like parameter pathways, introducing a continuous preconditioning exponent p to describe the relative write-in ratio of training signals. It shows that weight and bias corrections preserve different residual signal components, and optimizer preconditioning adjusts their relative strength during training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies analyze optimizer biases from the geometry of final solutions, but lack a clear understanding of how these biases form during training. This work aims to reveal the internal dynamical mechanism of optimizer implicit bias formation.

Method: The authors propose an information allocation dynamics perspective, using a continuous preconditioning exponent p to describe the relative allocation of training signals between weight-like and bias-like parameter pathways. They analyze weight and bias update contributions to the same residual signal in a minimal linear model, then substitute preconditioned updates into the residual update equation.

Result: Weight correction terms preserve input-dependent residual signals, while bias correction terms preserve the residual mean direction. Optimizer preconditioning changes the relative strength of these two terms, meaning optimizer implicit bias is reflected in the relative write-in ratio of training signals across different parameter pathways.

Conclusion: The paper moves optimizer implicit bias analysis from solution-space geometry to update dynamics during training, revealing that relative update allocation between weight and bias-like parameters is a key dynamical mechanism affecting parameter trajectories and generalization.

Abstract: Different optimizers have different update biases, but these biases are usually implicit. Existing studies mainly analyze or control such biases from the geometry of the final solution. However, how optimizer bias forms during training still lacks a clear internal mechanism.
  This paper proposes an information allocation dynamics perspective. It interprets optimizer implicit bias as the relative allocation of training signals between weight-like and bias-like parameter pathways. This allocation can be described and adjusted by a continuous preconditioning exponent \(p\).
  To characterize this mechanism, we first analyze the update contributions of weight and bias to the same residual signal in a minimal linear model. The weight correction term preserves input-dependent residual signals, while the bias correction term preserves the residual mean direction. They therefore correspond to different projection pathways of the residual signal. After substituting the preconditioned update into the residual update equation, the optimizer can change the relative strength of the weight correction term and the bias correction term through different preconditioning factors. Therefore, optimizer implicit bias is not only reflected in the final solution or the global training trajectory. It is also reflected in the relative write-in ratio of training signals across different parameter pathways.
  Overall, this paper moves the analysis of optimizer implicit bias from solution-space geometry to update dynamics during training. It reveals that the relative update allocation between weight and bias-like parameters is an important dynamical mechanism that affects parameter trajectories and generalization behavior.

</details>


### [117] [Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization for Multi-Task Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07178)
*Zetian Hu,Shunyu Liu,Junjie Zhang,Yongcheng Jing,Ting-En Lin,Yongbin Li,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EPPO introduces entropy-aware adaptive clipping to GRPO to address exploration-exploitation pace mismatch across tasks in multi-task agentic RL for LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-task agentic RL for LLMs focuses on single-task settings, but real-world deployment requires generalist agents solving multiple tasks simultaneously. The paper identifies a critical phenomenon: different tasks exhibit exploration-exploitation pace mismatch, where easier tasks converge prematurely to low-entropy policies hindering harder task learning, and harder tasks push easier tasks back to high-entropy exploration, creating inter-task entropy crossovers and spikes.

Method: Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization (EPPO) replaces GRPO's fixed clipping threshold with a task-wise dynamic clipping mechanism that adapts based on task entropy: tightening updates for over-confident (low-entropy) tasks and relaxing them for under-explored (high-entropy) tasks.

Result: Experiments on multi-task agentic benchmarks show EPPO yields superior results compared to counterparts.

Conclusion: EPPO effectively stabilizes multi-task optimization for agentic LLMs by coordinating entropy across tasks via adaptive clipping, mitigating the inter-task entropy mismatch problem.

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs of Reinforcement Learning (RL) have highlighted its potential for complex agentic Large Language Model (LLM) tasks. However, existing efforts largely focus on single-task settings, whereas real-world deployment necessitates a generalist agent capable of solving multiple tasks simultaneously. In this work, we identify a critical yet underexplored phenomenon in multi-task agentic RL: different tasks can exhibit exploration-exploitation pace mismatch. Specifically, easier tasks may converge early to low-entropy policies that hinder learning on harder tasks, while harder tasks can, in turn, push easier tasks back toward high-entropy exploration. This back-and-forth interaction creates inter-task entropy crossovers and frequent entropy spikes. Inspired by this observation, we introduce Entropy Pacing Policy Optimization (EPPO) for multi-task agentic LLMs, which coordinates entropy across tasks to stabilize multi-task optimization. At the core of EPPO is a task-wise dynamic clipping mechanism that replaces the fixed clipping threshold in Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a task entropy-aware adaptive bound, tightening updates for over-confident tasks while relaxing them for under-explored ones. Experiments on the multi-task agentic benchmarks demonstrate that the proposed EPPO yields results superior to its counterparts.

</details>


### [118] [Predicting LLM Safety Before Release by Simulating Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07184)
*Marcus Williams,Hannah Sheahan,Cameron Raymond,Tomek Korbak,Deng Pan,Peilin Yang,Leon Maksin,Ningyi Xie,Phillip Guo,Ian Kivlichan,Micah Carroll*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes "deployment simulation" — a method that replays real conversation prefixes from prior model deployments with a candidate model's responses to estimate pre-deployment misbehavior rates. Evaluated across GPT-5-series models, it outperforms traditional adversarial evaluations in predicting real-world misalignment rates, and can work with public chat datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional pre-deployment safety evaluations provide limited evidence about how often undesired model behavior will occur in deployment because they have insufficient coverage, are unrepresentative, and are generally recognizable as tests.

Method: Starting from de-identified conversations from a previous model deployment, hold fixed the initial conversation prefix and regenerate the next response using a candidate model. The resulting responses are audited for novel misalignments and used to estimate the prevalence of model misbehavior before deployment.

Result: Deployment simulation produces informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior rates and outperforms baselines based on adversarially selected production data. Its evaluation-awareness point estimates were much closer to production traffic than traditional evaluations. The method can be seeded from public chat datasets while remaining informative about production misbehavior rates.

Conclusion: Deployment simulation helps evaluators forecast how language models will behave in the real world and supports more quantitative assessment of deployment risk.

Abstract: Pre-deployment safety evaluations aim to inform the downstream risks of releasing a new AI model. Yet most evaluations provide limited evidence about how often undesired model behavior will occur in deployment: they generally have insufficient coverage, are unrepresentative, and are generally recognizable as tests. To address these concerns, we study a simple way to simulate a model deployment: starting from de-identified conversations from a previous model deployment, we hold fixed the initial conversation prefix and regenerate the next response using a candidate model. The resulting responses can then both be audited for novel misalignments and used to estimate the prevalence of model misbehavior before deployment. We evaluate deployment simulation across four GPT-5-series deployments, using registered, outcome-blinded predictions for GPT-5.4 and retrospective analyses of three earlier releases. We find that deployment simulation produces informative estimates of post-deployment misbehavior rates and outperforms baselines based on adversarially selected production data; its evaluation-awareness point estimates were also much closer to production traffic than those from traditional evaluations. We also identify the realism of tool resampling as a central challenge for further improving predictions and share results suggesting that this challenge is surmountable even in complex tool-use settings. Finally, we show that deployment simulation can be seeded from public chat datasets and remain informative about production misbehavior rates, suggesting a path for external researchers to run deployment-grounded evaluations without access to private production logs. Overall, deployment simulation helps evaluators forecast how language models will behave in the real world and supports more quantitative assessment of deployment risk.

</details>


### [119] [Geometric--Nongeometric Optimizer Calculus: A Modular Language for Reachable Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07206)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a modular "geometric–nongeometric optimizer calculus" for auditing and analyzing adaptive optimizers, proving that full positive-definite geometry can express all strict descent directions. It defines direction residuals for restricted metrics, formulates optimizer design as Pareto optimization over module budgets, and proposes trajectory-level residual complexity. Diagnostic prototypes (full-metric probe and Muon-style matrix-operator) are provided as evidence, without claiming large-scale SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive optimizers combine multiple mechanisms (metric, memory, step-size, constraints, etc.) but lack a unified formal framework to audit their reachable gradient directions under explicit constraints. The paper aims to provide a modular language and formal theory to decompose, analyze, and compare optimizers based on which descent directions they can express, rather than just empirical performance.

Method: The authors propose a modular decomposition of optimizers into a geometric module (positive cometric family mapping covectors to parameter-space directions) and nongeometric modules (information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, discretization). They prove a direction-expressivity theorem for full positive-definite geometry, define restricted direction residuals for admissible metric families (diagonal, block), and lift pointwise residuals to trajectory-level residual complexity. They provide diagnostic prototypes: a full-metric probe and a Muon-style matrix-operator candidate.

Result: Full positive-definite geometry can express exactly all strict descent directions away from critical points. Diagonal and block geometries have restricted expressivity characterized by exact conditions. Optimizer design is a Pareto optimization problem over module budgets. Trajectory-level residual complexity couples direction mismatch with variation of the explaining geometry. The full-metric probe solves deterministic quadratic benchmarks to numerical precision; the Muon-style candidate shows matrix-operator updates can be audited with the calculus.

Conclusion: The geometric–nongeometric optimizer calculus provides a principled, modular language for auditing and reasoning about gradient methods. It reframes optimizer comparison from single global rankings to Pareto analysis over module budgets. The theory is supported by diagnostic prototypes, but the paper serves as a theory and benchmark-language manuscript, not a large-scale empirical claim.

Abstract: Adaptive optimizers mix several mechanisms: a metric or preconditioner maps gradients to descent directions, while estimation, memory, step-size control, constraints, stochasticity, target modification, and discretization determine which directions are available and how they are used. We introduce geometric--nongeometric optimizer calculus, a modular language for auditing reachable gradient methods under explicit oracle, budget, state, and rule constraints. The geometric module is a positive cometric family that maps covectors to parameter-space directions; the nongeometric modules are information, memory, control, operator, noise, target, and discretization mechanisms. The main formal result is a direction-expressivity theorem: away from critical points, full positive-definite geometry expresses exactly the strict descent directions. We then define restricted direction residuals for admissible metric families, prove exact expressivity conditions for diagonal and block geometries, and separate this direction-level diagnostic from condition-number geometric complexity. The resulting design problem is a Pareto optimization over module budgets, not a single universal optimizer ordering. We also lift pointwise residuals to a trajectory-level residual complexity that couples direction mismatch with the variation of the explaining geometry. We include diagnostic prototypes only as evidence for the language: a high-information full-metric probe solves deterministic quadratic benchmarks to numerical precision, while a practical Muon-style PyTorch candidate gives small-scale evidence that matrix-operator updates can be audited by the calculus. The paper is a theory and benchmark-language manuscript; it does not claim large-scale optimizer state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [120] [HPG-Diff: Hierarchical physics-guided diffusion with differentiable connectivity constraints for topology optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07233)
*Jinbo Yang,Mingyue Yuan,Boyuan Zhang,Yoshifumi Kitamura,Shikai Jing*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HPG-Diff is a physics-guided diffusion framework for topology optimization that uses hierarchical physics features and thermal-conduction-inspired floating material suppression to achieve low compliance errors (0.87% in-distribution, 5.29% out-of-distribution) and minimal floating material ratios (~2.5-2.9%), with preliminary evidence of adaptability to rectangular domains via LoRA fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep generative models for topology optimization lack intrinsic physics guidance, leading to poor generalizability across unseen boundary conditions and the formation of floating material artifacts.

Method: Proposes HPG-Diff with two synergistic mechanisms: (1) hierarchical physics-guided strategy aligning precomputed physics features with the denoising process, and (2) floating material suppression loss as a differentiable connectivity constraint inspired by thermal conduction, simulating virtual heat propagation from load positions to penalize floating material.

Result: Average compliance errors of 0.87% (in-distribution) and 5.29% (out-of-distribution); floating material ratios reduced to 2.90% and 2.44% respectively. Case studies on 3:1 rectangular domains show adaptability via LoRA fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The hierarchical physics guidance and connectivity constraints effectively enhance generalizability and topological connectivity in generative topology optimization, with potential for domain adaptation through lightweight fine-tuning.

Abstract: Deep generative models offer a promising paradigm for topology optimization, enabling rapid design exploration. However, these approaches lack intrinsic physics guidance, often leading to poor generalizability across unseen boundary conditions and the formation of floating material artifacts. To address these limitations, we propose Hierarchical Physics-Guided Diffusion (HPG-Diff), a novel diffusion framework that enforces physics consistency through two synergistic mechanisms. First, we introduce a hierarchical physics-guided strategy that aligns different precomputed physics features with the denoising process, guiding material distribution toward optimal load paths to enhance generalizability. Second, we propose a floating material suppression loss as a differentiable connectivity constraint inspired by thermal conduction to improve topological connectivity. By simulating a virtual heat propagation process from load positions, this mechanism explicitly penalizes floating material during training. Quantitative evaluations demonstrate that HPG-Diff achieves average compliance errors of 0.87% (in-distribution) and 5.29% (out-of-distribution), while reducing floating material ratios to 2.90% and 2.44%, respectively. Furthermore, case studies on a 3:1 rectangular domain, including cantilever and bridge benchmarks, provide preliminary evidence that lightweight LoRA fine-tuning with a small dataset can support the adaptation of HPG-Diff to rectangular non-square domains.

</details>


### [121] [Safe Reinforcement Learning using Ideas from Model Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07252)
*Georg Schäfer,Jakob Rehrl,Stefan Huber,Simon Hirlaender*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid framework integrating deep reinforcement learning with model predictive control to enforce strict safety constraints during RL exploration. Offline MPC computes a feasible state-action space, and a safety filter projects RL actions onto this verified set. Validated on a non-linear 1-DoF testbed, the approach ensures safe exploration and stable policy convergence on physical hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensuring strict, hard safety constraints during active RL learning in real-world cyber-physical systems and robotics, where violating mechanical limits can cause irreversible physical damage.

Method: A generalized framework combining DRL with MPC safety guarantees: offline MPC computes a feasible state-action space (all safe state-control combinations); during training/deployment, a safety filter projects the RL agent's instantaneous actions onto this globally verified feasible set.

Result: Systematic evaluation on a non-linear 1-DoF laboratory testbed demonstrated successful exploration and stable policy convergence on physical hardware.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively enables safe exploration and stable convergence in real-world RL applications by enforcing hard safety constraints through formal MPC guarantees.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) enables the synthesis of control policies directly from data, making it highly appealing for complex cyber-physical systems (CPSs) and robotics. A persistent challenge, however, is ensuring strict, hard safety constraints during the active learning phase. In real-world physical systems, violating mechanical limits can cause irreversible damage, necessitating that exploration remains strictly within safe operational regions. We propose a generalized framework that combines the adaptive, high-performance nature of deep reinforcement learning (DRL) with the formal safety guarantees of model predictive control (MPC). Using a mathematical model of the system dynamics, offline MPC computations define a feasible state-action space, representing all safe combinations of system states and control inputs that guarantee constraint satisfaction. During training and deployment, the RL agent's instantaneous actions are projected onto this globally verified feasible set via a safety filter. We systematically evaluate our generalized approach on a non-linear 1-DoF laboratory testbed, demonstrating successful exploration and stable policy convergence on physical hardware.

</details>


### [122] [FMMVCC: Fuzzy Mamba-based Multi-View Contrastive Clustering for Univariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07258)
*Donato Cerciello,Leonardo Schiavo,Angel Panizo-LLedot,Javier Huertas Tato,David Camacho*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FMMVCC is a Mamba-based deep clustering framework for time series that uses state space sequence modeling (linear complexity) and multi-view self-supervised learning with temporal masking/augmentations, outperforming baselines on 15 benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series data often lack annotations, making supervised learning impractical. Existing deep clustering methods either fail to capture long-range temporal dependencies or suffer from high computational cost, motivating the need for an efficient unsupervised approach.

Method: FMMVCC employs Mamba (state space sequence modeling) to learn temporal representations with linear complexity, combined with multi-view self-supervised learning that leverages temporal masking and augmentations.

Result: Evaluated on 15 benchmarks, FMMVCC achieved the best performance in 29 out of 60 metric evaluations and the highest average rank across all tested scenarios, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed Mamba-based framework with multi-view self-supervision is an effective and efficient solution for unsupervised time series clustering, addressing long-range dependency capture and computational cost limitations of prior methods.

Abstract: In many realistic scenarios, large volumes of time series data are generated with limited or expensive annotations. This limitation makes supervised learning methods difficult to apply and leads to the use of unsupervised approaches capable of discovering meaningful structures directly from raw data. Clustering therefore plays a crucial role in organizing time series into groups that share similar temporal patterns, enabling exploratory analysis and downstream tasks without requiring manual labeling. However, existing deep clustering methods often struggle to capture long-range temporal dependencies or rely on architectures with high computational cost. This paper introduces FMMVCC, a Mamba-based deep clustering framework for time series that leverages state space sequence modeling to efficiently learn temporal representations with linear complexity. Additionally, it utilizes multi-view self-supervised learning with temporal masking and augmentations. Experimental evaluation in 15 benchmark datasets proves that FMMVCC consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving the best overall performance in 29 of 60 total metric evaluations and the highest average rank in all tested scenarios.

</details>


### [123] [BubbleSH: A Dataset of Rising Bubbles with Deformable Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07275)
*Rachna Ramesh,Kiet Bennema ten Brinke,Douwe Orij,Ivo Roghair,Vlado Menkovski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BubbleSH is a dataset of 3D bubble-swarm dynamics from high-fidelity simulations, representing bubble morphology via spherical harmonics, designed for data-driven modeling of deformable bubbly flows with benchmark metrics for trajectory and shape prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bubbly flows exhibit complex multiscale dynamics with deformable bubbles and strong coupling between kinematics and morphology, yet no suitable compact, physically expressive dataset existed for data-driven modeling of such systems.

Method: High-fidelity direct numerical simulations of bubbles rising in a periodic domain produce time-resolved trajectories, velocities, and shape evolution, with bubble morphology compactly represented using spherical harmonics. Evaluation metrics for trajectory and shape prediction are introduced.

Result: A lightweight yet physically expressive dataset (BubbleSH) capturing bubble kinematics, morphology, and interaction patterns is created. A permutationally and translationally equivariant probabilistic emulator is evaluated on the dataset using proposed metrics.

Conclusion: BubbleSH establishes a compact, high-fidelity dataset and benchmark for developing and evaluating data-driven models of deformable, chaotic multiphase systems, particularly generative models for trajectory prediction.

Abstract: Bubbly flows exhibit complex multiscale dynamics, with deformable bubbles interacting through the surrounding liquid and giving rise to strongly coupled kinematic and morphological behavior. We present BubbleSH, a bubbly flows dataset consisting of transient, three-dimensional bubble-swarm dynamics obtained from high-fidelity direct numerical simulations of bubbles rising in a periodic domain. The dataset provides time-resolved bubble trajectories, velocities, and shape evolution, with bubble morphology compactly represented using spherical harmonics. Designed to be lightweight yet physically expressive, the dataset enables data-driven modeling of bubbly flow simulators where shape deformation and bubble-bubble interactions play a central role. We characterize the dataset with bubble kinematics, morphology, and interaction patterns, and introduce evaluation metrics for both trajectory and shape prediction. The sensitivity of bubble-swarm dynamics to local perturbations makes BubbleSH particularly well suited to generative models that learn distributions over possible future trajectories. We evaluate a permutationally and translationally equivariant probabilistic emulator on BubbleSH given the proposed metrics. Therefore, we establish a compact, high-fidelity dataset and a benchmark for developing and evaluating data-driven models of deformable, chaotic multiphase systems.

</details>


### [124] [Nonlinear Bandit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07304)
*Tianshuo Zheng,Ting Wu,Zhi-Hua Zhou,Keqin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes the EHM algorithm for generalized linear bandits (GLB) under heavy-tailed noise using online mirror descent and adaptive Huber loss, achieving near-optimal regret. It extends to piecewise-constant contextual GLB (PGLB-EHM) and nonlinear bandits (NB-EHM) via bisection and affine lifting, all with sublinear regret.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
