TL;DR: The paper proposes Riemann-Normal-Coordinate Levenberg-Marquardt (RNC-LM), which extends geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections by reformulating the geodesic equation, eliminating parameter-effects curvature order by order in a moving tangent frame, and demonstrates improved convergence and robustness on classical benchmarks, PINN failure modes, and large-scale potential-energy-surface fitting (34x speedup).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) is limited because its tangent-space step is applied as a straight update in parameter coordinates, causing parameter-effects curvature to dominate nonlinearity. Geodesic acceleration only corrects this exactly in the infinitesimal-step limit, motivating a method that handles finite-step reparameterization consistency.

Method: RNC-LM reformulates the geodesic equation to extend geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections. It constructs finite-step updates with progressively higher reparameterization consistency in a moving tangent frame, combined with a line search along the RNC curve to control step length while keeping cost close to standard LM.

Result: On classical benchmarks, RNC-LM improves convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems. On a reaction-diffusion PINN failure-mode benchmark, it reduces relative L2 error to ~1e-3 and recovers physically meaningful solutions. On large-scale potential-energy-surface fitting, it achieves a 34-fold speedup over standard LM.

Conclusion: RNC-LM effectively eliminates the tangential component of residual acceleration order by order, making objective reduction more consistent with LM's linear model prediction, and delivers substantial gains in both convergence robustness and computational efficiency across diverse nonlinear least-squares problems.

Abstract: Nonlinear least-squares optimization is central to regression, physics-informed neural networks, and other machine-learning tasks. Such problems have a natural geometric interpretation, model predictions form a manifold in data space, while the chosen parameterization can introduce parameter-effects curvature that becomes a dominant source of nonlinearity. This exposes a limitation of the Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) method, its tangent-space step is applied as a straight update in parameter coordinates. Geodesic acceleration gives a second-order correction, but its removal of parameter-effect curvature is exact only in the infinitesimal-step limit. We propose a Riemann-normal-coordinate Levenberg-Marquardt method (RNC-LM) to improve this consistency for finite optimization steps. By reformulating the geodesic equation, RNC-LM extends geodesic acceleration to arbitrary-order corrections and constructs finite-step updates with progressively higher reparameterization consistency. A line search along the resulting RNC curve controls the traveled distance while keeping the cost close to standard LM. The method eliminates the tangential component of residual acceleration order by order in a moving tangent frame, making the actual objective reduction more consistent with the linear model prediction of LM. On classical nonlinear least-squares benchmarks, RNC-LM improves convergence and robustness in curved valleys and rank-deficient problems. On a reaction-diffusion PINN failure-mode benchmark, it reduces the relative L2 error to the order of 1e-3 and recovers a physically meaningful solution. On a large-scale machine-learning potential-energy-surface fitting task, it achieves a 34-fold speedup over standard LM.

</details>


### [150] [An optimal control approach for neural network architecture adaptation with a posteriori error estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07637)
*C G Krishnanunni,Thomas Scott,Tan Bui-Thanh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a depth-adaptive neural network method using a posteriori error estimation derived from continuous-time optimal control theory, employing dual weighted residual techniques to insert layers where error is maximal, and demonstrates superior generalization on scientific datasets including Navier-Stokes equation learning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of automatically determining optimal neural network depth, by deriving rigorous error estimates that quantify approximation error distribution across layers, enabling principled architecture adaptation rather than heuristic or exhaustive search methods.

Method: Formulates NN training as a continuous-time optimal control problem; derives error bounds via dual weighted residual (DWR) methodology from finite element analysis; treats weights/biases as piecewise linear functions varying across layers; inserts new layers at locations of maximum estimated error based on interval-wise error decomposition.

Result: Outperforms existing architecture adaptation methods in generalization performance on scientific datasets, including learning the observable-to-parameter map for the Navier-Stokes equation.

Conclusion: The proposed error-driven depth adaptation framework provides a theoretically grounded and practically effective approach for automatically refining neural network architectures, with particular promise for scientific machine learning applications involving complex nonlinear phenomena.

Abstract: This work presents a novel approach for adapting neural network architecture along the depth based on a posteriori error estimation. By formulating neural network training as a continuous-time optimal control problem, we derive rigorous error estimates that quantify how approximation error distributes across network layers. This error decomposition enables a principled depth adaptation strategy: new layers are inserted at locations of maximum estimated error, allowing the network to efficiently capture complex, nonlinear variations in the underlying problem. Our framework introduces a novel network architecture that treats weights and biases as piecewise linear functions varying across layers, with the error estimator bounding the discrepancy between this discrete representation and the true continuous optimal control solution. The approach leverages dual weighted residual methodology from finite element analysis to derive computable upper bounds on the functional error. A key theoretical contribution is the derivation of explicit error bounds that decompose the total approximation error into interval-wise contributions, providing a rigorous basis for targeted architecture refinement. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on scientific datasets, including learning the observable-to-parameter map for the Navier-Stokes equation. Numerical results reveal that our approach consistently outperforms existing architecture adaptation methods in terms of generalization performance.

</details>


### [151] [Guidance Breaks the Fitted Operator: A Terminal-Fitted Repair for Classifier-Free Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07665)
*Shiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) re-stiffens the discriminative subspace, causing residual divergence as sigma_min→0. The paper proposes a one-coefficient repair (r^(1+w)-r) that stabilizes high-guidance sampling without extra NFE, validated on CIFAR-10 and Stable Diffusion 1.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFG oversaturates and destabilizes at large guidance, forcing practitioners to use more steps or limited-interval schedules. The paper seeks to understand and fix this artifact through an asymptotic-preserving numerical-analysis lens.

Method: The authors analyze CFG via asymptotic-preserving numerical analysis, building on the result that DDIM is a fitted operator for the unguided terminal layer. They prove a guided clock barrier with three ordered step-size thresholds, showing that guidance re-stiffens the discriminative subspace to exponent 1+w, causing DDIM to no longer be fitted. They propose replacing CFG's w(r-1) with r^(1+w)-r on the guidance direction.

Result: The proposed repair removes CFG's sigma_min-divergent blow-up and is first-order accurate against the exact guided flow. On CIFAR-10 and Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM, it provides 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG, cuts residual amplification and saturation, and preserves classifier-proxy accuracy in hard-cell blocks.

Conclusion: The repair is a high-guidance stabilizer at no extra cost, not a universal quality knob. It has limits: not a universal image-quality win and not a uniformly better integrator against dense vanilla-CFG reference.

Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is the standard way to strengthen class-conditioning in diffusion and flow-matching samplers, yet at large guidance it oversaturates and destabilizes, symptoms practitioners suppress with more steps or limited-interval schedules. We analyze CFG through an asymptotic-preserving, numerical-analysis lens. Building on a recent result that the deterministic DDIM step is the unique fitted operator for the unguided terminal layer, exact on the final small-sigma stretch of sampling, we show that guidance re-stiffens exactly the discriminative subspace to an anomalous exponent 1+w. DDIM is therefore no longer fitted there, and on coarse meshes its guided residual diverges as sigma_min goes to zero. We prove a guided clock barrier with three ordered step-size thresholds, and read one-step oversaturation as its endpoint: a solver artifact on the calibration model rather than the continuous guided law. The same analysis yields a one-coefficient, zero-extra-NFE repair: replace CFG's w(r-1) by r^(1+w)-r on the guidance direction. On the calibration model's discriminative crossover, this removes CFG's sigma_min-divergent blow-up and is first-order accurate against the exact guided flow as sigma_min goes to zero. On learned CIFAR-10 checkpoints, and as a cross-domain smoke test on Stable Diffusion 1.5 DDIM, it acts as a high-guidance stabilizer at no extra cost rather than a universal quality knob: it cuts residual amplification and saturation, gives 9/9 point-FID wins over CFG on the tested grid, and preserves classifier-proxy target accuracy in the hard-cell blocks. We report the limits alongside: it is not a universal image-quality win, and against a dense vanilla-CFG reference it is not a uniformly better integrator of that field.

</details>


### [152] [PeTeR: Post-Training Robustification of Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07671)
*Adrian Ciotinga,Yeming Dai,YooJung Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PeTeR is a data-free post-training framework that robustifies pre-trained probabilistic circuits against distribution shifts without retraining from scratch, achieving competitive or superior performance to data-dependent robust learning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard likelihood-based PC learning is vulnerable to overfitting and fragile generalization under data noise, small sample sizes, or distribution shifts. Existing distributionally-robust optimization methods require training from scratch.

Method: PeTeR is a data-free post-training framework that robustifies pre-trained PCs against distribution shifts without retraining, applied after standard likelihood-based training.

Result: PeTeR effectively robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, achieving competitive or superior performance compared to data-dependent robust learning baselines across multiple density estimation benchmarks.

Conclusion: PeTeR provides an efficient alternative to training from scratch for robustifying PCs, eliminating the need for retraining while maintaining strong robustness properties.

Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) can model complex joint distributions while supporting exact and efficient computation of many inference queries. However, standard likelihood-based PC learning is vulnerable to overfitting and fragile generalization when confronted with data noise, small sample sizes, or distribution shifts. This can be mitigated using distributionally-robust optimization which consider worst-case distributions within a Wasserstein ball of the empirical distribution, but current methods are limited to training a model from scratch in this framework. Instead, we propose PeTeR: a novel, data-free post-training framework designed to robustify pre-trained PCs against distribution shifts without retraining from scratch. Empirical evaluations across multiple density estimation benchmarks demonstrate that PeTeR effectively robustifies baseline models against both random and adversarial perturbations, achieving competitive or superior performance to data-dependent robust learning baselines.

</details>


### [153] [Max Out GRPO Signal: Adaptive Trace Prefix Control for Hard Reasoning Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07674)
*Vladislav Beliaev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaPrefix-GRPO dynamically adjusts prefix length per problem to keep success rates near 50%, maximizing gradient signal and more than doubling GRPO's accuracy on hard math problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GRPO suffers from vanishing gradients when no rollout in a group succeeds, causing difficult problems to contribute no learning signal and wasting the most valuable frontier examples.

Method: AdaPrefix-GRPO prepends correct prefixes of reference solutions to problems, and uses a feedback controller that dynamically adjusts prefix length during training to maintain each problem's success rate near 50% (where GRPO's gradient signal is maximal), then gradually withdraws the prefix so the final model solves problems unaided. The implementation requires only data preparation and a loss mask on prefix tokens.

Result: At matched training FLOPs, AdaPrefix-GRPO achieves 2.1x accuracy improvement on a 0.6B model, 1.6x on Qwen3-1.7B, and 1.7x on AIME, while roughly halving trace length. Smaller models show larger gains.

Conclusion: AdaPrefix-GRPO significantly outperforms standard GRPO on hard math problems by dynamically adjusting prefix length to maintain optimal learning conditions, more than doubling accuracy for small models while reducing computational cost.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) stalls on a model's hardest problems: when no rollout in a group succeeds, the group-relative advantages vanish and the problem contributes no gradient, wasting the frontier examples we most want to learn from. Prepending a correct prefix of a reference solution raises the success rate, making prefix length a continuous knob on difficulty. Concurrent methods set the knob once; AdaPrefix-GRPO turns it into a feedback controller: throughout training it adjusts how much of the solution each problem gets, holding its success rate near 50%, where GRPO's gradient signal is largest, then withdraws the assistance entirely, so the deployed model solves problems unaided. On hard math, at matched training FLOPs, it more than doubles GRPO's accuracy on held-out problems from the training distribution for a 0.6B model (2.1x), with 1.6x on Qwen3-1.7B and 1.7x on AIME, while roughly halving trace length. The method is implemented in data preparation plus a loss mask on prefix tokens; the trainer is otherwise stock. The smaller the model, the larger the gain.

</details>


### [154] [How Data Shapes RoPE Frequency Usage: From Positional Scale Matching to Length Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07678)
*Xinyi Wu,Siyuan Liu,Ali Jadbabaie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoPE frequencies in transformers are selected to match the relative-distance structure of training data, formalized as a frequency-matching principle where optimal frequency scales inversely with dependency width. This explains non-uniform frequency usage, connects to position-interpolation-based length generalization, and shows that natural language's approximate self-similarity across scales enables test-time frequency scaling for long-context generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why trained transformers use RoPE frequencies highly non-uniformly despite being provided a fixed grid, and to identify the data-driven mechanism behind emergent frequency selection.

Method: Proposes a data-centered explanation: RoPE frequencies match the relative-distance structure of training data. Formalizes a field-resolution tradeoff using a data-induced dependency profile of width W, showing optimal frequency scales as 1/W. Validates on synthetic and text-based data, and connects to position-interpolation-based length generalization.

Result: Frequency-matching principle explains controlled observations; mid-low frequency bands in LMs arise from multi-scale dependency structure of natural language. Frequency scaling helps when longer-context dependencies are approximate dilations of training dependencies, but fails when dependencies don't scale with context length. Natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales.

Conclusion: Long-context generalization requires two forms of scale matching: between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how dependencies extend to longer contexts.

Abstract: Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) provide transformers with a fixed grid of positional frequencies, yet trained models use these frequencies highly non-uniformly. We study what determines this frequency usage and propose a data-centered explanation: RoPE frequencies are selected to match the relative-distance structure of the training data. Viewing each frequency as a positional lens, we formalize a field-resolution tradeoff and show that, for a data-induced dependency profile of width $W$, the optimal frequency scales as $1/W$. This frequency-matching principle explains controlled observations on synthetic and text-based data, and suggests that the mid-low frequency bands observed in language models arise from the multi-scale dependency structure of natural language. We further connect frequency selection to position-interpolation-based length generalization: scaling frequencies down expands the effective field while reducing resolution. This helps when longer-context dependencies are approximate dilations of those seen during training, but can fail when relevant dependencies do not scale with context length. Empirically, we show that natural language exhibits approximate self-similarity across positional scales, explaining why test-time frequency scaling can support long-context generalization. Overall, our results identify a data-driven mechanism behind emergent RoPE frequency usage and show that long-context generalization depends on two forms of scale matching: between learned frequencies and training-time dependencies, and between frequency scaling and how those dependencies extend to longer contexts.

</details>


### [155] [Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy for PDE-Constrained Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07682)
*Xiangming Huang,Guannan Zhang,Lu Lu,Raphaël Pestourie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NOTES integrates DeepONet-based neural operators with CMA-ES evolutionary strategy to perform inverse design in a compact latent space, achieving >95% efficiency in nanophotonic beam deflectors and compliance down to 246 in structural optimization, reducing dimensionality from 256 to 25.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse design of PDE-governed physical systems is computationally expensive due to high-dimensional, non-convex design spaces. Generative models lack robustness and transferability, while evolutionary strategies struggle in high-dimensional spaces.

Method: NOTES couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with CMA-ES to perform global optimization in a compact latent space encoding topology-aware priors. It decouples topology learning from governing physics via a PDE solver.

Result: Reduces design dimensionality from 256 to 25; achieves >95% efficiency in nanophotonic beam-deflector design (outperforming CMA-ES, topology optimization, and other baselines); discovers designs with compliance down to 246 in structural optimization.

Conclusion: NOTES provides a flexible, transferable, and robust framework for inverse design of physical systems by decoupling topology learning from physics solving.

Abstract: The inverse design of physical systems governed by partial differential equations is computationally demanding due to the high dimensionality and non-convexity of design spaces. Generative models for inverse design often lack robustness and transferability, whereas evolutionary strategies are robust but struggle in high-dimensional spaces. This paper introduces a Neural Operator-enabled Topology-informed Evolutionary Strategy (NOTES) that integrates dimensionality reduction, representation learning, and evolutionary optimization for efficient and transferable inverse design. NOTES couples a DeepONet-based neural operator with the Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES) to perform global optimization in a compact latent space that encodes topology-aware priors while discovering high-performance designs for unseen operating conditions. Applied to nanophotonic beam-deflector inverse design governed by Maxwell's equations, NOTES reduces the design dimensionality from 256 to 25 and consistently achieves over 95 percent efficiency, outperforming CMA-ES, topology optimization, and other baselines. Applied to structural optimization, NOTES discovers designs that achieve compliance down to 246. By decoupling topology learning of a DeepONet from the governing physics in a PDE solver, NOTES provides a flexible and transferable framework for the inverse design of physical systems.

</details>


### [156] [ECGLight: Compute-Light Framework For Paper ECG Digitization and Myocardial Infarction Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07683)
*Shreyasvi Natraj,Cyrus Achtari,Felice Gragnano,Andrea Milzi,Marco Valgimigli,Diego Paez-Granados*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight on-device pipeline that digitizes paper ECG photos into calibrated 12-lead signals and detects myocardial infarction using interpretable AI, running in <30 seconds on CPU-only devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Remote clinics still rely on paper ECG printouts due to limited connectivity and computational resources, preventing access to AI-based decision support and causing overlooked conditions like ACS with delayed reperfusion therapy.

Method: End-to-end lightweight on-device digitization-to-diagnosis pipeline: converts smartphone photos/scans of paper ECGs into calibrated 12-lead signals, screens for MI pathologies using AI, and employs SHAP for interpretability. Trained on 21,799 ECGs from PTB-XL and validated on hospital-acquired ECG-Matrix dataset.

Result: Runs in <30 seconds per ECG on CPU-only resources; achieves 95.51% accuracy (F1=0.9519) for MI detection on PTB-XL and 88.89% accuracy (F1=0.8862) for OMI detection on ECG-Matrix.

Conclusion: Legacy paper ECG records can be reliably democratized globally, providing scalable decision support when digital ECG export, connectivity, or high-end compute are unavailable.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG) is one of the most widely used tests for diagnosing cardiovascular disease. Yet several remote clinics still utilize paper ECG printouts for their analysis due to limited connectivity and computational capacity. As a result, vast numbers of physical ECGs obtained in remote areas still remain incapable of being accessed by contemporary artificial-intelligence (AI)-based decision support as they require high computational resources or strong high-speed internet connectivity. This causes several cases where conditions like acute coronary occlusion (ACS) is overlooked and reperfusion therapy delayed. Although prior work has tackled digitization and diagnosis separately, and utilized advanced AI models for them, there still remains a lack of a compute-light, on-device framework that reconstructs paper ECGs at high fidelity, while accurately supporting multiple clinically relevant endpoints. We address this need with an end-to-end lightweight on-device digitization-to-diagnosis pipeline that converts a smartphone photo or scan of a paper ECG into a calibrated 12-lead signal and screens for Myocardial Infarction (MI) pathologies, with SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to support interpretability. Trained and evaluated on 21,799 ECGs from the PTB-XL dataset and further validated on hospital-acquired ECG-Matrix dataset, the complete system runs in <30 s per ECG on CPU-only resources, achieving 95.51% accuracy (F1 = 0.9519) for MI detection on PTB-XL and 88.89% accuracy (F1 = 0.8862) for OMI detection on ECG-Matrix. This work showcases that legacy paper records can be reliably democratized in any part of the world, providing a scalable decision support when digital ECG export, connectivity, or high-end compute are unavailable

</details>


### [157] [The Key to Going Linear: Analysis-Driven Transformer Linearization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07706)
*Anna Kuzina,Paul N. Whatmough,Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates post hoc linearization of causal self-attention for long-context transformers, showing that softmax's key-dependent rank-1 orthogonal projections explain why delta-style networks outperform gated accumulation. The authors identify approximation error sources and introduce structural interventions (sink tokens, short convolutions, fixed-budget cache routing) that reduce the gap. Their approach scales to 32B-parameter models, outperforming prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matching complex adaptive-caching frameworks on long-context retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quadratic cost of causal self-attention creates a severe bottleneck for long-context transformer inference, and existing post hoc linearization pipelines make it difficult to identify which components preserve model quality.

Method: The authors isolate the effect of state update design in a strict frozen-backbone regime. They show softmax relies on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, which explains why delta-style networks outperform purely gated accumulation. They identify sources of approximation errors and introduce structural interventions: sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing.

Result: The approach scales across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters, outperforming prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matching the long-context retrieval of complex adaptive-caching frameworks.

Conclusion: Delta-style state updates are fundamentally better suited for linearizing pretrained softmax attention due to the rank-1 orthogonal projection structure. With targeted structural interventions, the remaining approximation gap can be substantially closed, enabling competitive long-context performance at scale without retraining.

Abstract: The quadratic cost of causal self-attention severely bottlenecks long-context transformer inference. While numerous post hoc linearization pipelines exist, it is difficult to identify which components preserve model quality. This work isolates the effect of state update design in a strict frozen-backbone regime. We show that softmax relies on key-dependent, rank-1 orthogonal projections, elucidating why delta-style networks outperform purely gated accumulation. We identify a potential source of approximation errors and introduce structural interventions, specifically sink tokens, short convolutions, and fixed-budget cache routing, which reduces the remaining gap. We scale this linearization approach across LLaMA and Qwen models up to 32B parameters, outperforming prior post hoc baselines on MMLU and matching the long-context retrieval of complex adaptive-caching frameworks.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [158] [Extending Xenakis: From Architectural Geometry to Sonification of the Philips Pavilion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06589)
*Changda Ma,Sunshiyu Wang,Canting Zhu,Alexandria Smith*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper inverts Xenakis' architectural-to-musical transformation by sonifying the Philips Pavilion's ruled surfaces into a temporal musical composition, generating glissandi, density-based energy blocks, and sparse wind subsequences from geometric data, implemented in Python with MIDI output and real-time 3D visualization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To revisit and invert Xenakis' one-directional transformation from music to architecture, exploring how architectural geometry (specifically ruled surfaces) can serve as a generative source for musical structure, thereby extending Xenakis' thinking into sonification and interactive music practice.

Method: The Pavilion is reconstructed as nine ruled surfaces; governing ruling lines are extracted; surfaces subdivided into structural lines and spatial sampling points. Four evenly spaced ruling lines per surface generate continuous string glissandi; 3357 sampled points produce five density-based energy blocks and a sparse brass/woodwind subsequence. Implemented in Python with MIDI rendered in Ableton Live and real-time 3D visualization.

Result: A system that produces a temporal musical composition (MIDI) from the geometric data of the Philips Pavilion, accompanied by a real-time 3D visualization that reveals architectural motion, stasis, and structural contrast through synchronized sound and image.

Conclusion: This work paves the way for transferring architectural geometry into performable musical structures, extending Xenakis' legacy into sonification and interactive music practice.

Abstract: Architecture and music have been linked through proportion and temporal structure, yet architectural geometry is rarely viewed as a source of generative music. Revisiting Xenakis' one-directional transformation from string glissandi in Metastaseis to the ruled surfaces of the Philips Pavilion, we invert this workflow and sonify the completed Pavilion as a temporal composition. We reconstruct the Pavilion as nine ruled surfaces, extract their governing ruling lines, and subdivide each surface into structural lines and spatial sampling points. Four evenly spaced ruling lines per surface generate continuous string glissandi, while 3357 sampled points develop five density-based energy blocks and a sparse brass and woodwind subsequence. Implemented in Python, the system produces MIDI rendered in Ableton Live, accompanied by a real-time 3D visualization that reveals architectural motion, stasis, and structural contrast through sound and image. In general, this work paves the way for the transfer of architectural geometry as a performable musical structure, extending Xenakis's architectural and musical thinking to sonification and interactive music practice.

</details>


### [159] [MMGenre: Benchmarking Singing Voice Synthesis across Multiple Musical Genres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06986)
*Wenhao Feng,Yuxun Tang,Jiatong Shi,Qin Jin*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MMGenre is a benchmark for multi-genre singing voice synthesis evaluation spanning 10 genres and 26 subgenres, revealing that existing SVS models show limited genre discrimination with similar acoustic characteristics across genres. Lightweight genre-specific continued training substantially improves performance over zero-shot adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SVS benchmarks are heavily biased toward pop music, limiting systematic analysis of how models generalize across diverse musical genres. The authors aim to address this gap by introducing a standardized multi-genre evaluation framework.

Method: The authors introduce MMGenre, a benchmark spanning 10 major genres and 26 subgenres, supported by an automatic pipeline for constructing genre-aligned music scores. They evaluate representative SVS models and compare zero-shot genre adaptation versus lightweight genre-specific continued training.

Result: Representative SVS models exhibit limited genre discrimination, with synthesized vocals across genres showing highly similar acoustic characteristics and weak separability. Zero-shot genre adaptation yields only marginal improvements, while lightweight genre-specific continued training leads to substantial gains.

Conclusion: MMGenre provides a standardized framework for multi-genre SVS evaluation and exposes critical challenges in achieving genre-aware singing voice synthesis, highlighting the need for better genre-specific modeling.

Abstract: Singing voice synthesis (SVS) has progressed rapidly, yet its ability to generalize across diverse musical genres remains underexplored. Existing benchmarks are heavily biased toward pop music, limiting systematic analysis of genre-dependent behavior. We introduce MMGenre, a benchmark for multi-genre SVS diagnosis, supported by an automatic pipeline for constructing genre-aligned music scores. MMGenre spans 10 major genres and 26 subgenres, enabling comprehensive analysis of genre-aware synthesis. Extensive evaluation of representative SVS models reveals limited genre discrimination: synthesized vocals across genres exhibit highly similar acoustic characteristics and weak separability. While zero-shot genre adaptation yields only marginal improvements, lightweight genre-specific continued training leads to substantial gains. MMGenre provides a standardized framework for multi-genre SVS evaluation and exposes critical challenges in achieving genre-aware singing voice synthesis.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [160] [Mining Workflow Graphs for Black-Box Boundary Testing of Conversational LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06873)
*Liting Lin,Boxi Yu,Yuzhong Zhang,Lionel Briand,David-Paul Niland,Emir Muñoz*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: AgentEval is a black-box testing framework that discovers and tests state-dependent failures in conversational LLM agents by mining a conversational workflow graph to identify hidden boundaries (e.g., identity checks, confirmation gates) and generating targeted tests through replay and perturbation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational LLM agents can cause real-world harm when internal workflows fail (e.g., completing transactions without confirmation). Testing these state-dependent failures is difficult because critical boundaries are hidden behind multi-turn conversational prerequisites, making them inaccessible to standard tests.

Method: AgentEval is a black-box framework that: (1) interacts with an agent to mine a conversational workflow graph modeling its behavior; (2) uses the graph's structure to enumerate guards and prerequisites as test targets; (3) replays the conversational path to a boundary; (4) applies a perturbation; (5) determines pass/fail using only conversation turns.

Result: On four τ^3-bench agents, AgentEval generated tests covering 23-38 distinct boundaries per agent. Ablation studies show the graph's structure yields 23 distinct boundaries versus 12 with a prompt-only baseline, at lower duplicate and false-alarm rates.

Conclusion: AgentEval effectively discovers stateful boundaries in conversational LLM agents using a black-box approach, outperforming prompt-only baselines by leveraging conversational workflow graph structure for targeted test generation.

Abstract: Conversational LLM agents can cause real-world harm when their internal workflows fail, such as completing a transaction without confirmation. Testing these state-dependent failures is difficult because critical boundaries, such as identity checks and confirmation gates, are hidden behind multi-turn conversational prerequisites, rendering them inaccessible to standard tests. We present AgentEval, a black-box testing framework that discovers and stresses these stateful boundaries. AgentEval interacts with an agent to mine a \emph{conversational workflow graph}, a model of its behavior. Instead of prompting blindly, AgentEval uses this graph's structure to enumerate specific guards and prerequisites as test targets, replaying the conversational path to a boundary before applying a perturbation. AgentEval then executes each test, determining whether it passes or fails using only the conversation turns. We benchmark AgentEval against a privileged, white-box auditor with access to the agent's underlying source code, which AgentEval never sees. On four $τ^3$-bench agents, AgentEval successfully generates tests covering $23$--$38$ distinct boundaries per agent; ablation studies attribute the gain to the graph's structure: $23$ distinct boundaries versus $12$ with a prompt-only baseline, at lower duplicate and false-alarm rates.

</details>


### [161] [Scaling Author Identity Disambiguation to the World of Code: A Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06920)
*Audris Mockus*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper describes a methodology to alias ~107M free-text author/committer identities from the World of Code collection into canonical persons at scale, addressing the over-merge problem through a combination of betweenness cuts, per-edge classifiers trained on 2.6M GitHub no-reply labels, and GitHub account assertions. The largest cluster shrinks from 170K to under 7K, gold recall rises from 0.44 to 0.70 at increasing precision, and the final map outperforms prior state-of-the-art global, privacy-preserving resolvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale the resolution of free-text author/committer identities across the entire World of Code collection (~107M distinct author strings, ~6B commits) by an order of magnitude beyond prior work, where the central challenge shifts from missed merges to over-merge (i.e., bridge identities like bots, role accounts, placeholder emails, and multi-author fields causing transitive welding into million-member mega-clusters).

Method: The approach combines: (1) node-level gates (information score, project spread, degree) to preserve recall; (2) per-value blocklists of high-quality-but-shared attributes; and (3) the working composition—a betweenness cut over the exact union graph plus a per-edge classifier trained on 2.6M labels mined from GitHub no-reply identifiers, augmented by GitHub's own account assertions. The paper reports the full experimental record (>20 experiments, including failures).

Result: The per-edge model transfers at AUC 0.99 against human-adjudicated pairs. End-to-end, the largest cluster falls from 170,431 (and a predecessor's 3.0M) to under 7,000. Gold recall rises from 0.44 to 0.70 at increasing precision. On an independent 21M-alias GitHub ground truth, the final map outperforms predecessors and the published state of the art among global, privacy-preserving resolvers.

Conclusion: Structural cuts do not transfer to unseen edge sets, and recall-only or precision-only benchmarks invert verdicts unless read together. The paper serves as a catalog of scale lessons for identity resolution at the billion-commit scale.

Abstract: We describe the methodology used to alias the free-text author/committer identities of the entire World of Code (WoC) collection (version V2604, ~107M distinct author strings over ~6B commits) into canonical persons, extending the fingerprint-based anti-aliasing of ALFAA and the 38M-identity resolution of Fry et al. by an order of magnitude. At this scale the central problem is over-merge, not missed merges: a few bridge identities (bots, role accounts, placeholder emails, multi-author commit fields) transitively weld unrelated clusters through the global union step into million-member "mega-clusters." We report the full experimental record (more than twenty experiments, including unsuccessful ones) behind the deployed design. Node-level gates (information score, project spread, degree) preserve recall but cannot dissolve the mega-cluster; per-value blocklists of high-quality-but-shared attributes are recall-safe but cannot break a redundant mesh; the working composition is a betweenness cut over the exact union graph plus a per-edge classifier trained on 2.6M labels mined from GitHub no-reply identifiers. That classifier, filtering dormant cross-project shingle groups and joined by GitHub's own account assertions, then recovers the recall the precision work had foregone. Against human-adjudicated pairs the per-edge model transfers at AUC 0.99; end to end the largest cluster falls from 170,431 (and a predecessor's 3.0M) to under 7,000 w hile gold recall rises from 0.44 to 0.70 at increasing precision, and on an independent 21M-alias GitHub ground truth the final map outscores its predecessors and the published state of the art among global, privacy-preserving resolvers. The record doubles as a catalog of scale lessons: structural cuts do not transfer to edge sets they never saw, and recall-only and precision-only benchmarks invert verdicts unless read together.

</details>


### [162] [Progressive Crystallization: Turning Agent Exploration into Deterministic, Lower-Cost Workflows in Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07052)
*Arun Malik*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Progressive crystallization is a lifecycle that converts validated AI agent behaviors from costly LLM-driven execution into cheaper deterministic workflows, achieving 45% deterministic execution, 70% cost reduction, and improved safety in a production cloud networking AIOps system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents for IT operations are permanent cost centers because every execution requires full LLM inference, even for solved problems.

Method: Defines a three-stage execution taxonomy (agent-orchestrated → hybrid → fully deterministic) with evidence-based promotion/demotion mechanisms that convert repeatedly validated agent behaviors into cheaper deterministic workflows.

Result: Increased deterministic execution from 0% to 45% over eight months, reduced per-incident agent costs by >70% despite doubling incident volume, and improved safety through greater reproducibility and auditability.

Conclusion: Progressive crystallization effectively transforms AI agents from permanent cost centers to discovery mechanisms, significantly reducing operational costs while improving reliability and safety in production AIOps systems.

Abstract: AI agents deployed for IT operations are typically permanent cost centers because every execution requires full LLM inference, even for previously solved problems. This paper introduces progressive crystallization, a lifecycle that treats agent exploration as a discovery mechanism rather than a permanent execution model. It defines a three-stage execution taxonomy, from fully agent-orchestrated to hybrid to fully deterministic workflows, together with an evidence-based promotion mechanism that converts repeatedly validated agent behaviors into cheaper and more reproducible deterministic workflows, while automatically demoting workflows that regress. Evaluated on a production cloud networking AIOps system processing tens of thousands of incidents per month, the approach increased deterministic execution from 0% to 45% over eight months, reduced per-incident agent costs by more than 70% despite doubling incident volume, and improved safety through greater reproducibility and auditability. The paper also presents the execution taxonomy, promotion and demotion criteria, trace extraction methodology, economic model, safety considerations, and discusses limitations and threats to validity.

</details>


### [163] [Evaluating Static and Process Evidence for Code Authorship in Programming Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07400)
*Marek Horváth*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study evaluates source-code authorship attribution in educational programming contexts, showing that process features (e.g., edit history) improve attribution over final code alone, but performance remains far below contest datasets, making these models suitable only as instructor-mediated decision support tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instructors need to interpret whether student submissions align with their prior programming profiles, especially when code similarity is inconclusive. Existing authorship methods are validated on contest/open-source data where strong author signals exist, but educational repositories—where students solve shared assignments and skills are developing—pose different challenges that are underexplored.

Method: The study uses task-aware evaluation to contrast production contexts (contest vs. educational). It tests whether repository-visible process features (e.g., editing patterns) add information beyond final code across six matched educational comparisons. Contest data (Google Kick Start) provides a high-signal baseline.

Result: Contest data achieves a mean top-1 accuracy of 0.938. Educational datasets show substantially lower attribution performance: adding process features raises educational mean from 0.094 to 0.233, and mean pairwise verification ROC-AUC from 0.556 to 0.752.

Conclusion: Measured authorship signal depends heavily on production context. Process patterns can complement weak final-code signals in educational repositories, but the performance is insufficient for independent proof of authorship; models should only be used as instructor-mediated decision support.

Abstract: In programming courses, instructors may need to interpret whether a submission is consistent with a student's prior programming profile, especially when code similarity alone is inconclusive. Existing source-code authorship methods are often evaluated on programming-contest or open-source datasets, where reusable templates and local code patterns can produce strong author-related signal. Educational repositories present a different setting. Students solve shared assignments while their programming practices are still developing. This study uses task-aware evaluation to contrast these production contexts and tests whether repository-visible process features add information beyond final code in six matched educational comparisons. Contest data provide a high-signal contrast, with a Kick Start mean top-1 of 0.938. Educational datasets produce substantially lower attribution performance. Adding process features raises the educational mean from 0.094 to 0.233 and mean pairwise verification ROC-AUC from 0.556 to 0.752. The comparisons show that measured signal depends on production context and that process patterns can complement weak final-code signal in educational repositories. Such models are therefore appropriate only as instructor-mediated decision support, not as independent proof of authorship.

</details>


### [164] [Biased or Personalized? The Impact of Personal Information on AI-driven Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07480)
*Erfan Entezami,Madeline Endres*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates how inferred developer attributes (age, gender) can bias AI-generated code beyond task requirements, finding through controlled experiments on 800 websites and an observational study with 20 participants that personalization significantly influences interface design, template content, and code structure, revealing a tension between personalization and fairness in AI-assisted programming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work shows generative AI can produce biased software artifacts, little is known about how developer identity (inferred attributes) can bias generated code. The authors aim to characterize this underexplored tension between personalization and fairness.

Method: Mixed-methods approach: (1) Controlled experiments on 800 AI-generated websites examining age- and gender-related signal effects across three dimensions (interface design, template content, code structure); (2) Observational study with follow-up interviews of 20 participants who used AI to create personal websites.

Result: Age- and gender-related signals produce statistically significant differences in interface design, template content, and code structure. The observational study and interviews confirm that personalization impacts software artifacts in practice and reveal how programmers perceive the boundary between personalization and bias.

Conclusion: Developer attributes can meaningfully influence generated software beyond stated requirements, highlighting a previously underexplored tension between personalization and fairness in AI-assisted programming.

Abstract: Generative AI is increasingly permeating software engineering, enabling developers to generate functions, files, and even entire applications from natural language specifications. AI systems are also becoming more personalized, adapting outputs based on inferred user characteristics and interaction history. While personalization may improve the development experience, it raises concerns that generated software could be shaped by attributes of the developer rather than by task requirements alone. Prior work has shown that generative AI can produce biased software artifacts, but little is known about how developer identity can bias generated code. We characterize three dimensions through which inferred developer attributes can influence generated artifacts: interface design, template content, and code structure. First, through controlled experiments on 800 AI-generated websites, we find that age- and gender-related signals produce significant differences across all three dimensions. Second, we conduct an observational study and follow-up interviews with 20 participants who used AI to create a personal website to both examine how personalization impacts software artifacts in practice, and also to understand how programmers perceive the boundary between personalization and bias. Together, our results show that developer attributes can meaningfully influence generated software beyond stated requirements, highlighting a previously underexplored tension between personalization and fairness in AI-assisted programming.

</details>


### [165] [The Poisoned Chalice of LLM Evaluation Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07481)
*Jonathan Katzy,Ali Al-Kaswan,Razvan Mihai Popescu,Zhou Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the first Poisoned Chalice of LLM Evaluation Competition, which frames data contamination detection as a white-box membership inference task on source code to determine if LLMs have seen benchmark instances during pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data contamination in LLM evaluation for software engineering tasks is prevalent, yet training corpora are often undisclosed. There is a need for practical methods to detect prior exposure to training data without full corpus access.

Method: Organized a competition (co-located with FSE-AIWare 2026) that frames contamination detection as a white-box membership inference task on source code, providing curated datasets, target models, baseline attacks, and held-out evaluation.

Result: Reports the setup and results of the competition; the design encourages methods that generalize beyond superficial dataset artifacts and single training settings.

Conclusion: The competition aims to catalyze the community toward trustworthy LLM evaluation for software engineering by enabling practical contamination detection without full training corpus access.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to evaluate and support software engineering tasks, yet the validity of these evaluations is often undermined by uncertainty about whether benchmark instances were seen during pretraining. This can lead to data contamination, which may inflate performance and result in misleading conclusions about model capability. Despite this, the training corpora of many modern models are only partially disclosed, making direct decontamination infeasible. This creates a need for practical methods that can detect a large language models' prior exposure to training data without access to the full training corpus.
  To address this challenge, we organize the first Poisoned Chalice of LLM Evaluation Competition, co-located with the FSE-AIWare 2026 Competition Track. The competition frames contamination detection as a white-box membership inference task on source code and provides participants with curated datasets, target models, baseline attacks, and a final evaluation on a held-out model and dataset. This design encourages methods that generalize beyond superficial dataset artifacts and beyond a single training setting.
  This paper reports the setup and results of the competition. More broadly, the competition aims to catalyze the community around trustworthy LLM evaluation for software engineering.

</details>


### [166] [Holistic B2X Mobile Application Development -- A Reference Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07511)
*Oliver Werth,Nadine Guhr,Michael H. Breitner*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper reviews mobile app development process models and conducts 28 expert interviews to bridge the research-practice gap, ultimately proposing a reference model for B2X mobile app development that synthesizes mobile-specific characteristics and challenges for management decision support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge about mobile app development models' usage remains restricted despite many published process models for B2X app development, creating a research-practice knowledge divide that needs examination.

Method: First reviewed available process models for mobile app development, then conducted 28 expert interviews with various stakeholders involved in typical B2X mobile app development processes.

Result: Hybrid process models are often advantageous for B2X mobile app development, with technical backgrounds and communication processes being crucial factors. No process model can be used unadopted.

Conclusion: The study theorizes mobile-specific characteristics and challenges into a reference model valuable for management decision support, creating avenues for better theorizing toward successful B2X mobile app development.

Abstract: Business-to-X (B2X, "X" for business, customer, etc.) mobile applications ("apps") show various mobile-specific chances and challenges that must be ad-dressed in the whole development process. Knowledge about mobile app devel-opment models' usage remains restricted, although various process models for B2X app development have been published already. This article first reviewed available process models for mobile app development. In addition, 28 expert in-terviews with various stakeholders involved in typical B2X mobile app develop-ment processes were conducted to examine this research-practice knowledge di-vide. Since hybrid process models are often advantageous for B2X mobile app development, technical backgrounds or communication processes, are also cru-cial. Since no process model can be used unadopted, this study theorizes mobile-specific characteristics and challenges of app development process models to a reference model generally valuable for management decision support. The find-ings create avenues for better theorizing toward successful B2X mobile app de-velopment.

</details>


### [167] [What Makes a Good Bug Report for an AI Agent?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07593)
*Lara Khatib,Noble Saji Mathews,Meiyappan Nagappan,Pengyu Nie,Thomas Zimmermann*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates what makes a bug report effective for LLM-based automated program repair (APR) agents, finding that concrete, executable, and well-localized information (fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, source code, localization info) correlates with higher repair success, while longer reports and natural-language-heavy descriptions are associated with lower success. Two models (Qwen and Gemma) diverge in handling missing information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: APR agents are transitioning from research to developer workflows but still use bug reports written for humans. It is unknown whether traditional bug-report features (e.g., steps to reproduce, stack traces) that help human developers also benefit LLM-based agents.

Method: Two analyses: (1) Statistical modeling of 27 bug-report features across 433 SWE-bench Verified issues attempted by 87 repair agents. (2) Controlled ablations on SWE-bench Pro with 2 models (Qwen, Gemma) and 17 problem-statement mutations, varying information content, localization cues, and structural formatting.

Result: Fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, source code, and localization info are associated with higher resolution likelihood; longer reports reduce success. Both models depend on localization cues and expected behavior. Structural changes (flattening lists, removing headers) reduce solve rates even without content removal. Qwen searches broadly and exhausts budget; Gemma commits early and patches.

Conclusion: A good bug report for agents overlaps with but differs from one for humans: agents need concrete, executable, well-localized information; natural language steps to reproduce and readability features contribute little or correlate with lower success.

Abstract: Automated program repair (APR) agents are transitioning from research benchmarks to developer workflows, yet they still begin with bug reports written for human developers. While decades of research have established what makes a good bug report for humans (e.g., steps to reproduce, stack traces), it remains unclear whether these features transfer to LLM-based agents. We study this question in two analyses. First, we use statistical modeling to examine associations between 27 bug-report features and repair success across 433 SWE-bench Verified issues attempted by 87 repair agents. We find that fix suggestions, reproduction scripts, repository source code, and localization info are associated with higher resolution likelihood, while longer reports are associated with lower odds. Second, we conduct controlled ablations across 2 models and 17 problem-statement mutations on SWE-bench Pro, varying the information available to an agent while holding the underlying task fixed. We remove or isolate selected bug-report content, delete fault-localization cues, and test structural changes that flatten lists or remove section headers. We find that both models depend on localization cues and expected behavior, and that structural changes alone can reduce solve rates, even without removing any content. The two models diverge in how they handle missing information: Qwen searches more widely and can exhaust its turn budget, while Gemma commits to a plausible interpretation early and patches on it. Our findings indicate that a good bug report for an agent overlaps with, but is not identical to, a good report for a human: agents benefit most from concrete, executable, and well-localized information, whereas some qualities long emphasized for human readers, such as natural language steps to reproduce and readable descriptions, contribute little or even correlate with lower success.

</details>


### [168] [Rethinking Code Performance Benchmarks for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07619)
*Nhat Minh Le,Yisen Xu,Zhijie Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Revisiting four LLM code generation benchmarks reveals that only 6.11% of performant implementations are statistically faster than canonical solutions due to insufficient performance tests. The authors propose an LLM-based multi-agent framework for generating performance-oriented tests that expose runtime differences more effectively (24-25% significant improvements vs. original near-0%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing function-level performance benchmarks fail to reliably distinguish between canonical and LLM-generated performant implementations under rigorous statistical testing. The authors identify that benchmark-provided performance tests are insufficient to expose actual runtime differences, motivating the need for better performance-oriented test generation.

Method: The authors (1) re-evaluate 1,538 tasks across four benchmarks with 30 runs each using statistical testing; (2) manually analyze 308 non-significant tasks to classify performance improvements vs. unchanged implementations; (3) propose an LLM-based multi-agent framework with three agents (generator, diagnoser, repairer) to generate deterministic performance-oriented tests that preserve functional correctness while exposing runtime differences.

Result: Only 6.11% of performant implementations are significantly faster with original tests. Manual analysis shows 99/308 contain no meaningful change, 209/308 have potential improvements masked by insufficient tests. The proposed framework with DeepSeek-v3.1 and GPT-4o reveals significant improvements in 24.01% and 25.43% of 1,345 tasks, outperforming SOTA methods.

Conclusion: The main limitation is not just evaluation methodology but also the insuffiency of benchmark-provided performance tests. The proposed multi-agent framework significantly improves the ability to expose performance differences, suggesting that better test generation is crucial for meaningful evaluation of LLM code generation efficiency.

Abstract: Many function-level performance benchmarks have been proposed to evaluate whether large language models (LLMs) can generate efficient programs. However, results on these benchmarks often show that LLM-generated implementations have little or no execution-time difference from canonical solutions. In this paper, we revisit four popular benchmarks: EffiBench, Enamel, EvalPerf, and Mercury. We evaluate 1,538 tasks under more rigorous setting by running each task 30 times and assessing the runtime differences between the canonical solutions and benchmark-provided performant implementations with statistical testing. With the benchmark-provided test suites, only 6.11% of the performant implementations are significantly faster than the canonical solutions. In a manual analysis of 308 non-significant tasks, 99 performant implementations contain no meaningful performance change, while 209 contain potential performance improvements that are not exposed by the original tests. These results suggest that the main limitation is not only the evaluation method, but also the limited sufficiency of the benchmark-provided performance tests. To address this limitation, we propose an LLM-based multi-agent framework to generate performance-oriented tests that expose runtime differences more effectively than the original tests. The framework uses three separate agents to generate, diagnose, and repair deterministic tests that preserve functional correctness while better exposing performance differences. Across 1,345 benchmark tasks for which the original tests found no significant performance difference, tests generated by our framework with DeepSeek-v3.1 and GPT-4o reveal statistically significant improvements in 24.01% and 25.43% of the tasks, respectively, outperforming the SOTA LLM-based performance test generation method.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [169] [Cost-Effective Agent Harnesses for Abstract Reasoning and Generalization on ARC-AGI-1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06764)
*Kabir Moghe,Peter Chin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An open-weight model (DeepSeek V3.2) with no ARC-specific fine-tuning, using agentic architectures (Explorer-Definer Pipeline and Reflective Orchestrator), achieves 57.50-67.25% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-1 at low cost ($0.25-$0.62 per task), lifting a 15.50% baseline by ~52 points. Analysis shows the pipeline is generation-bound, and the orchestrator's adaptive re-exploration confirms this diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a third regime—beyond heavy test-time compute over frontier models or benchmark-specific fine-tuning—for solving ARC-AGI-1, using an open-weight model in non-thinking mode under strict budget without ARC-specific training.

Method: (1) Explorer-Definer Pipeline: two-stage agent pipeline separating pattern discovery from executable transformation synthesis. (2) Reflective Orchestrator: augments the pipeline with autonomous exploration of new transformations when prior hypotheses fail on training pairs. Uses DeepSeek V3.2 in non-thinking mode with no ARC-specific fine-tuning.

Result: Explorer-Definer Pipeline: 57.50% pass@2 at $0.25/task. Reflective Orchestrator: 67.25% pass@2 at $0.62/task. Combined lift of ~52 points over 15.50% one-shot baseline. Unbiased pass@k analysis shows pipeline is generation-bound; selection via training-pair accuracy captures ~95% of candidate ceiling. Orchestrator adaptive re-exploration confirms prediction with unbiased pass@1 lift +9.81 pp. Think tool ablation reduces pass@2 by 5.75 pp.

Conclusion: Significant ARC-AGI-1 progress is achievable through architecture alone with an open-weight model, without benchmark-specific training or heavy compute. The pipeline is generation-bound, meaning future improvements require broader generation diversity rather than better ranking mechanisms, a prediction confirmed by the orchestrator's adaptive re-exploration strategy.

Abstract: Recent progress on ARC-AGI-1 from disclosed architectures has come broadly from two regimes: heavy test-time compute over frontier models (evolutionary search, exhaustive sampling, extended chain-of-thought), or benchmark-specific training in which small models are fine-tuned on ARC data, often with task-specialized architectures. We study a third regime: an open-weight model in non-thinking mode (DeepSeek V3.2) under a strict budget, with no ARC-specific fine-tuning. We study what is recoverable through architecture alone, building agentic harnesses that decompose pattern-discovery and program-synthesis stages explicitly. First, we introduce an Explorer-Definer Pipeline that separates pattern discovery from executable transformation synthesis, implemented as a two-stage agent pipeline. Next, we present the Reflective Orchestrator, which augments the pipeline with autonomous exploration of new transformations when previous hypotheses fail on training pairs. On the ARC-AGI-1 public 400-task evaluation set, the pipeline reaches 57.50% pass@2 at \$0.25 per task, and the orchestrator reaches 67.25% pass@2 at \$0.62 per task. Together these architectures lift a 15.50% one-shot baseline by ~52 points without benchmark-specific training or heavy test-time compute. Furthermore, the orchestrator-driven lift tests a falsifiable diagnostic the pipeline produces; unbiased pass@k analysis suggests the pipeline is generation-bound, not selection-bound (selection via training-pair accuracy captures ~95% of the candidate ceiling) and predicts that significant improvement requires broader generation, not better ranking. The orchestrator implements this prediction via adaptive re-exploration and confirms it (unbiased pass@1 lift +9.81 pp, matching selection-mediated pass@2 lift). An additional pipeline ablation identifies its think tool as a significant component, with removal reducing pass@2 by 5.75 pp.

</details>


### [170] [Evaluating SageMath-Augmented LLM Agents for Computational and Experimental Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06820)
*Pavel Snopov,German Magai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a ReAct-style agentic setup combining LLM reasoning with SageMath (a CAS) and up-to-date documentation (Context7) for solving research-level math problems. It introduces refinements to the RealMath benchmark (multi-step post-processing, multi-stage validation) and shows CAS access yields substantial performance gains (+9.7pp on average, up to 27.8pp), narrowing the gap between open-weight and closed models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior AI-for-Math work focused on autoformalization and theorem proving, the role of Computer Algebra Systems (CAS) in LLM agentic workflows remains underexplored. The paper aims to address this gap by investigating how CAS-augmented agents can assist mathematicians in computational exploration.

Method: A ReAct-style agentic setup is proposed that combines LLM reasoning with verifiable feedback from SageMath and Context7 for up-to-date documentation. The RealMath benchmark is refined with multi-step post-processing and multi-stage validation to improve problem set quality. The setup is evaluated across frontier models in a computational-mathematics research loop setting.

Result: SageMath access yields substantial performance gains across all evaluated models: +9.7pp on average (range 1.5pp–27.8pp), narrowing the open-weight vs. closed model gap. Qwen 3.7-Max benefits most from SageMath; GPT-5.5 achieves the highest solve rate (75.2%) with lowest token usage among tool-enabled configurations.

Conclusion: CAS-augmented agents represent a promising direction for assisting mathematicians in computational exploration, and this work is a step towards automated conjecture discovery.

Abstract: Recent advances in AI for Mathematics have focused largely on autoformalization and theorem proving, leaving the role of Computer Algebra Systems (CAS) in agentic LLM workflows underexplored. We propose a ReAct-style agentic setup that combines LLM reasoning with verifiable feedback from SageMath, together with Context7 for the up-to-date documentation. We evaluate this agentic setup across frontier models for solving research-level mathematical problems from the RealMath benchmark in a setting that emulates a computational-mathematics research loop. We also propose a refinement to the RealMath benchmark by introducing a multi-step post-processing procedure and a multi-stage validation pipeline, both of which improve the quality and reliability of the extracted problem set. Our experiments reveal substantial performance gains from SageMath access across all evaluated models on +9.7~pp on average, the gains range from 1.5~pp to 27.8~pp and narrow the gap between open-weight and closed models. Qwen~3.7-Max benefits from SageMath the most, while GPT-5.5 achieves the highest solve rate of $75.2\%$ and the lowest token usage among tool-enabled configurations. Our findings suggest that CAS-augmented agents represent a promising direction for assisting mathematicians in computational exploration, and we believe that this work is a step towards automated conjecture discovery. The project repository is available online.

</details>


### [171] [The Harness Effect: How Orchestration Design Sets the Token Economics of Enterprise Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06906)
*Muayad Sayed Ali,Aliaksandra Novik,Anji Boddupally,Artem Yavorskyi,Chris Nickerson,Daniel Rica,Emily DuGranrut,Felix Leung,Garrett Prince,Grace Barnett,Heath Robinson,Hosain Al Ahmad,Jesse Resnick,Juan Carlos Farah,Jyothi Swaroop Meruga,Leonid Kuznetsov,Luke Gorham,Marie Schmoll,Michael Paciullo,Saumya Das,Sharath Sheripally,Tommy Griscom,Mykyta Osadchyi,Neha Mantri,Nick Westrum,Olivia Benowitz,Parikshith Kulkarni,Radik Chernyshov,Rakshith Vasudev,Rohith Nadimpally,Vikas Gangadevi,Waseem AlShikh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Token maxing (buying capability with tokens) is the dominant inefficiency in agentic AI; the orchestration harness layer cuts cost per task 41%, wall-clock time 44%, and tokens per task 38% across six foundation models while maintaining quality parity, with efficiency gains model-invariant and quality gains correlated with model baseline strength (r=0.99).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic AI development suffers from "token maxing" — using increasingly more tokens (reasoning traces, turns, tool payloads, replayed contexts) to gain capability, where tokens per task grow faster than task value. Falling per-token prices mask this pattern but total spend still rises, motivating the need to identify the decisive efficiency lever.

Method: Controlled swap experiment: 22 locked evaluation tasks, six foundation models (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3.1, Gemini Flash 3.5, Qwen 3.6, GLM 5.1, Palmyra X6), changing only the orchestration layer — a frozen conventional production loop vs. the Writer Agent Harness. Measures cost, wall-clock time, tokens per task, and task-completion quality.

Result: The harness cuts blended cost per task 41% ($0.21→$0.12), median wall-clock 44% (48s→27s), tokens per task 38% (14.2k→8.8k), with quality at parity (0.78→0.81, directional). Every model gets cheaper (33-61%). Quality gains correlate near-perfectly with baseline model strength (r=0.99). Quality per dollar rises 82%; task-completions per million tokens rise from 54.9 to 92.0. The orchestration layer moved cost per task more than the full spread of the model menu.

Conclusion: The harness is the one component whose efficiency multiplies across every model an organization runs (present and future). The paper formalizes token economics at the orchestration layer, details six mechanism families (cache-shape discipline to failure-spend governance), and compares six widely used agent systems on the same axes.

Abstract: Agentic AI development today runs on token maxing: buying capability with tokens -- longer reasoning traces, more turns, wider tool payloads, bigger replayed contexts -- so tokens per task grow faster than task value. Falling per-token prices mask the pattern; total spend rises anyway. We argue the decisive lever against token maxing is the harness: the orchestration layer that assembles context, exposes tools, sequences turns, delegates work, and carries enterprise observability and governance. We isolate it with a controlled swap: 22 locked evaluation tasks, six foundation models (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3.1, Gemini Flash 3.5, Qwen 3.6, GLM 5.1, Palmyra X6), changing only the orchestration layer -- a frozen conventional production loop versus the Writer Agent Harness. Holding models constant, the harness cuts blended cost per task 41% ($0.21->$0.12), median wall-clock 44% (48s->27s), and tokens per task 38% (14.2k->8.8k), with task-completion quality at parity (0.78->0.81, directional at this sample size). Efficiency is model-invariant -- every model gets cheaper (33-61%) -- while quality gains are capability-dependent: a model's gain correlates almost perfectly with its baseline strength (r=0.99, n=6), a phenomenon we term harness leverage. Quality per dollar rises 82%; task-completions per million tokens rise from 54.9 to 92.0. On this workload the orchestration layer moved cost per task more than the full spread of the model menu did. We formalize token economics at the orchestration layer (including effective input price under prompt caching), detail the six mechanism families behind the effect -- cache-shape discipline to failure-spend governance -- compare six widely used agent systems on the same axes, and argue the harness is the one component whose efficiency multiplies across every model an organization runs -- present and future.

</details>


### [172] [Grounding Spatial Relations in a Compact World Model: Instruction Leakage and a Goal-Free Dynamics Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06925)
*Yufeng Wang,Lu Wei,Haibin Ling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Goal-conditioned world models can achieve high relation-readout accuracy through "instruction transcription" rather than genuine perception—the model simply copies the answer from the instruction. This leakage occurs when the scored quantity is directly transcribable from the instruction, and is detected by withholding or counterfactually altering the goal. The fix is to remove the goal from the dynamics and supervise the read path separately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether goal-conditioned world models that use language goals truly ground spatial relations (e.g., "put the red block left of the blue block") through perception of the scene, or whether they exploit a spurious shortcut by transcribing the answer directly from the instruction.

Method: The authors design a detection protocol: (1) withhold the goal to see if accuracy collapses, (2) supply a counterfactual instruction to see if predictions follow the false instruction. They test across tabletop settings, BabyAI benchmark, and Language-Table world models, with within-task ablations.

Result: A model achieving 0.90 relation-readout accuracy collapses to 0.27 when the goal is withheld. With counterfactual instructions, predicted anchors follow the false instruction 94.5% of the time vs. 2.3% for the true scene. BabyAI and the tabletop setting leak; Language-Table does not until the instruction names the direction. Degrading actions never increases leakage.

Conclusion: Instruction leakage is a confound when the scored quantity is transcribable from the instruction. The fix is to keep the goal out of the dynamics (assign it to the planner's cost) and supervise the read path, recovering genuine grounding (0.88 accuracy, identical with and without the goal).

Abstract: Compact world models that condition on a language goal promise to ground relations such as ``put the red block left of the blue block'' using a sparse set of explicit \emph{reference anchors}. We ask when such references actually ground a relation, and identify a trap: a goal-conditioned predictor reaches a striking $0.90$ relation-readout accuracy, yet this is \emph{instruction transcription}, not perception. Withholding the goal collapses it to chance ($0.90\!\to\!0.27$, three seeds) and a counterfactual instruction makes the predicted anchors follow the \emph{false} instruction $94.5\%$ of the time (true scene $2.3\%$; $N{=}256$). Tested across three settings and a within-task ablation, our central claim characterizes the confound: \textbf{instruction leakage occurs when the scored quantity is transcribable from the instruction (when the instruction names the answer) and is essentially independent of how predictive the non-instruction inputs are.} Our tabletop and the external BabyAI benchmark leak, whereas a Language-Table forward-dynamics world model whose instruction names \emph{referents} does not, until the instruction is augmented to name the direction; and degrading the action never increases leakage, the opposite of what predictor-competition predicts. The diagnosis prescribes the fix: keep the goal out of the dynamics (it belongs to the planner's cost) and supervise the \emph{read} path, recovering genuine, instruction-independent grounding ($0.88$, identical with and without the goal). The detection protocol and remedy apply to any goal-conditioned world model whose instruction names the scored quantity.

</details>


### [173] [Learning social norms enhances compatibility in dynamic human-AI coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07021)
*Yi Yang,Siyuan Liu,Xin Gao,Huamu Sun,Chao Liu,Qing Zhou,Bingbing Nie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes that AI agents fail to coordinate with humans because they don't explicitly quantify underlying social norms. Using pedestrian-vehicle interactions as a testbed, the authors identify three principles (outcome predictability, value alignment, advantage awareness) from 3,456 human interactions. Incorporating these principles into LLM agents improves coordination nearly fourfold over baselines and 43% beyond human-human interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
