Motivation: Existing AI agents, including LLMs, fail to coordinate with humans in dynamic interactions because current alignment approaches mimic human demonstrations without explicitly quantifying the underlying social norms that generate such behaviors.

Method: The authors used pedestrian-vehicle interaction as a representative dynamic interaction, built a simplified experimental platform, collected 3,456 dynamic human interactions, and identified three principles underlying human social norms: outcome predictability, value alignment, and advantage awareness. These principles were then incorporated into LLM-based AI agents for closed-loop interaction testing.

Result: The social-norm-informed LLM achieved a nearly fourfold higher total score than the baseline strategy and outperformed human-human interactions by 43% in closed-loop interaction tasks.

Conclusion: Formalizing tacit social norms into explicit, quantifiable principles enables AI agents to achieve mutually beneficial coordination in dynamic interactions, supporting their more natural integration into human society.

Abstract: Humans continuously coordinate with others in dynamic interactions, often through implicit, hard-to-quantify social norms that act as shared tacit expectations among interacting agents. As AI agents, including large language models (LLMs), become embedded in daily life, they increasingly participate in such interactions and reshape social interaction structures. Yet they often fail to coordinate with humans in an effective, considerate, and natural manner. We hypothesize that this gap arises because existing approaches align model behavior with human demonstrations without explicitly quantifying the underlying norms that generate such behavior. We selected pedestrian-vehicle interaction as a representative dynamic interaction and developed a simplified experimental platform that captures its key interactive features. From 3,456 dynamic human interactions collected via this platform, we identified three principles underlying human social norms: outcome predictability, value alignment, and advantage awareness. Incorporating these principles into AI agents significantly improves human-AI coordination. In the closed-loop interaction task with humans, the social-norm-informed LLM achieved a nearly fourfold higher total score than the baseline strategy and outperformed human-human interactions by 43%. These findings indicate that formalizing tacit social norms into explicit, quantifiable principles can enable AI agents to achieve mutually beneficial coordination in dynamic interactions, supporting their more natural integration into human society.

</details>


### [174] [Measuring Intelligence Beyond Human Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07040)
*Jerry Han,Rafael Moschopoulos,Ella Colby,Vishrut Goyal,Andrew Tu,Kia Ghods,Mark Braverman,Elad Hazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new paradigm for measuring intelligence beyond human capability using relative measurement, where models generate public challenges to separate other systems, creating an adversarial psychometric rating system that scales with capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-authored benchmarks saturate and above human capability, examiners cannot reliably identify tasks that are both hard and verifiable, making absolute-scale evaluation inherently difficult for measuring superhuman intelligence.

Method: The method involves a relative measurement paradigm where models generate public challenges that separate other systems, aggregating outcomes into an adversarial psychometric rating system. Practical protocols reduce incentives for private-information attacks, support judge-free adjudication, and scale naturally with agent capabilities.

Result: The framework is instantiated across both verifiable and open-ended, non-verifiable domains, demonstrating how model-generated evaluation can continue to measure systems beyond the human frontier.

Conclusion: The proposed adversarial psychometric rating system offers a scalable solution for measuring intelligence beyond human capability, overcoming the inherent limitations of absolute-scale evaluation and human-authored benchmarks.

Abstract: How can we measure intelligence beyond human capability?
  Human-authored benchmarks saturate, and above human capability, examiners may not know which tasks are both hard and verifiable. We argue that this difficulty is inherent to absolute-scale evaluation and propose a new paradigm based on relative measurement in which models generate public challenges that separate other systems. Aggregating these outcomes yields an adversarial psychometric rating system that can scale with the systems being measured. We describe practical protocols that reduce incentives for private-information attacks, support judge-free adjudication, and naturally scale with agent capabilities. We instantiate the framework across verifiable and open-ended, non-verifiable domains, illustrating how model-generated evaluation can continue to measure systems beyond the human frontier.

</details>


### [175] [Operational Reframing and Approval-Framed Delegation in Multi-Agent LLM Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07097)
*Lifei Liu,Haoran Yu,Xiaochong Jiang,Su Wang,Pin Qian,Yihang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that comparing a direct prompt with a planner-executor pipeline conflates three distinct mechanisms (operational reframing, planner behavior, and delegation framing). Using a five-condition controlled contrast design across 30 synthetic scenarios and benchmarks, they show that aggregate pipeline safety is not stable: operational reframing is the most portable risk signal, planner refusal can offset it but executor compliance may increase under executable steps, and approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design and model pairing. Raw-direct rankings mispredict deployed behavior (e.g., Gemini safest raw but amplifies with Claude planner).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety evaluations of multi-agent LLM systems often compare a direct prompt with a planner-executor pipeline and report the aggregate "pipeline effect," which conflates three distinct mechanisms and is thus difficult to interpret.

Method: A five-condition controlled contrast design is introduced, evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios and an exploratory external validation set from four agent-safety benchmarks using LLM-judged compliance.

Result: Aggregate pipeline safety is not a stable architectural property. Operational reframing is the most portable risk signal. Planner behavior can offset risk mainly through refusal, but when executable steps are produced, executors become more compliant. Approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design, model pairing, and scenario source. Raw-direct rankings mispredict deployed behavior.

Conclusion: Multi-agent safety evaluations should report reframing, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model pairing separately before attributing failures to architecture itself.

Abstract: Safety evaluations of multi-agent LLM systems often compare a direct prompt with a planner-executor pipeline and report the difference as a single "pipeline effect." We argue that this aggregate is difficult to interpret because it conflates three mechanisms: harmful intent may be reframed as plausible operational work, the planner may refuse or transform the request, and the executor may act under delegation prompts implying prior approval. To separate these factors, we introduce a five-condition controlled contrast design, evaluated on 30 synthetic harmful scenarios and an exploratory external validation set from four agent-safety benchmarks using LLM-judged compliance.
  Our results show that aggregate pipeline safety is not a stable architectural property. Operational reframing is the most portable risk signal, increasing compliance for GPT, Gemini, and DeepSeek across both scenario sets, while Claude is comparatively resistant. Planner behavior can offset this risk mainly through refusal; however, when the planner produces executable steps, the executor may become more compliant than under the direct operational baseline. Approval-framed delegation is sensitive to prompt design, model pairing, and scenario source, and a skeptical executor prompt sharply reduces compliance.
  Raw-direct model rankings can also mispredict deployed planner-executor behavior. Gemini is safest under raw direct prompts in the primary set yet shows the largest amplification with a Claude planner, rising from 8.9 percent to 38.9 percent compliance. GPTs near-zero aggregate pipeline effect instead hides a reframing increase canceled by planner refusal. These findings suggest that multi-agent safety evaluations should report reframing, planner behavior, delegation framing, and model pairing separately before attributing failures to architecture itself.

</details>


### [176] [Does AI Understand Imaging? A Systematic Benchmark of Agentic AI for Computational Imaging Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07189)
*Ethan Chung,Chuanjun Zheng,Jasper Tan,Jingxi Li,Haopeng Zhang,Huaijin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ImagingBench is a benchmark of 20 computational imaging tasks evaluating vision-language models and agentic AI across five categories, finding that agentic models significantly underperform specialized methods, especially on physics-based tasks like lensless imaging and holography, revealing a gap between semantic visual competence and physically grounded imaging performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While VLMs and agentic AI perform well on semantic visual tasks, it is unclear whether they can handle the physics and inverse problems underlying computational imaging. The authors aim to measure this gap.

Method: The authors construct ImagingBench with 20 tasks across five categories (ray/wave optics, ISP, inverse reconstruction, computational sensing, calibration) and evaluate in three settings: Expert (fixed expert-guided inverse reconstruction), Planner (planner-guided inverse reconstruction), and Forward (forward-system simulation). They benchmark proprietary (Gemini, GPT) and open-source (Qwen) VLMs against task-specific non-agentic baselines.

Result: Agentic models are consistently weaker than specialized methods, especially on computational sensing (lensless imaging, event-based reconstruction, ToF, holography). Planner guidance offers only modest/inconsistent gains over fixed-prompt Expert. Outputs are visually plausible but have poor reference-based fidelity.

Conclusion: There is a substantial gap between semantic visual competence and physically grounded imaging performance. ImagingBench provides a unified testbed for measuring this gap and tracking progress in agentic AI for computational imaging.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) and agentic AI have shown strong performance on semantic visual tasks, but it remains unclear whether they can handle the physics and inverse problems that underlie computational imaging. We present ImagingBench, a benchmark of 20 computational imaging tasks spanning five categories: ray and wave optics, image signal processing, inverse reconstruction, computational sensing, and calibration. ImagingBench evaluates three complementary settings: Expert, fixed expert-guided inverse reconstruction; Planner, planner-guided inverse reconstruction; and Forward, forward-system simulation for consistency checking. We benchmark leading proprietary and open-source image-centric multimodal systems, including Gemini, GPT, and Qwen, and compare them with representative task-specific non-agentic baselines. Across tasks, agentic models remain consistently weaker than specialized methods, especially on computational sensing problems such as lensless imaging, event-based reconstruction, time-of-flight imaging, and holography. Planner guidance provides only modest and inconsistent gains over the fixed-prompt Expert baseline. Although the models often generate visually plausible outputs, their reference-based fidelity remains poor, revealing a substantial gap between semantic visual competence and physically grounded imaging performance. ImagingBench provides a unified testbed for measuring this gap and tracking progress in agentic AI for computational imaging.

</details>


### [177] [Reasoning Consistency Scanning: A Framework for Auditing Chain-of-Thought Validity in AI Safety Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07229)
*Silvia Santano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces reasoning consistency scanning, a method to detect whether a model's stated chain-of-thought reasoning is logically consistent with its final answer, as distinct from faithfulness. The authors formalize a six-subtype taxonomy of inconsistency, build a validated benchmark of 60 transcripts, implement a scanner for InspectScout, and show that reasoning inconsistency is present, detectable, and varies across models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows chain-of-thought reasoning is often unfaithful, but detecting unfaithfulness requires controlled interventions that cannot be applied post-hoc. The authors shift focus to a more tractable question—reasoning consistency—which can be assessed from a transcript alone without intervention.

Method: The authors formalize reasoning consistency as distinct from faithfulness, define a six-subtype taxonomy of inconsistency, build a validated benchmark of 60 transcripts manually adapted from InstrumentalEval outputs, and implement a working scanner for InspectScout to detect this property.

Result: Experiments across four generator models and three evaluations from inspect_evals show that reasoning inconsistency is present, detectable, and varies systematically across both models and task types.

Conclusion: Reasoning inconsistency is a detectable and systematic property that can be assessed from evaluation transcripts alone, providing a practical tool for AI safety evaluation without requiring experimental interventions.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning is often unfaithful: a model's stated reasoning does not reliably reflect the process that produced its output. Detecting unfaithfulness, though, requires controlled experimental interventions, which cannot be applied to evaluation transcripts after the fact. We turn instead to a more tractable question that has received less attention: whether the stated reasoning is logically consistent with the answer it accompanies. Unlike faithfulness, consistency can be assessed from a transcript alone, with no intervention. We introduce reasoning consistency scanning, a reusable method for detecting this property in AI safety evaluation transcripts. Our contributions are fourfold. First, we formalize reasoning consistency as distinct from faithfulness and define a six-subtype taxonomy of inconsistency. Second, we build a validated benchmark of 60 transcripts, manually adapted from InstrumentalEval outputs. Third, we implement a working scanner for InspectScout, the first to target this property in safety evaluation transcripts. Fourth, we report results across four generator models and three evaluations from inspect_evals, showing that reasoning inconsistency is present, detectable, and varies systematically across both models and task types.

</details>


### [178] [From Atomic Actions to Standard Operating Procedures: Iterative Tool Optimization for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07321)
*Haipeng Ding,Yuexiang Xie,Zhewei Wei,Yaliang Li,Bolin Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSOP enables LLM agents to self-evolve by synthesizing atomic actions into reusable Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs), significantly improving task success rates while reducing interaction rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent frameworks rely on static, granular atomic actions, forcing agents to reinvent low-level logic for recurring workflows, leading to high reasoning overhead and failure rates.

Method: EvoSOP extracts SOPs from execution trajectories and iteratively optimizes the toolset through a lifecycle of construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning.

Result: EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates and substantially reduces the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Iterative tool optimization fosters reliable and efficient tool-use patterns, providing a scalable pathway for developing self-evolving agents.

Abstract: Tool utilization enables Large Language Model (LLM) agents to interact with the real world and resolve complex tasks. However, existing agent frameworks predominantly rely on static toolsets composed of granular atomic actions (e.g., basic file I/O or single-turn search), which forces agents to reinvent low-level logic for every recurring workflow, leading to increased reasoning overhead and failure rates. In this study, we propose that agents can achieve self-evolution by synthesizing these atomic actions into reusable Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs), which function as callable higher-order tools that encapsulate multi-step logic. We further introduce EvoSOP, a framework that empowers agents to extract SOPs from execution trajectories and iteratively optimize the toolset through a systematic lifecycle of construction, merging, evaluation, and pruning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EvoSOP significantly boosts task success rates while substantially reducing the number of interaction rounds compared to baselines. Our analysis also reveals that iterative tool optimization fosters reliable and efficient tool-use patterns, providing a scalable pathway for the development of self-evolving agents.

</details>


### [179] [Physics-Audited Agentic Discovery in Scientific Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07379)
*Diab W. Abueidda,Bilal Ahmed,Panos Pantidis,Mostafa E. Mobasher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PA-SciML introduces a verification-first workflow for agentic scientific machine learning that audits surrogate models against physics requirements (boundary conditions, causality, etc.) before reporting them as verified, preventing error-only baselines from selecting physically invalid surrogates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard error metrics in agentic SciML do not guarantee that surrogate models satisfy essential physics constraints such as boundary conditions, superposition, stiffness scaling, or causality, leading to physically invalid models despite low prediction error.

Method: PA-SciML enforces a verification-first workflow: (1) fix a scoring evaluator before search, (2) derive machine-checkable physics requirements, (3) check each candidate on its outputs, (4) search for high-violation cases without reference solutions, (5) report surrogates as verified only under stated checks, (6) optionally add numerical probes before training and test single-edits for causal score attribution.

Result: In static elasticity, PA-SciML selects a surrogate with lower validation error than an error-only baseline, with both passing linear-elastic checks. In transient elastodynamics, the error-only baseline with similar mean error fails a causality check (responding to future loading), while the PA-SciML surrogate passes all stated checks.

Conclusion: The key distinction is per-candidate physics evidence on predicted fields rather than a richer aggregate score; physics auditing is necessary to verify surrogate validity beyond error metrics.

Abstract: In agentic scientific machine learning (SciML), large language model (LLM) agents can discover surrogate models and select one by an automated score, typically an error metric. A low error, however, does not establish that the predicted fields satisfy the physics that matter for mechanics, such as boundary conditions, superposition, stiffness scaling, or causality. We introduce Physics-Audited Agentic SciML (PA-SciML), a verification-first workflow for agentic SciML discovery. The workflow fixes a scoring evaluator before search, derives reviewable machine-checkable physics requirements, checks each trained candidate on its outputs, and separately searches prescribed input ranges or measured load-history spans for high-violation cases without reference solution fields. A surrogate is reported as verified only under the stated checks. When enabled, the workflow also adds advisory numerical probes before training and tests one modeling change at a time to record which isolated edits are associated with score gains before reuse. In the reported computational-solid-mechanics numerical examples, the static elasticity run selects a surrogate with lower validation error than the error-only baseline while both selected models pass the common linear-elastic checks. In the transient elastodynamics run, an error-only baseline with similar mean error fails a stricter causality check by responding to future parts of the loading history, while the selected surrogate passes the stated checks. The main distinction is per-candidate physics evidence on predicted fields, not a richer aggregate score.

</details>


### [180] [MIRA-Math: A Benchmark for Minimal Information Requesting and Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07391)
*Charbel Al Bateh,Samer Saab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIRA-Math is a benchmark of 2,310 mathematical problems where each problem hides exactly one necessary atomic fact that the solver must explicitly request in natural language under a strict budget, then integrate for a final answer. Experiments show request success and final accuracy are separable behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks either provide all necessary facts (mathematical reasoning) or mix reasoning with tools, retrieval, and dialogue (interactive benchmarks). There is no diagnostic benchmark isolating the capability of requesting missing atomic information under a strict budget in mathematical problem-solving.

Method: The authors generate 2,310 instances across 22 typed mathematical families. Each instance withholds exactly one atomic fact. A constrained LLM responder provides the fact only if the solver's request matches the hidden fact; otherwise it declines. Solvers operate under a strict budget of requests. Instance generation, hint specification, validation, and verification are deterministic.

Result: Experiments across frontier and small models show request success and final-answer accuracy are separable: models may correctly request the missing fact yet fail downstream computation, or fail to request the right fact despite correct computation abilities.

Conclusion: MIRA-Math provides a reproducible, diagnostic benchmark for minimal information requesting in mathematical reasoning, revealing that the ability to identify and request missing facts is distinct from the ability to solve the problem given all facts.

Abstract: Mathematical reasoning benchmarks typically provide all facts needed to solve each problem, while interactive benchmarks often mix reasoning with tools, retrieval, and long-horizon dialogue. We introduce MIRA-Math, a benchmark for a narrower diagnostic capability: solving mathematical problems whose full latent state has a unique answer, but whose solver-facing view is missing exactly one necessary atomic fact. The solver must request the missing information in natural language under a strict budget and then integrate the returned fact into an exact final answer. A fixed constrained LLM responder sees only the dataset-provided atomic fact and must either offer the quoted fact when the request matches it, or decline otherwise. Thus, instance generation, typed hint specifications, validation, and final-answer verification are deterministic, while request metrics are measured under a fixed LLM-mediated responder channel. MIRA-Math contains 2{,}310 generated instances from 22 typed mathematical families spanning algebra, probability, linear systems, discrete structures, signal processing, Markov chains, circuits, interpolation, and numerical boundary-value problems. Experiments across frontier and small models show that request success and final-answer accuracy are separable: models may ask for the right fact yet fail the downstream computation, or fail before obtaining the canonical hint. We release generators, verifiers, prompts, run metadata, and dataset documentation to support reproducible evaluation of minimal information requesting in mathematical reasoning.

</details>


### [181] [Agentic Data Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07397)
*Elaine Ang,Chenxi Huang,Georgios Liargkovas,Jerry Liu,Jinhui Liu,Nikos Pagonas,Charlie Summers,Haonan Wang,Jiakai Xu,Tianle Zhou,Yusen Zhang,Zhou Yu,Zhuo Zhang,Tianyi Peng,Kostis Kaffes,Eugene Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of Agentic Data Environments, reframing data systems from passive storage into active execution substrates that both enhance agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees to address the challenge of balancing automation benefits with failure cost containment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents risk abrupt, irreversible failure costs despite offering gains in speed, scale, and labor efficiency; the central challenge is increasing automation benefits while bounding failure consequences.

Method: Proposes Agentic Data Environments as an execution substrate spanning files, APIs, applications, and system state, reframing data systems into active substrates for safe, reliable agent execution.

Result: Early work outlining how Agentic Data Environments can simultaneously amplify agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees beyond traditional database-centric approaches.

Conclusion: Data systems should evolve from passive stores into active execution substrates that enable safe, reliable agentic automation with bounded failure costs.

Abstract: Autonomous agents promise substantial gains in speed, scale, and labor efficiency, but their failures can impose abrupt and often irreversible costs. The central challenge for agentic automation is therefore to increase the benefits of automation while bounding the consequences of failure.
  While databases remain central to modern computing, agents operate over a broader data environment spanning files, APIs, applications, and system state. In this talk, I will outline early work on Agentic Data Environments -- the execution substrate in which agents operate -- that both amplify agent capabilities and enforce safety guarantees. This perspective reframes data systems from passive stores of state into active substrates for safe, reliable execution.

</details>


### [182] [Reason Less, Verify More: Deterministic Gates Recover a Silent Policy-Violation Failure Mode in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07405)
*Vikas Reddy,Sumanth Reddy Challaram,Abhishek Basu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents with tool-use can silently violate domain policies via "silent wrong-state failures" where tools execute forbidden actions without errors. In the τ²-bench airline domain, 78% of failures are this type. A lightweight intervention—deterministic pre-execution gates that inspect tool calls before allowing writes—raises success from 29.6% to 42.0% on gpt-4o-mini (+12.4pp), with the effect concentrated on firing tasks (+19.2pp). The same failure mode exists in frontier models (gpt-5.2), and the gates improve success from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using LLM agents can silently violate domain policies by executing well-formed tool calls whose state transitions are forbidden, producing incorrect states (e.g., cancelled bookings, unverified claims) that neither the tool nor the agent's self-report detects.

Method: Introduces deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates that inspect the proposed tool call and current state before permitting write operations. Evaluated on τ²-bench airline domain with gpt-4o-mini and gpt-5.2, alongside negative controls (retail domain, BFCL).

Result: 78% of failures in the airline domain are silent wrong-state failures. Four-gate suite raises full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% on gpt-4o-mini (+12.4pp; P=0.0012), replicated on disjoint seeds (+12.3pp; P=0.0008). On firing tasks (26/50): +19.2pp; non-firing tasks: no significant change. On gpt-5.2: success improved from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020).

Conclusion: Deterministic gates cannot guarantee task success but can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary. The effect is bounded and reliable, with strongest results where policy-permissive tools exist.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents can violate the very policies they are deployed to enforce while appearing to complete the task successfully. In policy-permissive environments, a tool may execute any well-formed call even when the corresponding state transition is forbidden by domain policy. The result is a silent wrong state (a booking cancelled, a passenger count changed, a claim acted on without verification) that neither the tool nor the agent's self-report exposes.
  We study this failure mode in the $τ^2$-bench airline domain. On a budget agent, 78% of observed failures are silent wrong-state failures with no tool error, and the aggregate failure rate is reproducible across disjoint seeds, not sampling noise. We then evaluate a lightweight intervention: deterministic, read-only pre-execution gates that inspect the proposed call and current state before allowing a write. A four-gate suite raises full-benchmark success from 29.6% to 42.0% on gpt-4o-mini (+12.4pp; paired task-level bootstrap P=0.0012), and the lift reproduces on a disjoint 15-seed set (+12.3pp; P=0.0008).
  The effect is concentrated where the gates fire: on the 26/50 firing tasks, success rises by +19.2pp, while movement on the 24 non-firing tasks does not exclude zero. Two negative controls (a self-enforcing retail domain and BFCL) bound the mechanism: gates help when tools are policy-permissive and add little where tools already self-enforce. As suggestive evidence, not a central claim, the same failure mode persists at the frontier: gpt-5.2 at default reasoning still attempts policy-violating writes, and the same suite improves success from 61.2% to 71.6% (+10.4pp; P=0.020; n=5, no replication). The contribution is a bounded evaluation and reliability result: deterministic gates do not guarantee task success, but they can deterministically prevent a known class of silent policy-violating writes at the action boundary.

</details>


### [183] [InductWave: Inductive Multi-Hop Logical Query Answering on Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07422)
*Mayank Kharbanda,Michael Cochez,Rajiv Ratn Shah,Raghava Mutharaju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InductWave is a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical multi-hop query answering over knowledge graphs that uses fewer message-passing layers (25-50% less) than baseline models while achieving comparable or better performance, enabling evaluation on massive graphs like Wiki-KG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for logical query answering over KGs rely on transductive reasoning (cannot handle unseen entities) and assume resource-abundant training conditions. In real-world scenarios, resource scarcity limits the ability to train on all nodes of large KGs, motivating the need for inductive approaches with lower resource requirements.

Method: InductWave employs a wavelet-based inductive embedding approach that trains on a subgraph with fewer nodes than the test graph. It uses significantly fewer message-passing layers (25-50% of baseline models) to perform logical multi-hop query answering with conjunction, disjunction, and negation operators over EFO queries.

Result: InductWave performs on par with baseline models while using half the number of message-passing layers, and outperforms all baselines in most cases using 75% of the layers. The reduced resource requirements enable evaluation on massive graphs like Wiki-KG. Experiments were conducted across varying train-test graph proportions on FB15k-(237).

Conclusion: InductWave demonstrates that inductive reasoning with wavelet-based embeddings can achieve strong performance on logical query answering over large KGs while requiring substantially fewer computational resources (fewer message-passing layers), making it feasible to scale to massive knowledge graphs.

Abstract: Logical Multi-Hop Query Answering over Knowledge Graphs (KGs) can be formulated as querying, with an implicit completeness assumption. Current works mainly focus on Existential First Order Logic (EFO) queries. These EFO queries contain conjunction, disjunction, and negation operators. Most existing works employ transductive reasoning, meaning they are not capable of reasoning over entities unseen during training. In the real world, there is a resource scarcity, and we cannot train a model with all the nodes of a large KG. Hence, we propose InductWave, a wavelet-based inductive embedding method for logical query answering on large KGs. Here, the training graph consists of fewer nodes than the test graph. Our model performs on par with the baseline models while having half the number of message-passing layers. It outperforms all of them in most cases, with 75% of the layers. These fewer resource requirements enable us to evaluate InductWave on massive graphs, such as Wiki-KG. We test our model using extensive experiments across varying train-test graph proportions of the FB15k-(237) dataset, comparing it with the state-of-the-art models. The code and datasets for the model are available at https://github.com/kracr/inductwave/.

</details>


### [184] [The Blind Curator: How a Biased Judge Silently Disables Skill Retirement in Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07436)
*Xing Zhang,Yanwei Cui,Guanghui Wang,Ziyuan Li,Wei Qiu,Bing Zhu,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving agent system retires bad skills, but biased LLM judges (especially false-pass bias) silently disable the curator's ability to retire failures, causing mechanism failure that no amount of data can fix, though the degradation may remain hidden in aggregate metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate what happens when the reward signal used for skill retirement in self-evolving agents is biased (as is the case with LLM judges in reference-free tasks), since unbiased reward is an assumption that does not hold in practice.

Method: Corrupted-reward analysis, behavioral study on a reference-free report-writing testbed with code-generation cross-check, causal injection of corruption on top of deterministic reward, and a defect-injection audit for pre-deployment detection.

Result: Symmetric noise is harmless, but false-pass bias disables contribution-based retirement past a sharp, uncrossable threshold. This mechanism failure is universal across domains and failure rates. The downstream outcome is regime-dependent: eval quality degrades only when corruption also starves skill synthesis, otherwise remaining hidden.

Conclusion: The contribution is a behavioral safety result, not a performance one; a cheap defect-injection audit can tell operators which side of the threshold their judge occupies before deployment.

Abstract: A self-evolving agent retires its bad skills by watching them fail, so what happens when the judge cannot see the failures? Skill retirement is the structural constraint that keeps a growing library from drifting below the no-skill baseline, but its guarantee assumes an unbiased reward, which is false for the LLM judges that reference-free tasks force upon us. We show that a biased judge does not merely add noise; it \emph{silently switches off the curator}. We make this precise with a corrupted-reward analysis and, isolating the causal channel by injecting corruption on top of a deterministic reward, a behavioral study on a reference-free report-writing testbed with a code-generation cross-check. Symmetric noise leaves retirement intact, but \emph{false-pass} bias (failures slipping through as passes) disables contribution-based retirement past a sharp threshold that no amount of data can cross. Separating genuine retirement from cap-eviction churn shows this \emph{mechanism} failure is universal, holding across domains and failure rates and sparing only near-zero-false-pass, verifier-like graders. The downstream \emph{outcome}, though, is regime-dependent: eval quality degrades only where the same corruption also starves skill synthesis, and otherwise holds steady, so the disabled curator is \emph{silent}, surfacing in no aggregate metric. The contribution is a behavioral safety result, not a performance one. A cheap defect-injection audit then tells an operator, before deployment, which side of the threshold their judge occupies.

</details>


### [185] [SpaCellAgent: A Self-Evolving LLM-Based Multi-Agent Framework for Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07467)
*Songhan Wang,Haoang Chi,He Li,Zhiheng Zhang,Jiayan Yuan,Cheems Wang,Hao Peng,Xinwang Liu,Wenjing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SpaCellAgent is an LLM-based multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end spatiotemporal trajectory inference and narrative generation from natural language, achieving over 40% efficiency improvement across six heterogeneous datasets while maintaining expert-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory inference methods require extensive manual intervention and proficiency in heterogeneous tools, creating a significant barrier to efficient TI analysis. There is a need for an automated, end-to-end solution that bridges this gap.

Method: SpaCellAgent employs a multi-agent architecture for strategic workflow planning, a dynamic tool-orchestration engine for adaptive algorithm selection, and a self-evolution module that iteratively refines performance through feedback, converting natural language specifications into optimized analytical workflows.

Result: Evaluated on six heterogeneous datasets spanning temporal developmental trajectories, diverse sequencing platforms, and spatially-resolved tissue architectures, SpaCellAgent demonstrates over 40% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining expert-aligned performance.

Conclusion: SpaCellAgent democratizes advanced spatiotemporal modeling by fully automating the pipeline, establishing a scalable, agent-driven paradigm for computational biology and lowering the barrier for researchers to perform sophisticated trajectory inference analysis.

Abstract: Spatial and Single-cell transcriptomics are transformative in deciphering cellular dynamics. As the fundamental paradigm for reconstructing cell developmental paths, trajectory inference (TI) is critical. However, existing methods require extensive manual intervention and proficiency in heterogeneous tools, posing a significant barrier to efficient TI analysis. To bridge this gap, we propose SpaCellAgent, an autonomous large language model (LLM) multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end spatiotemporal analysis and narrative generation. SpaCellAgent utilizes a multi-agent architecture for strategic workflow planning, a dynamic tool-orchestration engine for adaptive algorithm selection, and a self-evolution module that iteratively refines performance through feedback. We evaluate SpaCellAgent on six heterogeneous datasets encompassing complex temporal developmental trajectories, diverse sequencing platforms, and spatially-resolved tissue architectures. SpaCellAgent consistently demonstrates over 40\% improvement in analytical efficiency while maintaining expert-aligned performance. By converting natural language specifications into optimized analytical workflows and fully automating the pipeline, SpaCellAgent democratizes advanced spatiotemporal modeling and establishes a scalable, agent-driven paradigm for computational biology. The code and materials are available at https://github.com/LittleXH-shw/SpaCellAgent.

</details>


### [186] [Search, Fail, Recover: A Training Framework for Correction-Aware Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07492)
*Dmitry Beresnev,Vladimir Makharev,Roman Khalikov,Ivan Oseledets,Petr Anokhin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pyligent is a training and inference framework that represents reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains, using task validators to label continuations and failures. It converts search trees into supervised targets for continue, finish, and backtrack actions with optional traces. Experiments show significant improvements over gold-only SFT across hidden graphs (72.7pp), Sudoku variants (14-27pp), and Blocksworld (13pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many reasoning tasks require non-linear search (backtracking after delayed failure) but standard left-to-right chain supervision does not teach recovery from incorrect branches.

Method: Pyligent uses a task validator to label generated continuations and failures during search, converting resulting search trees into supervised training targets for three actions: continue, finish, and backtrack, with optional traces summarizing abandoned branches.

Result: Pyligent improves solve rate by 72.7 percentage points on hidden graphs, 17 and 18 points on mixed and expert Sudoku, 27 and 14 points on mixed and expert Sudoku with reasoning traces, and 13 points on Blocksworld, compared to gold-only supervised fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Explicit failed-branch supervision can teach useful recovery behavior that goes beyond simple imitation of polished solution chains.

Abstract: Many reasoning tasks are not well described by a single left-to-right chain: a solver may need to pursue a plausible branch, observe delayed failure, and return to the latest prefix that can still be completed. We introduce Pyligent, a training and inference framework inspired by the Diligent Learner formulation that represents reasoning as validated search over partial solution chains. A task validator labels generated continuations and failures, and the resulting search trees are converted into supervised targets for three actions: continue, finish, and backtrack, with optional traces that summarize abandoned branches. We evaluate Pyligent on a hidden directed graph task designed to isolate delayed-failure recovery, and on structured reasoning domains with exact validators, including $4{\times}4$ Sudoku, Sudoku with reasoning traces, and Blocksworld. Compared with gold-only supervised fine-tuning, Pyligent improves solve rate by $72.7$ percentage points on hidden graphs, by $17$ and $18$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku, by $27$ and $14$ points on mixed and expert Sudoku with reasoning traces, and by $13$ points on Blocksworld. These results suggest that explicit failed-branch supervision can teach useful recovery behavior beyond imitation of polished solution chains.

</details>


### [187] [Do LLM-Generated Skills Make Better AI Data Scientists? A Component Ablation Across Data-Science Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07504)
*Wei-Jung Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-generated skills for data science tasks do not improve performance over no-skill prompting, with extensive ablation (7,560 runs) showing no significant gains (p≥0.396, spread ≤1.2 pp).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expert-written skills for data-science task families are effective but create a manual bottleneck in writing and maintenance. The authors explore whether LLM-generated skills can serve as a low-curation alternative to reduce this overhead.

Method: The authors test one generated skill per lifecycle stage (data preparation, extraction, statistical analysis, reporting) across 56 tasks, 9 model configurations, 3 providers (7,560 runs). They perform ablation on skill components and a token-matched control with 1,512 runs.

Result: No full generated skill or ablated variant significantly outperforms No-Skill prompting (all p≥0.396, spread 1.2 pp). Full skills perform similarly to task-irrelevant skill-formatted content. No reliable improvement found across any stage.

Conclusion: Using one LLM-generated skill per data-science workflow as a default single-shot prompting strategy is not recommended; generated skills do not provide reliable improvements over task-only prompting.

Abstract: Product data scientists often ask LLM-based agents to help with recurring execution tasks such as cleaning data, writing SQL, choosing statistical tests, and formatting results. Reusable skill files are meant to avoid prompting from scratch by packaging guidance for a task family. Expert-written skills can encode high-quality guidance, but writing and maintaining them across many data-science task families creates a manual bottleneck. We ask whether LLM-generated skills offer a useful low-curation alternative: do they improve performance over the task prompt alone? We test this question across four lifecycle stages: data preparation, data extraction, statistical analysis, and reporting, using one generated skill per stage. We find no reliable improvement from full generated skills over No-Skill prompting. We then ask whether any part of the skill is useful by ablating different skill components. The main ablation covers 56 tasks, nine model configurations, and three providers, yielding 7,560 runs. Compared with prompting using the task alone, neither the full generated skill nor any ablated skill variant significantly improves performance; all p-values are at least 0.396, and the total spread across variants is only 1.2 pp. A supplemental token-matched control adds 1,512 runs and finds that Full skills perform similarly to task-irrelevant skill-formatted content. The results caution against using one LLM-generated skill per data-science workflow as a default single-shot prompting strategy.

</details>


### [188] [RL Post-Training Builds Compositional Reasoning Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07646)
*Azwar Abdulsalam,Nishil Patel,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RL post-training can compose primitive skills into novel higher-level strategies (sequential and parallel compositions) beyond mere amplification of latent abilities. In a rewrite-grammar environment with known pretraining distribution, RL discovers reusable composed procedures that solve held-out problems rarely solved by the base model, while rejection fine-tuning plateaus due to invalid shortcut-like rewrites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether RL post-training merely amplifies primitive skills already present in a base model, or can actually compose them into new higher-level strategies.

Method: Pretrain a Transformer on primitive symbol-rewrite chains; post-train via RL on a Trace-based reasoning task with binary final-answer reward, in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment. Compare against rejection fine-tuning (RFT). Analyze rewrite traces to uncover compositional mechanisms.

Result: RL solves held-out problems rarely solved by the pretrained model even with larger sampling; RFT improves early but plateaus. RL reorganizes primitive competence through phased compositional mechanism: strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers sequential and parallel compositions. Composed procedures are reused and consolidated. RFT produces many invalid shortcut-like rewrites. Emergence of composition is gated by how pretraining organizes primitive competence into reducible procedures, not just exposure.

Conclusion: RL can compose primitive skills into reliable higher-level strategies beyond mere amplification. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reusable composed procedures. Key difference from RFT is selectivity, not exploration volume.

Abstract: Does RL post-training merely amplify primitive skills already latent in a base model, or can it compose primitive skills into new higher-level strategies? We study this question in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment where the pretraining distribution is known and every generated rewrite can be audited. A Transformer is pretrained on primitive symbol-rewrite chains and post-trained on a Trace-based reasoning task with only a binary final-answer reward. RL solves held-out problems that remain rarely solved by the pretrained model even under much larger sampling budgets, while rejection fine-tuning improves early but plateaus. Trace analysis shows that RL reorganizes primitive competence through a phased compositional mechanism: it first strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers valid composed procedures. These include sequential compositions, which collapse ordered chains of primitive contractions, and parallel compositions, which combine independent primitive contractions in a single step. The composed procedures are not isolated samples; they are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire. Comparing RL with rejection fine-tuning shows that the key difference is not exploration volume but selectivity: RFT produces many shortcut-like rewrites, much of them invalid, whereas RL concentrates exploration into valid reusable structure. Pretraining ablations show that the emergence of compositional strategies is gated not by primitive exposure alone, but by whether pretraining organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures that RL can later compress. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reliable higher-level strategies.

</details>


### [189] [Recursive Self-Improvement in AI: From Bounded Self-Refinement to Autonomous Research Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07663)
*Mingguang Chen,Licheng Wang,Bo Qu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) to taxonomize AI self-improvement along two axes: what is improved (behavior, policy, evaluator, or research process) and loop closure degree (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). It separates bounded self-refinement from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI), maps evaluator designs onto a verification hierarchy, and identifies governance-grade measurement as the field's most underpopulated niche.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The vocabulary used to describe AI self-improvement ("self-refine," "self-reward," "self-play," "self-evolve") conflates fundamentally different ambitions, creating confusion about capabilities, risks, and limits. A principled taxonomy is needed to separate practical bounded self-refinement from aspirational open-ended recursive self-improvement.

Method: A survey of 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) classified along two axes: (1) what the system improves (deployment behavior, training policy, evaluator, or the research process itself), and (2) the degree of loop closure (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). The evaluator design space is analyzed through a verification hierarchy from formal verifiers (strongest) to intrinsic self-assessment (weakest).

Result: Demonstrated self-improvement strength tracks the verification hierarchy. Failure modes (self-confirming loops, model collapse, diversity collapse) follow from violations of this hierarchy. Open-ended RSI remains bounded by grounding requirements, collapse dynamics, and compute constraints on every measured axis. Governance-grade measurement of self-improvement is identified as the most underpopulated niche.

Conclusion: The research direction-setting bottleneck that keeps humans in the loop sits at the top of the verification hierarchy. The paper connects technical findings to RSI limit theory and safety/governance questions raised by frontier-lab accounts of closing the loop, highlighting the need for governance-grade measurement.

Abstract: AI systems increasingly participate in their own improvement: revising their outputs, adapting their own harnesses during deployment, training on data they generate, and, increasingly, conducting AI research itself. This literature is described under a vocabulary ("self-refine," "self-reward," "self-play," "self-evolve") that conflates fundamentally different ambitions. We survey 1,250 arXiv papers (2024-2026) along two axes: what the system improves -- its behavior in deployment, its policy through training, its evaluator, or the research process itself -- and the degree of loop closure (human-in-the-loop to fully closed). The taxonomy separates bounded self-refinement -- convergent, evaluable, and already industrial practice -- from open-ended recursive self-improvement (RSI), which remains bounded by grounding requirements, collapse dynamics, and compute constraints on every measured axis. Its distinctive feature is a dedicated category for self-evaluation: every improvement loop is a claim that some signal can substitute for human judgment. We survey the evaluator design space -- judges, process reward models, verifiers, rubrics, meta-evaluation -- order the signals into a verification hierarchy from formal verifiers (strongest) to intrinsic self-assessment (weakest), and observe that demonstrated self-improvement strength tracks this hierarchy, that its failure modes (self-confirming loops, model collapse, diversity collapse) follow from its violations, and that the "research direction-setting" bottleneck keeping humans in the loop sits at the top of that hierarchy. We connect the technical literature to the theory of RSI limits and to the safety and governance questions raised by frontier-lab accounts of closing the loop, and identify governance-grade measurement of self-improvement as the field's most underpopulated niche.

</details>


### [190] [SkillCenter: A Large-Scale Source-Grounded Skill Library for Autonomous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07676)
*Tianming Sha,Yue Zhao,Lichao Sun,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillCenter is the largest open skill library for AI agents with 216,938 structured skills across 24 domains, sourced from both pipeline-filtered (114,565 source-grounded skills from peer-reviewed journals and technical sources) and community contributions (102,373 from GitHub/ClawHub), built via an LLM-based quality gate (SkillGate) with full source-grounding traceability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous AI agents lack grounded operational knowledge to produce correct, secure, and maintainable outputs under limited human review.

Method: End-to-end framework: multi-source acquisition, LLM-based quality gate (SkillGate), template-driven generation, iterative source-grounding, and quality-controlled publishing; outputs as offline-searchable SQLite FTS5 bundles.

Result: 216,938 structured skills across 24 domain bundles; 114,565 source-grounded skills from peer-reviewed journals, ArXiv, and 24,000+ technical sources; 102,373 community skills from GitHub and ClawHub.

Conclusion: SkillCenter provides a large-scale, traceable, and searchable skill library to ground autonomous AI agents with verified operational knowledge.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents can execute complex tasks with limited human review, yet they often lack the grounded operational knowledge to make their outputs not just executable but correct, secure, and maintainable. We introduce SkillCenter, to our knowledge the largest open skill library for agents by total count: 216,938 structured skills across 24 domain bundles. A SkillGate-filtered pipeline contributes 114,565 source-grounded skills from peer-reviewed journals, ArXiv, and over 24,000 technical sources, integrated with 102,373 community skills from GitHub and the ClawHub marketplace. We present the end-to-end framework that builds the pipeline subset: multi-source acquisition, an LLM-based quality gate (SkillGate), template-driven generation, iterative source-grounding, and quality-controlled publishing. Source grounding is a traceability guarantee: each retained claim maps to an exact quotation in its source. All skills ship as offline-searchable SQLite FTS5 bundles.

</details>


### [191] [Institutional Red-Teaming: Deployment Rules, Not Just Models, Causally Shape Multi-Agent AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07695)
*Yujiao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces institutional red-teaming, a methodology for testing deployment rules in multi-agent AI systems, and instantiates it in IABench-CA. The authors find that rules causally alter collective safety, there is no universally safe default rule, identity salience drives targeting behavior, and they provide a safety-case workflow for certifying provisional rule regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a rigorous evaluation methodology for testing how deployment rules affect collective safety in multi-agent AI systems, addressing the gap in understanding causal effects of institutional rules on multi-agent behavior.

Method: Institutional red-teaming methodology that holds agents, objectives, and task state fixed while varying only one rule, then attributing behavioral changes to that rule. Instantiated in IABench-CA benchmark with 228 contexts, 5 canonical rules, 7 model populations (33,924 games), with normative cooperative reference and auto-labelled reasoning traces.

Result: (1) Deployment rules causally alter collective safety (mean fatality changes by 22-58 percentage points). (2) No safe default exists; regressive identity-targeting is never safest, eliminates the least-resourced agent in 30-87% of games, and is selection-unsafe for all populations. (3) Identity salience is the mechanism—anonymization ablation on gpt-5.1 reduced targeting from 81% to 22%, but under repeated play agents re-infer hidden rules.

Conclusion: The methodology enables safety-case workflows that certify provisional rule regions per context and population, with explicit residual risks and monitoring obligations, providing a rigorous framework for safe deployment of multi-agent AI systems.

Abstract: We introduce institutional red-teaming, an evaluation methodology for testing deployment rules in multi-agent AI: hold the agents, objectives, and task state fixed, vary only one rule, and attribute the resulting change in collective behavior to that rule. We instantiate the methodology in IABench-CA, a consequence-allocation benchmark spanning 228 contexts, five canonical rules, and seven model populations (33,924 games), with a normative cooperative reference and auto-labelled reasoning traces. Three findings emerge. (1) Deployment rules causally alter collective safety: changing only the consequence rule moves mean fatality by 22 to 58 percentage points within every population. (2) There is no safe default, but the targeting hazard is universal: the safest rule, the least-safe rule, and even the direction of the incidence effect vary across populations, yet regressive identity-targeting is never decisively safest in any context for any population, eliminates the least-resourced agent in 30-87% of games everywhere, and is selection-unsafe relative to the cooperative reference for all seven populations. (3) Identity salience is the mechanism: a one-shot anonymization ablation on the most exploitation-prone population (gpt-5.1) shows that merely naming the loss bearer in the rule text drives targeted elimination from 22% to 81% at identical payoffs; under repeated play, anonymization only delays the targeting, as agents re-infer the hidden rule from observed eliminations. We package the methodology as a safety-case workflow that certifies a provisional rule region $Φ(c,P)$ per deployment context and population, with explicit residual risks and monitoring obligations.

</details>
